CHAPTER II
Eigenvarieties
In this chapter, we explain the construction of eigenvarieties from suitable fam-
ilies of Banach modules with action of a commutative algebra, and prove their
general properties. This is a version at large and in a rigid analytic setting of the
construction of eigenalgebras of the previous chapter.
In this endeavor, our work is greatly facilitated by the now classical exposition,
formalization and generalization of Mazur and Coleman’s method by Buzzard, cf.
[23], as well as the treatment of similar questions in Chenevier’s thesis (cf. [26] and
[28]). The main diﬀerence with those treatments is that we oﬀer a self-contained all-
in-one-place exposition of this material, from the ﬁrst principles in non-archimedean
functional analysis up to the hard comparison theorems for eigenvarieties of Ch-
enevier. Our only prerequisite beyond elementary mathematics are some familiarity
with Tate’s rigid analytic geometry, at the level of [ 21, Chapters 7 and 8] (other
good references, suﬃcient for this chapter, include [40,§1 and§2] or the ﬁrst half of
[20]). Even so, rigid analytic geometry is not used before§II.3.3, that is roughly for
the ﬁrst half of this chapter. We also give some simpliﬁcations and generalizations
of the methods and results in [ 23].
Compared with the setting of a construction of an eigenalgebra of the preceding
chapter (a base ring R, a ﬁnite ﬂat R-module M, a morphism ψ :H→ EndR(M)
whereH is some commutative ring), the setting for eigenvarieties is enlarged in
two directions. The base ring R, or rather its spectrum Spec R is replaced by a
rigid analytic varietyW, which will not be an aﬃnoid in general. Hence it would
be natural to replace M by a coherent locally-free sheaf over W, but here comes a
second, more important, enlargement: we want to replace the ﬁnite module M by a
non-ﬁnite Banach module. To avoid talking of sheaf of Banach modules (which we
could, as in [34] or [28]), we simply deﬁne our data as in [23] by the giving, for every
aﬃnoid subdomain W = SpR ofW, of a Banach-module MW over R (satisfying
some technical condition of orthonormability), provided with an action of the ring
H. The modules MW for various W are required to satisfy some compatibility
relations, that are explained in section §II.5. To deal with the new inﬁniteness of
our module, we introduce a crucial hypothesis: that our ringH contains a privileged
elementUp∈H that acts compactly on the various Banach modules MW .
53
===== PDF PAGE 54 =====
54 II. EIGENVARIETIES
From those data, our aim is to construct in a canonical way an eigenvariety E,
that is a rigid analytic variety together with a locally ﬁnite map κ :E→W , and a
morphism of rings ψ :H→O (E) allowing us to see elements of H as functions on
E. The eigenvariety should have the fundamental property that its points z (over
a given point w = κ(z)∈W ) classify the systems of H-eigenvalues appearing in
the ﬁber Mw of the modules MW at w that are of ﬁnite slope , that is for which
Up has a non-zero eigenvalue (the set of such systems do not depend of the chosen
aﬃnoid W ofW). The bijection should be naturally given as follows: to a point z
should correspond the system of eigenvalues T↦→ψ(T )(z), for any T∈H .
In order to construct the eigenvariety, we glue some local pieces that are rigid
analytic spectrum of eigenalgebras constructed as in the preceding chapter. More
precisely, if W = SpR is an admissible aﬃnoid subdomain of W, and ν is a real
number, it is often, though not always, possible to deﬁne in a natural way a ﬁnite
ﬂat direct summand R-submodule M≤ν
W ofMW which is morally the “slope less or
equal to ν” part of MW , that is the submodule of vectors on which Up acts with
generalized eigenvalues of valuation less or equal than ν. When this is possible, we
say that W and ν are adapted. Those notions are explained in section II.4, which
also proves that there are enough, in a precise technical sense, pairs (W,ν ) that are
adapted.
In section §II.6, we explain our variant of Buzzard’s eigenvariety machine of
[23], constructing the eigenvariety by gluing the spectrum of eigenalgebras TW,ν
deﬁned by the action of H on M≤ν
W for adapted pairs (W,ν ).
In section II.7 we list the most important properties of the eigenvarieties, due
to Coleman-Mazur ([ 34]) or Chenevier ([ 26]), in particular we explain why their
points parametrize system of eigenvalues of ﬁnite slope.
In the last section II.8 we discuss two very useful results of Chenevier (cf. [ 28])
allowing to compare two eigenvarieties, or to prove that an eigenvariety is reduced.
II.1. Non-archimedean Fredholm’s theory
In this section, we give a self-contained treatment of the theory of the Fred-
holm’s determinant of compact operators over Banach algebras. Our treatment is
inspired by the one in [23], himself inspired by [31], and by the paper [93]. We get
rid of the noetherian hypothesis that is used in [ 23].
II.1.1. General notions. Let R be a (commutative, with unity) Qp-algebra
with a norm || extending the standard p-adic norm on Qp. We recall that this
means that|| is a mapR→ R+, satisfying, for everyλ∈ Qp,x,y∈R,|λ1R| =|λ|,
|x +y|≤ max(|x|,|y|),|xy|≤| x||y|, and|x| = 0 if and only if x = 0. We assume
===== PDF PAGE 55 =====
II.1. NON-ARCHIMEDEAN FREDHOLM’S THEORY 55
moreover that R is a Banach algebra, that is complete for the topology deﬁned by
|| .
Exercise II.1.1. An element x in R∗ is called multiplicative for|| if|x||x−1| = 1.
Show that x is multiplicative if and only if for all y∈R,|xy| =|x||y|. Show that elements
of Q∗
p are multiplicative in R.
A norm on an R-module M is a map, usually also denoted || , from M to
R+, such that for every x ∈ R, m,n ∈ M, we have |m +n| ≤max(|m|,|n|),
|xm|≤| x||m|, and |m| = 0 if and only if m = 0. An R-module M with a norm
is a Banach R-module if it is complete for the topology deﬁned by the norm. Two
norms|| 1 and|| 2 onM are equivalent if there exists positive real constants c<C
such that c|m|1≤|m|2≤C|m|1 for every m∈M.
Exercise II.1.2. Show that two norms on M are equivalent if and only if they deﬁne
the same topology.
A morphism φ :M→N between two Banach R-modules is continuous if and
only if it is bounded, that is if there exists a constantC >0 such that|φ(m)|≤ C|m|
for every m in M. In this case, the norm of φ, denoted |φ|, is deﬁned as the
smallest constant C satisfying this condition. One evidently has |φ′φ| ≤|φ′||φ|
when φ : M → N and φ′ : N → K are continuous morphisms of Banach R-
modules. We denote by Hom R(M,N ) the R-module of continuous morphisms of
R-modules from M to N. It is a Banach R-module for the norm we just deﬁned.
When M =N we write EndR(M) for HomR(M,M ).
The Open Mapping Theorem states that every continuous surjective map be-
tween BanachR-modules is open. It is proven exactly as in the case of real Banach
spaces, using the Baire category theorem, see Wikipedia or any standard textbook
of functional analysis – or see [ 22, Th´ eor` eme 1, Chapitre 1,§3.3] for a general
proof valid in our case. The open mapping theorem implies that if f :M→N is
a continuous surjective map of R-modules, there exists a constant c> 0 such that
for every n in N we can ﬁnd m∈M with f(m) =n and|m|≤ c|n|.
II.1.2. Compact operators.
Definition II.1.3. We say that a morphism of R-modules φ : M→ N is of
ﬁnite rank if its image is contained in a ﬁnite (i.e. ﬁnitely generated) submodule of
N. We say that a continuous morphism φ :M→N between BanachR-modules is
compact (or completely continuous) if it is in the closure of the module of morphisms
of ﬁnite rank in HomR(M,N ).
===== PDF PAGE 56 =====
56 II. EIGENVARIETIES
It follows from the deﬁnition that the set of compact morphisms is a closed
sub-module of HomR(M,N ). In particular the sum of two compact morphisms is
compact.
Lemma II.1.4. Let M,N,K be three Banach R-modules and φ : M → N a
compact morphism. If f :N→K is continuous, then f◦φ is compact. Similarly
if f :K→M is continuous, then φ◦f is compact
Proof — It is enough to prove the same result with ‘compact’ replaced by ‘ﬁnite
rank’. If φ is of ﬁnite rank,φ(M) is contained in a ﬁnite submoduleN0 ofN, and for
f :N→K, (f◦φ)(M) is contained in f(N0) which is ﬁnite as the image of a ﬁnite
module, and so f◦φ is of ﬁnite rank. For f :K→M, (φ◦f)(K)⊂φ(M)⊂N0
and φ◦f is also of ﬁnite rank. □
II.1.3. Potentially orthonormalizable modules.
Definition II.1.5. LetM be a Banach R-module, whose norm is also denoted
|| . An orthonormal basis for the module M is a family ( ei)i∈I of elements of M
such that|ei| = 1 for every i∈ I, and such that for every element m∈ M there
exists a unique sequence ( ai)i∈I of elements of R converging to zero 1 such that
m =∑
i∈Iaiei and|m| = supi∈I|ai|.
Definition II.1.6. A Banach module M is orthonormalizable if it has an or-
thonormal basis. It is potentially orthonormalizable if there exists a norm||′ onM
equivalent to|| such that (M,||′) is orthonormalizable.
Example II.1.7. The Banach R-module cI(R) of sequences (ri)i∈I of elements
of R converging to 0, normed with the sup norm, is orthonormalizable. There is
even a canonical orthonormal basis (ei)i∈I deﬁned by settingei the sequence whose
i-th term is 1 and all others 0. A Banach R-module M is orthonormalizable (resp.
potentially orthonormalizable) if and only if it is isometric (resp. isomorphic) to
cI(R) for some set I.
Let M and N be two Banach R-modules with orthonormal bases ( ei)i∈I and
(fj)j∈J respectively. If φ ∈ HomR(M,N ) we can write, for every i, φ(ei) =∑
j∈Jai,jfj, where ( ai,j)j∈J is a sequence of elements of R converging to 0. It
is clear that|φ| = supi∈I,j∈J|ai,j|. Conversely every matrix (ai,j)i∈I,j∈J of elements
of R, whose rows ( ai,j)j∈J go to zero for every i∈ I, and whose coeﬃcients are
bounded, deﬁnes an element φ∈ HomR(M,N ).
1Letmi,i∈I be elements in M. We say that (mi)i∈I converges to m if for every ϵ> 0 there
exists a ﬁnite subset S ofI such that|mi−m|<ϵ for everyi∈I−S. When the sequence (mi)i∈I
converges to 0, it is easy to see that the sequence ( ∑
i∈Jmi)J∈F(I) (whereF (I) is the set of ﬁnite
subsets of I) converges to some limit in M. We denote this limit by ∑
i∈Imi.

If M is an orthonormalizable module with basis ( ei)i∈I, and S ⊂ I a ﬁnite
subset, we denote by MS the ﬁnite free sub-module of M generated by the es,
s∈S, and by πS :M→M the projection of M ontoMS sending ei to ei if i∈S,
and ei to 0 if i̸∈S.
Lemma II.1.8. LetM be an orthonormalizable module with basis (ei)i∈I and P
a ﬁnite sub-module. For any ϵ> 0, there exists a ﬁnite subset S of I such that for
every p∈P ,|πS(p)−p|≤ ϵ|p|.
Proof — SinceP is ﬁnite, there exists a surjective continuous morphism π :Ar→
P . By the open mapping theorem, there is a constant c >0 such that for every
p∈P , there exists m∈Ar such that π(m) =p and|m|≤ c|p|.
If e1,...,e r is an orthonormal basis of Ar, there exists a ﬁnite subset S of I
such that|πS(π(ei))−π(ei)|≤ ϵ/c for i = 1,...,r . If m∈Ar, one can write m =∑r
i=1aiei, and |πS(π(m))−π(m)| =|∑ai(πS(π(ei))−π(ei))|≤ max(|ai|)ϵ/c =
ϵ|m|/c.
Now let p∈P , and choose m∈Ar such that π(m) = p and|m|≤ c|p|. Then
|πS(p)−p| =|πS(π(m))−π(m)|≤ ϵ|m|/c≤ϵ|p|. □
Proposition II.1.9. With notations as above, a morphism φ∈ HomR(M,N )
is compact if and only if the sequence (supi∈I|ai,j|)j∈J converges to 0. (In other
words,φ is compact if and only if the rows (ai,j)j∈J go to zero uniformly in i.)
Proof — If supi∈I|ai,j| converges to 0, then the sequence ( πS◦φ)S⊂I, S ﬁnite
converges to φ in HomR(M,M ) and therefore φ is compact.
Conversely assume that φ is compact and let ϵ >0. By deﬁnition there exists
a ﬁnite rank operator φ′ such that |φ−φ′| < ϵ. Since φ′ has ﬁnite rank, there
exists by Lemma II.1.8 a ﬁnite set S ⊂ J such that |πS◦φ′−φ′| < ϵ. Thus
|πS◦φ−φ| =|(πS◦φ−πS◦φ′) + (πS◦φ′−φ′) + (φ′−φ)|≤ max(ϵ,ϵ,ϵ ) =ϵ. This
means that supi∈I|ai,j|<ϵ forj̸∈S, which shows that supi∈I|ai,j| converges to 0.
□
Let us record an important technical result that we have obtained while proving
Prop. II.1.9:
Scholium II.1.10. If φ∈ HomR(M,N ) is compact, then the sequence of oper-
ators (πSφ)S⊂J, Sﬁnite converges in operator norm to φ.

§, we shall need the following hypothesis about R:

Hypothesis II.1.11. Among the elements r∈R such that|r|< 1, there is one
of largest norm which is multiplicative (see Exercise II.1.1).
We choose such an elementπ. It is then clear that the set of non-zero norms|R∗|
is the discrete subgroup |π|Z of the multiplicative group of positive real numbers.
We denote by R0 the set of elements r of R such that|r|≤ 1, which is a subring
of R, and by ˜R the quotient ring R0/πR0. For M a Banach R-module we deﬁne
M 0 ={m∈ M,|m|≤ 1}, which is an R0-submodule of M, and ˜M = M 0/πM 0,
which is a ˜R-module.
Lemma II.1.12. Assume Hypothesis II.1.11. Let M be a Banach R-module
such that |M|⊂| R|. Let (ei)i∈I be a family of elements of M 0, and for i∈ I
denote by ˜ei the image of ei in ˜M. Then (ei)i∈I is an orthonormal basis of M if
and only if (˜ei)i∈I is a basis (in the algebraic sense) of ˜M. In particular, M is
orthonormalizable if and only if ˜M is free over ˜R.
Proof — We note that since π is multiplicative,|πm| =|π||m| for every m∈M:
the proof is the same as in Exercise II.1.1.
Assume that (˜ei) is a basis of ˜M. If m∈M 0, denote by ˜m the image ofm in ˜M.
We can write ˜m =∑αi˜ei with theαi in ˜R, almost all 0. Choosing lifts a1
i of theαi
inR0, we havem−∑a1
iei =πm1 withm1∈M 0. Applying the same result to m1,
we get m−∑a2

ien =πnmn
with mn∈M0. By construction, the sequence ( an

hence is Cauchy, and therefore converges to an element ai∈ R0 for every i∈ I.
One has m =∑aiei. If |m| = 1, then some a1

thus|m| = supi|ai|. By replacing m by πnm for the n such that|m| =|π|−n, we
see that the same results holds for any m∈M. Hence (ei) is an orthonormal basis
for M.
The other direction is easy and left to the reader. □
Theorem II.1.13. Any Banach Qp-module is potentially orthonormalizable.
Proof — LetM be a Banach Qp-module with norm|| . Set|m|′ = infr∈pZ,r≥|m|r.

Definition II.1.16. We denote byR{{T}} the ring of power series∑∞
n=0anTn
with an ∈ R that converge everywhere, in the sense that |an|Cn → 0 for every
positive real C.
Lemma II.1.14 says that the series ∑∞
n=0cnTn belongs to R{{T}}. We call
it the Fredholm’s determinant of φ and denote it by det(1 −Tφ ). Lemma II.1.15
says that the assignment φ↦→ det(1−Tφ ) is continuous if we provide the set of
===== PDF PAGE 60 =====
60 II. EIGENVARIETIES
compact operators with the operator norm and R{{T}} with the norm of uniform
convergence of coeﬃcients (that is the norm |∑anTn| = supn|an|).
Note however that at this point, the Fredholm’s determinant of φ seems to
depend on the choice of the orthonormal basis ( ei). We shall soon show (Corol-
lary II.1.18) that it does not, and which is more that it depends only on the equiv-
alence class of the norm || on M.
If φ∈ EndR(M) is such that φ(M) is contained in a ﬁnite free sub-module
P of M, then we can give another, purely algebraic deﬁnition of the power series
det(1−Tφ ), which in this case is a polynomial that we shall denote by det(1−Tφ )P :
we consider the restriction of φ to P , which is an endomorphism of the ﬁnite free
module P , and deﬁne det(1 −Tφ )P as the determinant of (1 −Tφ|P ), which is a
polynomial of degree the rank ofP . It is not clear at this point that det(1−Tφ )P is
independent of P , or coincide with the power series det(1−Tφ ) constructed above
using the basis (ei).
However, when S is a ﬁnite subset of I, and PS the ﬁnite free submodule of I
generated by the es, s∈S, and φ has image in PS, then
det(1−Tφ )PS = det(1−Tφ ) (II.1.2)
as is seen immediately by extending the determinant of det(1 −Tφ|PS) and com-
paring with the deﬁnition of det(1 −Tφ ).
Let now N be an orthonormalizable R-module with basis ( fj)j∈J. If φ ∈
HomR(M,N ) and φ′∈ HomR(N,M ) are two operators, S⊂ J and S′⊂ I two
ﬁnite sets such that φ(M)⊂PS and φ′(N)⊂PS′, then
det(1−Tφφ′) = det(1−Tφ′φ) (II.1.3)
because the equation reduces by (II.1.2) to det(1 −Tφφ′)PS′ = det(1−Tφ′φ)PS
and is thus a well-known property of determinants of endomorphisms of ﬁnite free
modules.
Proposition II.1.17. LetM,N be two orthonormalizableR-modules with bases
(ei)i∈I and (fj)j∈J respectively. Let φ∈ HomR(M,N ) be a compact operator and
let u∈ HomR(N,M ). Then det(1−Tφu ) = det(1−Tuφ ). (Here det(1−Tφu ) is
computed in the basis (fj) and det(1−Tuφ ) in the basis (ei).)
Proof — Note that both members make sense, since both uφ∈ EndR(M) and
φu∈ EndR(N) are compact by Lemma II.1.4.
Since (πSφ)S⊂J, S ﬁnite converges in norm toφ (Scholium II.1.10), and therefore
also (uπSφ)S touφ and (πSφu)S toφu, it is enough by Lemma II.1.15 to prove the
result for φ replaced by πSφ where S⊂ J is a ﬁnite set. In other words, we may
and will assume that φ has image contained in PS.

Now let S′ be a ﬁnite subset of I. The operator πS′u has image contained in
PS′, hence one has by (II.1.3)
det(1−TπS′uφ) = det(1−TφπS′u).
Since uφ is compact, the sequence ( πS′uφ)S′ converges to uφ (Scholium II.1.10),
hence the left hand side det(1−TπS′uφ) converges to det(1−Tuφ ) (Lemma II.1.15).
As for the right hand side, it is equal by (II.1.2) to the algebraic determinant
det(1−TφπS′u|PS) since φ, and therefore φπS′u, have image contained in PS. But
πS′u|PS converge in norm to u|PS, hence det(1−TφπS′u|PS) converges to det(1−
Tφu|PS) = det(1−Tφu )
The proposition follows. □
Corollary II.1.18. If φ∈ EndR(M) is compact, the Fredholm’s determinant
det(1−Tφ ) of φ depends only on φ and on the topological module M, and not on
the orthonormal basis (ei) nor on the norm deﬁning the topology on M.
Proof — We need to prove that if (fi) is an orthonormal basis for some norm ||′
equivalent to|| , and u∈ HomR(M,M ) is the continuous invertible map sending
(ei) to (fi), then det(1−Tφ ) = det(1−Tuφu−1). But this follows from Prop. II.1.17
applied to the continuous operator u and the compact operator φu−1. □
II.1.6. Property (Pr). A Banach module P overR is said to have property
(Pr) if there exists a Banach module Q such that P⊕Q is potentially orthonor-
malizable.
Exercise II.1.19. Show that a Banach module P has property (Pr) if and only if for
every continuous surjectionf :M→N, and every continuous map α :P→N there exists
a continuous map β :P→M such that fβ =α.
Proposition II.1.20. If a ﬁnite R-module has property (Pr), then it is projec-
tive.
Proof — IfP is a ﬁniteR-module, choose a surjective continuous mapf :Ar→P
and apply Exercise II.1.19 to α = Id P : P → P. We get β : P → Ar such that
fβ = IdP, hence P≃β(P ) is a direct summand of Ar and is therefore projective.
□
If P have property (Pr), and φ∈ EndR(P ) is compact, one can deﬁne det(1 −
Tφ ) as follows: one chooses Q such that P⊕Q is potentially orthonormalizable,
deﬁnes ˜φ∈ EndR(P⊕Q) by extendingφ by zero, and sets det(1−Tφ ) = det(1−T ˜φ).
We need to check that if we have chosen two modulesQ andQ′ such thatP⊕Q and
===== PDF PAGE 62 =====
62 II. EIGENVARIETIES
P⊕Q′ are potentially orthonormalizable, the resulting Fredholm’s determinant is
the same, but this is true because both are equal to the determinant of the extension
by zero of φ on P⊕Q⊕Q′, by Prop. II.1.17.
It is easy to check that Prop. II.1.17 is still true for compact morphisms between
modules having the property (Pr).
Proposition II.1.21. Assume that R is noetherian. Let P be an R-module
with property (Pr) and φ a compact operator on P such that 1−φ is nilpotent.
Then P is ﬁnite and projective.
Proof — If (1−φ)n = 0 on P , then by expanding we see that the identity is
compact on P . Let Q be such that M :=P⊕Q is potentially orthonormalizable,
and let p denote the projector of M onto P alongside Q. Let u∈ EndR(M) be
the extension by 0 of the identity of P . Since u is compact, by Scholium II.1.10
there exists a projector q in EndR(M) whose image is ﬁnite free and such that
|u−qu|< 1/|p|. Then pu|P = IdP and|pu−pqu|< 1 so|Idp− pqu|P|< 1, which
shows that the morphismpqu|P =pq :P→P is invertible. Hence the image pq(P )
of that morphism isP . Therefore P is a submodule of p(q(M)) which is ﬁnite since
q(M) is, and P itself is ﬁnite. By Prop. II.1.20, it is also projective. □
II.1.7. Extension of scalars. Let us begin by recalling the deﬁnition of the

initial object of the category whose objects are pairs ( P,f ) where P is a Banach
R-module and f : M×N→ P a continuous R-bilinear map, and morphisms are
obviously deﬁned. Moreover one sees on the deﬁnition that | ˜f|≤| f|.
Consider now a morphism of Banach algebras R→R′. Then for M a Banach
module over R, M ˆ⊗RR′ is a Banach module over R′.

II.2. Everywhere convergent formal series and Riesz’s theory
We keep the running hypothesis of the preceding section: R is a Qp-Banach
algebra with a norm|| extending the absolute value of Qp. However, instead of the
multiplicative norm on R, it will be more convenient from now on to work with its
additive counterpart, the valuation vp on R deﬁned as vp(f) =− log|f|/ logp (in
particular vp(p) = 1). One has vp(fg )≥vp(f) +vp(g) but this inequality may be
strict; in particular, if f∈R∗,vp(f) +vp(f−1) may be non-zero. When it is 0, f is
multiplicative and vp(fg ) =vp(f) +vp(g) for every g∈R, cf. Exercise II.1.1.
We recall the ring R{{T}} of everywhere convergent formal power series, in-
troduced in the preceding section, cf. Deﬁnition II.1.16. In the language of the
valuation vp, this is the ring of power series ∑∞
n=0anTn, with an∈ R, such that
vp(an)−nν→∞ for every ν∈ R.
II.2.1. The ν-valuation and ν-dominant polynomials. Let ν∈ R. For
F = ∑∞
n=0anTn ∈ R[[T ]] we set v(F,ν ) = inf n∈Nvp(an)−nν. It is clear that
F ∈ R{{T}} is equivalent to having v(F,ν ) > −∞ for all ν ∈ R; it is even
suﬃcient to check it for a family of ν’s that tends to + ∞. For F,G ∈ R{{T}},
one has v(F +G,ν )≥ min(v(F,ν ),v (G,ν )) and v(FG,ν )≥ v(F,ν ) +v(G,ν ). In
other words, v(·,ν ) deﬁnes a valuation on R{{T}} which in turn deﬁnes a uniform
topology on R{{T}}.
Exercise II.2.1. Show that the topologies deﬁned by v(·,ν ) and v(·,ν′) are diﬀerent
if ν̸=ν′.
Exercise II.2.2. Let Fn be a sequence of power series in R{{T}}. Assume that for
everyν∈ R,Fn is Cauchy for the topology deﬁned by v(·,ν ). Then show that there exists
an F in R{{T}} such that for every ν∈ R, (Fn) converges to F for the topology deﬁned
byv(·,ν ).

Definition II.2.4. A polynomial Q(T )∈R[T ] is called ν-dominant if
(i) Q has degree N(Q,ν )
(ii) The dominant term of Q is invertible.
Lemma II.2.5. If Q(T )∈R[T ] is a polynomial whose dominant term is invert-
ible, there exists ν0∈ R such that for all ν≥ν0, Q is ν-dominant.
Proof — WriteQ(X) =∑d
n=0adTd with ad invertible. Let ν0 = max(0,vp(ad)−
vp(ad−1),...,v p(ad)−vp(a0)). Then if ν≥ν0 and 1≤i≤d, one has vp(ad)−dν =

ν0−iν≤ν0−ν≤ 0. This shows that Q is ν-dominant. □

Adding these two inequalities and simplifying gives vp(z)≥− ν. The converse is
easy. □

II.2.2. Euclidean division.
Proposition II.2.8. LetF∈R{{T}} and let B∈R[T ] a non-zero polynomial
whose dominant term is invertible in R. Then there exists a unique Q∈ R{{T}}
and S∈ R[T ] with degS <degB such that F = BQ +S. Furthermore we have,
for any ν∈ R, v(S,ν )≥v(F,ν ), and if the dominant term of B is multiplicative,
v(Q,ν )≥v(F,ν )−v(B,ν ).
Proof — We ﬁrst observe that it is enough to prove the proposition when B
is monic. Indeed, assume the theorem is known for B monic, and let b ∈ R∗
be the dominant term of B, so that B = bB0 with B0 monic. Then we have a
decompositionF =B0Q0+S withQ0∈R{{T}} andS∈R[T ] with degS <degB0
and v(S,ν )≥ v(F,ν ), v(Q0,ν )≥ v(F,ν )−v(B0,ν ). Then setting Q = b−1Q0 we
haveF =BQ+S and this decompositions satisﬁes all the stated conditions, namely
degS < degQ, v(S,ν )≥ v(F,ν ) and if b is multiplicative, v(Q,ν )≥ v(F,ν )−
v(B,ν ). Moreover the uniqueness of such a decomposition follows immediately
from the uniqueness in the case where B is monic.
So let us assume henceforth that B is monic.
For uniqueness we need to show that if BQ +S = 0 with deg S < degB,
then Q = 0. By Lemma II.2.5, B is ν-dominant for some ν∈ R, ν≥ 0, so we
can write B = Td +bd−1Td−1 +··· +b0 with vp(bi)−iν ≥− dν for i≤ d. If
Q = ∑qnTn is non-zero, let N = N(Q,ν ) so that vp(qn)−nν > vp(qN)−Nν
if n > N. Then the coeﬃcient of TN+d in BQ is qN +∑d
i=1qN+ibd−i, and for
1≤ i≤ d, vp(qN+ibd−i)≥ vp(qN+i) +vp(bd−i) > vp(qN) +iν + (−i)ν = vp(qN),
hence the coeﬃcient of TN+d inBQ is non-zero, and BQ +S̸= 0, a contradiction.
We now make an observation crucial to the proof of existence. Recall that
when F is a polynomial, then the existence of polynomials Q and S such that
F = BQ +S, deg S < degB is well-known. We claim that in this case, if B is
monic, then for any ν∈ R,
v(Q,ν )≥v(F,ν )−v(B,ν ), v(S,ν )≥v(F,ν ).(II.2.1)
We prove this by induction onn = degF . Forn< degB, one hasQ = 0,S =F and
the result is clear. So let n≥ degB and assume the result is true for all polynomials
of degree < n. Let anTn be the dominant term of F . Then by induction we can
write F−anTn = BQ0 +S0 with degS0 < degB and Q0, S0 satisfying (II.2.1),
that is v(Q0,ν )≥ v(F−anTn,ν )−v(B,ν ), v(S0,ν )≥ v(F−anTn,ν ). Since
v(F−anTn,ν )≥v(F,ν ) , one has:
v(Q0,ν )≥v(F,ν )−v(B,ν ), v(S0,ν )≥v(F,ν ).(II.2.2)
LetTd be the dominant term of B. We can write F =B(Q0 +anTn−d) +S0−
(B−Td)anTn−d. The polynomial −(B−Td)anTn−d has degree < n, so can also
===== PDF PAGE 67 =====
II.2. EVERYWHERE CONVERGENT FORMAL SERIES AND RIESZ’S THEORY 67
be written BQ1 +S1, with deg S1 < degB and Q1,S 1 satisfying (II.2.1), that is
v(Q1,ν )≥v((B−Td)anTn−d,ν )−v(B,ν ), v(S1,ν )≥v((B−Td)anTn−d,ν ). But
v(anTn−d,ν ) =vp(an)− (n−d)ν≥v(F,ν )−v(B,ν ),(II.2.3)
and therefore
v((B−Td)anTn−d,ν )≥v(B−Td,ν )+v(anTn−d,ν )≥v(Bν)+v(F,ν )−v(B,ν ) =v(F,ν ).
Thus
v(Q1,ν )≥v(F,ν )−v(B,ν ), v(S1,ν )≥v(F,ν ).(II.2.4)
Hence F = B(Q0 +Q1 +anTn−d) +S0 +S1, and by uniqueness Q = (Q0 +
Q1 +anTn−d) and S =S0 +S1. Now S satisﬁes (II.2.1) because S0 and S1 satisfy
(II.2.2) and (II.2.4) respectively, and Q satisﬁes (II.2.1) by (II.2.2), (II.2.4) and
(II.2.3). This completes the induction step and the proof of (II.2.1).
Now we prove the existence. Let F =∑∞
n=0anTn∈ R{{T}}. Write anTn =
BQn +Sn with Qn,Sn∈R[T ], degSn < degB. Let ν∈ R. Since v(anTn,ν ) goes
to 0, we see by (II.2.1) and Exercise II.2.2 that the series ∑∞
n=0Qn and∑
n=0Sn
converge respectively to Q∈R{{T}} andS∈R{{T}} for the topology deﬁned by
v(·,ν ) for any ν∈ R. Actually S is a polynomial of degree < degB since the Sn
are. Since F =BQ +S, the proposition is proved. □

R{{T}}/(B(T )) is an isomorphism.

II.2.4. A piece of resultant theory. Until further notice, R may be any

Following Coleman, we deﬁne, if B∈R[T ] and P (T ) = 1−a1T +a2T 2 +··· +
(−1)nanTn∈ R[T ] is of degree n, a polynomial D(B,P ) in R[T ] as follows: let

in those variables (i.e. s1 = t1 +··· +tn, sn = t1...t n, etc.) as elements of
R[T ][t1,...,t n]. The polynomial ∏n
i=1(1−TB (ti)) is symmetric in the ti, hence
lies in R[T ][sn,...,s n]; we denote by D(B,P ) the image of that product in R[T ]
by the morphism of R[T ]-algebras R[T ][s1,...,s n]→R[T ] sending si to ai. With
a slight abuse of notation, we can write like Coleman:
D(B,P )(T ) =
n∏
i=1
(1−TB (ti)), where si(t1,...,t n) =ai, i= 1,...,n.
If R is embedded in an algebraically closed ﬁeld K, and t1,...,t n are the roots
of the reciprocal polynomial P∗(T ) in K (with multiplicity), then the formula
D(B,P )(T ) =∏n
i=1(1−TB (ti)) is true in K.
Lemma II.2.13. LetB,C,P,Q ∈R[T ] with P (0) =Q(0) = 1. We have:
(i) D(B,PQ ) =D(B,P )D(P,Q ).
(ii) D(1−P∗,P )(T ) = (1−T )n.
(iii) D(1−P∗,Q +CP )(1) =D(1−P∗,Q )(1)
Proof — Note that if f :R→R′ is a morphism of rings, then D(f(B),f (P )) =
f(D(B,P )). Clearly it is enough to prove then for a ring of polynomials R =
Z[x1,...,x m] with suﬃciently many variables. This ring R is a domain and it
is enough to prove equations (i), (ii) and (iii) for an algebraically closed ﬁeld K
containingR. Thus we can assume that R is an algebraically closed ﬁeld K.
In this case, formula (i) is obvious, because the set of roots of P∗Q∗ = (PQ )∗
(with multiplicity) is the union of the sets of roots of P∗ and of Q∗. Formula (ii) is
clear too since D(1−P∗,P )(T ) =∏n
i=1(1−T (1−P∗(ti))) = (1−T )n. We leave
(iii) as an exercise to the reader. □
We now are back to our running hypothesis that R is a Banach algebra over
Qp.
ForB ﬁxed, the coeﬃcients of D(B,P ) are polynomials in the coeﬃcients ai
of P . They are therefore continuous in P for any of the valuations v(P,ν ). This
allows us, if P =∑∞

Proposition II.2.15. Let P ∈ R{{T}} with P (0) = 1 , and suppose that one
can write P = QS with Q∈ R[T ] such that Q(0) = 1, and S∈ R{{T}}. Assume
that the ideal generated by Q and S is the unit ideal. Let n be the degree of Q.
Then D(1−Q∗,P )(T ) has a good zero of order n at 1.

Proposition II.2.16. Let M be an R-module satisfying property (Pr ) and φ
be a compact operator of M. Let Q(T ) be a polynomial with Q(0) = 0 . Then
D(Q, det(1−Tφ )) = det(1−TQ (φ)).
Proof — SinceQ(0) = 0,Q(φ) is compact and the right hand side of the equality
makes sense. To prove it, we are reduced as usual to the case where M is orthonor-
malizable, and then to the case where φ is of ﬁnite rank. In this case, the formula
is purely algebraic and we shall prove it for any commutative ring R. Actually,
standard argument shows that is its enough to prove it when R is an algebraic
closed ﬁeldK. In this case, we can write det(1−Tφ ) = (1−a1T )... (1−anT ) with
a1,...,a n∈K∗ being the eigenvalues of φ (with multiplicity counted according to
the characteristic polynomial). By deﬁnition of D, one has D(Q, det(1−Tφ )) =
(1−Q(a1))... (1−Q(an)) which is just det(1−TQ (φ)). □
II.2.5. Riesz’s theory. In this subsection we keep assuming that R is a Ba-
nach algebra over Qp and we assume in addition that R is noetherian.
We now consider a Banach moduleM overR which has property (Pr). Let φ∈
EndR(M) be a compact operator. We denote by Pφ(T )∈R{{T}} the Fredholm’s
determinant of φ, det(1−Tφ ).
Proposition II.2.17. Assume that a∈R is a good zero of order s ofPφ. Then
there exists a unique decomposition M =N⊕F whereN andF are closedφ-stable
sub-modules of M, N is ﬁnite projective and F has property (Pr), and 1−aφ is
nilpotent onN and invertible onF . Moreover, N andF are stable by every operator
u∈ EndR(M) that commutes with φ.
===== PDF PAGE 70 =====
70 II. EIGENVARIETIES
Proof — The result is trivial when s = 0, so let us assume that s≥ 1. Let R′
be the closure of the subring of End R(M) generated by φ, so R′ is a commutative
Banach algebra. Deﬁne a power series Q(T ) in R′{{T}} by
(1−Tφ )Q(T ) =Pφ(T ).(II.2.5)
Applying (II.2.5) to a gives (1−aφ)Q(a) =Pφ(a) = 0. Deriving (II.2.5) i times for
i = 1,...,s and evaluating at a gives
(1−aφ)Qi(a)−iφQi−1(a) =P (i)
φ (a).(II.2.6)
Since P (i)(a) = 0 for i < s, we see by induction on i that (1−aφ)i+1Q(i)(a) = 0
for i < s. Since a is a good zero of order s of Pφ, c := P (s)
φ (a) is invertible in R.
Equation (II.2.6) for i = s gives (1−aφ)Qs(a)−iφQs−1(a) = c which, if we set
e :=c−1(1−aφ)Q(s)(a) and f :=−iφQs−1(a) can be written as e +f = 1. Since
(1−tφ)sQ(s−1)(a) = 0, we have esf = 0. We deﬁne p = es, q = (e +f)s−es, so
we have again p +q = 1 and pq = 0 since q is divisible by f in R′⊂ EndR(M).
It follows that p and q are orthogonal projectors in R′. Let F be the image of p
and N image of q; they are closed submodules of M (as they are also respectively
kernels of q andp), they are φ-stable (as p andq are in R′ and thus commute with
φ) and M = N⊕F . Since (1 −aφ)sq = 0, 1−aφ is nilpotent on N. It follows
(Prop. II.1.21) that N is projective of ﬁnite type; the submodule F has (Pr) since
it is a direct summand of M. Finally, by elevating the deﬁnition of e to the power
s we get (1−aφ)sQ(s)(a)s =csp which shows that 1−aφ is invertible on Imp =F .
This proves the existence of the decomposition.
For the uniqueness, let M = N′⊕F′ an other decomposition satisfying the
required properties. Consider the closed φ-stable submodule N′′ = p(N +N′) of
M. On N′′,φ is nilpotent since it is nilpotent on N +N′, and invertible since it is
invertible on F . Thus N′′ = 0 and N =N′. That F =F′ is proven the same way.
The last sentence is clear since we deﬁned M and F has image of projectors
that are power series in φ. □

u∈ EndR(M) that commutes with φ.
This theorem reduces to the preceding proposition in the case where Q has the

II.3. Adapted pairs
In this section, we restrict somewhat the setting of the preceding section. We
will assume that the Banach algebraR over Qp is actually a reduced aﬃnoid algebra,
provided with its natural supremum norm extending the absolute value over Qp.
We denote by Sp R the maximal spectrum of R, and if x∈ SpR we denote by
Lx the residue ﬁeld at x, which is a ﬁnite extension of Qp. If f ∈ R, we write
f(x)∈ Lx for the evaluation of f at x∈ SpR. By deﬁnition of the supremum
norm, vp(f) = inf x∈SpRvp(f(x)) and by the maximum modulus principle, this
inﬁmum is also a minimum. For f∈R∗, f is multiplicative (cf. Exercise II.1.1) if
and only if vp(f(x)) is constant on Sp R.
An element F∈R{{T}} deﬁnes a rigid analytic function on the rigid analytic
variety SpR× A1
rig where A1
rig is the rigid analytic aﬃne line over Qp.
II.3.1. Strongly ν-dominant polynomials. Let F∈R{{T}}. If x∈ SpR
is a closed point of ﬁeld of deﬁnitionL(x), we writeFx(T ) for the series∑∞
n=0an(x)Tn
which belongs to L(x){{T}}.
Lemma II.3.1. If N(Fx,ν ) is a constant N independent of x ∈ SpR, then
N(F,ν ) =N. Moreover, for b∈R∗ and for x∈ SpR, N(b(x)Fx,ν ) =N(bF,ν ) =
N.
Proof — The hypothesis means that vp(an(x))−nν≥ vp(aN(x))−Nν for all
n and all x, the inequality being strict if n > N. Taking the inf on x∈ SpR
gives vp(an)−nν≥vp(aN)−Nν for all n. For a ﬁxed n > N, there is an x such
that vp(an(x)) =vp(an) by the maximum modulus principle, hence vp(an)−nν =
vp(an(x)−nν)>v p(aN(x))−Nν≥vp(aN)−Nν . This means that N(F,ν ) =N.
To prove the second sentence, note that it is clear thatN(b(x)Fx,ν ) =N(Fx,ν ) =
N since b(x)̸= 0. It follows that N(bF,ν ) =N by the ﬁrst part. □
Definition II.3.2. A polynomial Q(T )∈R[T ] is called stronglyν-dominant if
(i) Q has degree N(Q,ν )
(ii) For all x∈ SpR, N(Q,ν ) =N(Qx,ν )
===== PDF PAGE 72 =====
72 II. EIGENVARIETIES
Note that if a polynomial Q(T )∈R[T ] is strongly ν-dominant, then its domi-
nant term aN(Q,ν) is invertible in R, for if it was not, aN(Q,ν)(x) would be zero for
some x, and we would have N(Qx,ν )≤ degQx < degQ = N(Q,ν ). This shows
that a strongly ν-dominant polynomial is ν-dominant, and more precisely, that a
polynomial Q is strongly ν-dominant if and only if it is ν-dominant and all the Qx,
for x∈ SpR, are ν-dominant.
Exercise II.3.3. If Q(T )∈ R[T ] is a polynomial whose dominant term is invertible,
there exists ν0∈ R such that for all ν≥ν0, Q is strongly ν-dominant.
Exercise II.3.4. Let Q be a polynomial of degree N. Assume that N =N(Q,ν ) and
that aN is multiplicative. Show that Q is strongly ν-dominant. Show that the converse is
false: a polynomial of degree N can beν-dominant without satisfyingvp(aN)+vp(a−1
N ) = 0.
Exercise II.3.5. Let Q be a polynomial of degree N and a∈R∗.
(1) Show that if a is multiplicative and Q is ν-dominant, then aQ is ν-dominant.
(2) Show that if Q is strongly ν-dominant, then aQ is strongly ν-dominant.
(3) But show by an example that ifa is not multiplicative,aQ may not beν-dominant
even if Q is.
II.3.2. A canonical factorization of everywhere convergent power se-
ries.
Theorem II.3.6. For F (T )∈ R{{T}} with F (0) invertible in R, and ν∈ R,
the following are equivalent:
(i) One has N(Fx,ν ) =N(F,ν ) for all x∈ SpR.
(ii) There exists a decompositionF =PG inR{{T}}, with P (0) = 1, whereP
is a strongly ν-dominant polynomial of degree N(F,ν ) and N(Gx,ν ) = 0
for all x∈W .
Moreover, if those properties hold, the decomposition F =PG of (ii) is unique.
Proof — Assume (i). Let N = N(F,ν ). Write F =∑∞
i=0aiTi. For x∈ SpR,
vp(aN(x))−Nν ≤ vp(a0) = 0, hence vp(aN(x)) < +∞ and aN(x) is not zero.
Therefore aN is invertible. If we replace F by a−1
N F , then hypothesis (i) is still
satisﬁed by Lemma II.3.1, and this change doesn’t aﬀect (ii). Hence we may and

Corollary II.3.7. Assume that L is a ﬁnite extension of Qp with its absolute
value. Let F (T )∈L{{T}} with F (0) = 1, and ν∈ R. Then there exists a unique
decompositionF = PG , with P ∈ L[T ], G∈ L{{T}}, P (0) = G(0) = 1 , P ν-
dominant of degree N(F,ν ), N(G,ν ) = 0. Moreover the zeros z in ¯L of F (T ) with
vp(z)≥−ν, are the same, with multiplicity, than the one of P and their number is
N(F,ν ).

Proof — The ﬁrst assertion follows from (ii) of the above theorem since (i) is

II.3.3. Adapted pairs. We begin by giving a rigid-analytic geometric inter-
pretation of Theorem II.3.6. As above R is a reduced aﬃnoid; we set W = SpR.
Fix an F∈R{{T}} and assume F (0) = 1. Let Z be the analytic subvariety of
W× A1
rig cut out by F (T ). Let f be the projection map Z→W . For ν∈ R, let
Zν be the aﬃnoid of Z of points z whose component in A1
rig havevp(z)≥−ν, and
let fν be the restriction of f to Zν. We record some elementary properties of the
map fν :Zν→W .
Lemma II.3.8. The ﬁber at x∈W of fν is a ﬁnite scheme of degree N(Fx,ν ),
whose points are the z such that Fx(z) = 0 and vp(z)≥− ν. In particular, fν is
quasi-ﬁnite. Moreover fν is quasi-compact, separated and ﬂat.
Proof — Using a decomposition Fx =PxGx as in Cor. II.3.7, we see that the ﬁber
at x is deﬁned by the equation Px(z) = 0, which is equivalent to Fx(z) = 0 and
vp(z)≥− ν. The degree of this ﬁnite scheme is the number of zeros of Px in an
algebraic closure of L(x) (counted with multiplicity), that is deg Px =N(Px,ν ) =
N(Fx,ν ). Since N(Fx,ν ) is always ﬁnite, it follows that fν is quasi-ﬁnite. That fν
is separated and quasi-compact is clear.
It remains to prove thatfν is ﬂat. We may assume thatν = 0 by rescaling, hence
thatF∈R⟨T⟩, and we just need to prove thatR⟨T⟩/(F ) is ﬂat overR. Since R⟨T⟩
is ﬂat, this can be done by applying [ 75, Theorem 22.6], which reduces to proving
that for every maximal ideal m of R⟨T⟩, the image of F in R⟨T⟩/(m∩R)R⟨T⟩ =
R/(m∩R)⟨T⟩ is not a zero-divisor. But the last ring is a domain, and the image
of F is non-zero (since F (0) = 1), so we are done. □
Proposition II.3.9. For F (T ) ∈ R{{T}} with F (0) = 1 , and ν ∈ R, the
following are equivalent:
(i) The map fν :Zν→W is ﬁnite.
(ii) There exists a decomposition F = PG in R{{T}}, with P (0) = G(0) =
1, where P is a strongly ν-dominant polynomial of degree N(F,ν ) and
N(Gx,ν ) = 0 for all x∈W .
(iii) One has N(Fx,ν ) =N(F,ν ) for all x∈W ,
If those properties hold, the decomposition F = PG as in (ii) is unique, the ideal
(P,G ) is the unit ideal of R{{T}}, Zν is disconnected from its complement in Z
(that is, there is an idempotent e∈O (Z) such that Z is deﬁned by e = 1 ) and
fν :Zν→W is ﬁnite ﬂat surjective of degree degQ =N(F,ν ).
===== PDF PAGE 76 =====
76 II. EIGENVARIETIES
Proof — If (i) holds, since fν is ﬁnite ﬂat, its ﬁbers all have the same rank, in
other words the power series Fx all have the same number (with multiplicity) of
zeros z satisfying vp(z)≥− ν, which means that N(Fx,ν ) is constant on W by
Corollary II.3.7, hence that N(Fx,ν ) =N(F,ν ) by Lemma II.3.1, and (iii) holds.
Also, if (iii) holds, then the ﬁbers of f : Zν→ W all have the same degrees
N(F,ν ). Since fν is ﬂat, separated and quasi-compact, fν is ﬁnite by a result of
Conrad ([41, Theorem A.1.2]) and (i) holds.
We have already seen the equivalence of (ii) and (iii) in Theorem II.3.6. Hence
the equivalence between (i), (ii) and (iii). The uniqueness of the decomposition in
(ii) has also been proved in Theorem II.3.6.
When they hold, the natural injective map j :Zν→Z is ﬁnite, hence Zν is the
support of the coherent sheaf j∗OZν, and this is Zariski closed. Since Zν is also an
open admissible of Z, it is disconnected from its complement in Z. If we deﬁne a
function a onZ equal to 0 on Zν and 1/P on its complement (since P has no zero
on that complement), and a function b equal to 1/G onZν (sinceG has no zero on
Zν) and 0 on its complement, then aP +bG = 1, hence the ideal (P,G ) is the unit
ideal. The other assertions are clear. □
Definition II.3.10. A power seriesF (T )∈R{{T}} withF (0) = 1 being ﬁxed,
we say that ν∈ R is adapted to F if the conditions of the above proposition are
satisﬁed.
When F is ﬁxed, for X an aﬃnoid open of W , of aﬃnoid ring RX, we call
FX(T ) the image of F (T ) in RX{{T}} and deﬁne ZX = f−1(X). Clearly ZX is
also the analytic subvariety in X× A1
rig cut by FX. We shall say that ν is adapted
toX (or thatX andν are adapted, or that (X,ν ) is an adapted pair) ifν is adapted
to FX. In this case ZX,ν :=ZX∩Zν is ﬁnite ﬂat surjective over X, and we have a
decomposition FX(T ) =QX(T )GX(T ) in RX{{T}} as in Prop. II.3.9.
Of course, this notion is useful only if we can prove that there are enough
adapted pairs (X,ν ).
Proposition II.3.11. For any realν, and anyx∈W , there exists an admissible
aﬃnoid neighborhoodX of x such that (X,ν ) is adapted.

Corollary II.3.12. The family of aﬃnoid subdomains ZX,ν of Z for X an
aﬃnoid of Z and ν adapted to X is a covering of Z.
A stronger version of this corollary is true. It is not needed for the construction
and for establishing the main properties of eigenvarieties below, but we shall use it
to prove the crucial fact that an eigenvariety is separated.

II.4. Submodules of bounded slope
In this section we keep the assumption of the preceding subsection: R is a
reduced aﬃnoid ring whose normalized valuation is denoted byvp; ifx∈ SpR =X,
the residue ﬁeld at x is called L(x).

Definition II.4.1. LetM be a Banach R-module satisfying property (Pr) and
Up a compact R-linear operator of M. Let F (T ) = det(1 −TUp)∈ R{{T}} be
the Fredholm’s determinant of Up. We assume that ν is adapted for F (Deﬁni-
tion II.3.10). Then by Prop II.3.9, there is a unique decomposition F = PG in
R{{T}}, with P a strongly ν-dominant polynomial of degree N(F,ν ),G such that
N(Gx,ν ) = 0 for all x∈ SpR, and P (0) = G(0) = 1. Moreover P and G gen-
erate the unit ideal. According to Theorem II.2.18, there exists a unique φ-stable
decomposition M =N⊕F with N,F φ-stable closed submodules of M such that
P∗(Up) is nilpotent on U and invertible onL. We call the module N the submodule
of slope less than ν of M, and denote it by M≤ν.
Proposition II.4.2. Under the same hypotheses as in the above deﬁnition, the
moduleM≤ν is a ﬁnite projective module overR. It is stable by every endomorphism
of M that commutes with φ. The formation of M≤ν commute with base change, in
the sense that if R→R′ is a morphism of aﬃnoid rings over L, then ν is adapted
for the image of F (T ) in R′{{T}}, and (M ˆ⊗RR′)≤ν =M≤ν⊗RR′ as submodules
of M ˆ⊗RR′.
Proof — That M≤ν is ﬁnite projective, and stable by every endomorphism that
commutes with φ directly results from its deﬁnition and Theorem II.2.18. For
the commutation with base change, note that U′
p :=Up ˆ⊗1 is a compact operator of
M ˆ⊗RR′ whose power series is the imageF′(T ) ofF (T ) inR′{{T}} by Lemma I.4.1
the decomposition F = QG in R{{T}} deﬁnes a decomposition F′ = Q′G′ which
obviously still satisﬁes (ii) of Prop II.3.9. Hence ν is still adapted for F′, and
P∗(U′
p) = P∗(Up) ˆ⊗1. It follows then from the uniqueness of the decomposition in
Theorem II.2.18 that (M ˆ⊗RR′)≤ν =M≤ν⊗RR′. □

The phrase “submodule of slope less thanν” is in part explained by the following
easy result:
Lemma II.4.4. Assume that R =L is a ﬁnite extension of Qp, normed by the
normalized valuation vp. Then in the situation of the deﬁnition above, M≤ν is the
largestUp-stable subspace of M on which the roots (in an algebraic closure of L) of
the characteristic polynomial of Up all have valuation ≤ν.
===== PDF PAGE 80 =====
80 II. EIGENVARIETIES
Proof — By deﬁnition P∗(Up) is nilpotent on M≤ν, so the characteristic polyno-
mial χUp of Up on M≤ν divides a power of P∗(Up). By Lemma II.2.7, all the roots
of P (Up) have valuation≥−ν, hence all the roots of P∗(Up) and of χUp have val-
uation≤ν. To show that M≤ν is the largest ﬁnite-dimensional φ-stable subspace
with this property, assume there is an Up-stable subspace N⊂M containingMν.
Remember the decompositionM =M≤ν⊕F of Deﬁnition II.4.1. The projection of
N ontoF alongsideM≤ν is a non-zeroUp-stable sub-module ofN on whichQ∗(Up)
is nilpotent. Since Q∗(Up) is invertible on F , the projection of N on F must be
zero, that is N =M≤ν. □
We shall now give a simple characterization ofM≤µ, in the spirit of Lemma II.4.4.
Since we will not use it in this book, the reader may safely skip to the next section.
Lemma II.4.5. Let F be a Banach R-module having property (Pr), N⊂ F a
ﬁnite-type sub-module. If for every x∈ SpR the natural map Nx→ Fx is zero,
then N = 0. (Here Nx is the ﬁber of N at x, that is N⊗RL(x), and similarly for
Fx)
Proof — We ﬁrst assume that F is ﬁnite projective, say of rank n. Then the
hypothesis implies that ( F/N)x = Fx for all x∈ SpR. Hence the ﬁnite module
F/N has rankn at every closed point of SpecR, hence has rank n at every point of
SpecR since closed points are dense (aﬃnoid rings are Jacobson) and the rank of
(F/N)x is semi-continuous as a function of x∈ SpecR and bounded above by the
rank of Fx which is n. By Nakayama this means that F/N is projective, and thus
N is direct summand in F , which implies that the maps Nx→Fx are injective. By
hypothesis, Nx = 0 for all x, and since N is of ﬁnite type we conclude that N = 0.
We now assume only that F is orthonormalizable, with orthonormal basis
(ei)i∈I. For S a ﬁnite subset of I, we denote by FS the submodule of F gener-
ated by (ei)i∈S and by πS the projection of F onto FS alongside other vectors of
the basis. Then the hypothesis implies that πS(N)x→ (FS)x is the zero map for
all x∈ SpR, so by the above result applied to FS, πS(N) = 0, and since it is true
for all ﬁnite S,N = 0. The case where F is potentially orthonormalizable trivially
follows.
Finally, if F has (Pr), then F⊕F′ is potentially orthonormalizable for some
Banach module F′, and if we see N as a sub-module of F ⊂ F⊕F′ then Nx→
Fx⊕F′
x is zero, so N = 0 by the preceding case. □
Proposition II.4.6. Same hypothesis as in Deﬁnition II.4.1. The submodule
M≤ν of M is the largest ﬁnite type sub-module M′ of M which is Up-stable, and
such that for every x∈ SpR, all the roots (in an algebraic closure of L(x)) of the
characteristic polynomial of Up on the image of M′
x in Mx have valuation≤ν.

Let M′ be a sub-module of M which is Up-stable, and such that for every x∈

M≤ν⊕F and callingπ the projection ontoF alongM≤ν, let us setN =π(M′)⊂F .
Then N is a ﬁnite type sub-module of F , and for every x∈ SpR, the action of
P∗(Up) on the image of Nx in Fx is nilpotent, and since this action is invertible
on Fx, this image is 0. By the lemma above, we conclude that N = 0, hence
M′⊂M≤ν. □
II.5. Links
Definition II.5.1. If R is an aﬃnoid ring, M and M′ be two Banach R-
modules satisfying property (Pr) with morphisms ψ :H→ EndR(M) andψ′ :H→
EndR(M′) such that ψ(Up) and ψ′(Up) are compact, we say that M and M′ are
linked ifψ(Up) and ψ′(Up) have the same characteristic power series F (T ), and for
every monic polynomial Q∈R[T ] with invertible constant term, kerQ(ψ(Up)) and
kerQ(ψ′(Up)) are isomorphic as R-modules with action of H.
It is clear that to be linked is an equivalence relation. Observe that if M and
M′ are linked, thenν is adapted for M if and only if it is adapted for M′, and then
the two modules M≤ν and M′≤ν are isomorphic as R-modules with action of H.
Lemma II.5.2. LetM and M′ be as in the deﬁnition above. Assume that there
are two continuous homomorphism of R-modules andH-modulesf :M→M′ and
g :M′→M such that g◦f =ψ(Up) andf◦g =ψ(U′
p). If either f org is compact,
then M and M′ are linked.
Proof — That the characteristic power series of ψ(Up) and ψ′(Up) are the same
results from the equality in R{{T}}: det(1 − Tfg ) = det(1 − Tgf ) proved in
Lemma II.1.17. Moreover f maps kerQ(ψ(Up)) into ker Q(ψ′(Up)) since it is an
H-homomorphism and g maps kerQ(ψ′(Up)) into kerQ(ψ(Up)). The composition
g◦f = ψ(Up) on ker Q(ψ(Up)) is invertible since Q has invertible constant term,
and so is f◦g. Thus ker Q(ψ(Up)) and kerQ(ψ′(Up)) are isomorphic. □
An interesting special case is the following:
Lemma II.5.3. LetM,M′ be two R-modules withH-actions as above. Assume
that there are two homomorphisms of R-modules and H-modules f : M → M′
and g : M′→ M such that f is injective and continuous, g is compact, and f◦
===== PDF PAGE 82 =====
82 II. EIGENVARIETIES
g = ψ(U′
p). Then M and M′ are linked. Moreover, if f factors as a composition
M ↪→M′′↪→M′, where M′′ is as in the deﬁnition above and the two morphisms in
this factorization are injective continuous morphism of R-modules andH-modules,
then M and M′ are also linked to M′′.
Proof — For the ﬁrst assertion, we observe that f◦g◦f =ψ(U′
p)◦f =f◦ψ(Up)
hence by injectivity of f, g◦f = ψ(Up) and we can apply Lemma II.5.2. The
second assertion follows form the ﬁrst by replacing M by M′′, f by the injection
M′′↪→M′, and g by the composition of g :M′→M with the injection M→M′′.
□
Lemma II.5.4. If M and M′ are linked, and Sp R′→ SpR is a morphism of
aﬃnoid, them M ˆ⊗RR′ and M′ ˆ⊗RR′ are also linked.
Proof — This is clear from Lemma II.1.23. □
II.6. The eigenvariety machine
II.6.1. Eigenvariety data. An eigenvariety data over a ﬁnite extension L of
Qp is the data of
(ED1) A commutative ring H, with a distinguished element Up.
(ED2) A reduced rigid analytic variety W overL, and an admissible covering C
by admissible aﬃnoid open subsets of W.
(ED3) For every admissible aﬃnoid W = SpR ofW, W ∈ C, a Banach mod-
ule MW satisfying property (Pr), and a ring homomorphism ψW :H→
EndR(MW ).
satisfying the following conditions:
(EC1) For every W∈ C, ψW (Up) is compact on MW .
(EC2) For every pair of aﬃnoid subdomains W′ = Sp R′⊂ W = Sp R⊂W ,
W,W′ ∈ C, the R′-Banach modules MW ˆ⊗RR′ and MW′ are linked (cf.
Deﬁnition II.5.1)
In (ED3) it would suﬃce to give the modules MW up to a link, as our construc-
tions shall depend only on the link-equivalence classes of the modules MW .
Given an eigenvariety data, and a closed pointw∈W of ﬁeld of deﬁnitionL(w),
we can deﬁne the ﬁberMw as follows: choose an aﬃnoid admissible subdomain in C,
W = SpR, containingw, and setMw =MW⊗RL(w), where the implicit morphism
R→L(w) is the evaluation atw. Then the space Mw inherits a linear action ofH,
denoted byψw :H→ EndL(w)(Mw), and it is together with that action, well deﬁned

With no loss of generality, we can and, when convenient, will assume that if

well. It suﬃces to deﬁne MX as MW⊗RR′ if W = SpR and X = SpR′, which
makesMX well-deﬁned up to a link.
II.6.2. Construction of the eigenvariety. For any admissible aﬃnoid sub-
domain W = SpR in C, let FW∈R{{T}} be the characteristic power series of Up
acting on MW (that is FW (T ) = det(1−TψW (Up)).) To any such W , and ν∈ R
adapted to W (that is, adapted to FW ), we attach a local piece of the eigenvariety
which consists of the following:
(a) An L-aﬃnoid varietyEW,ν, called the local piece.
(b) A ﬁnite morphism κ :EW,ν→W , called the weight map .
(c) A morphism of rings ψ :H→O (EW,ν).
The construction is as follows: since ν is adapted, we can deﬁnite the ﬁnite ﬂat
R-submodule M≤ν
W . Since H is commutative, it stabilizes M≤ν
W by Prop. II.4.2.
We deﬁneTW,ν to be the eigenalgebra (cf. §I.2) of H acting on M≤ν
W . In other
words,TW,ν is deﬁned as the R-subalgebra of End R(MW ) generated by ψW (H).
ThenTW,ν is a ﬁnite R-module, hence an aﬃnoid algebra since R is, and we deﬁne
EW,ν = SpTW,ν. The structural map deﬁnes a ﬁnite morphism of rigid aﬃnoid
varietyκ :EW,ν = SpTW,ν→ W = SpR. And we have by construction a natural
ring homomorphism ψ :H→T W,ν =O(EW,ν).
Lemma II.6.1. LetW′⊂W be an inclusion of admissible open aﬃnoids of W,
with W,W′∈ C. Let ν′≤ ν, and assume that (W,ν ) and (W′,ν′) are adapted.
Then there exists a unique open immersion EW′,ν′ ↪→ EW,ν compatible with the
weight maps κ and the maps ψ fromH. Its image is the aﬃnoid open subset of
EW,ν deﬁned by the conditions vp(Up(z))≤ν′, κ(z)∈W′.
Proof — Note that since ν is adapted to W , it is also adapted to W′. By
considering the intermediate (W′,ν ), we can reduce to the two cases whereW =W′
and where ν =ν′.
In the case W = W′ = SpR, for simplicity of notation write T =TW,ν and
T′ =TW,ν′ for the Hecke algebras attached to M≤ν and M≤ν′
respectively. Write
F = PG , resp. F = P′G′ the decomposition given by Prop. II.3.9 for ν and ν′
respectively. Since N(Gx,ν ) = 0 for all x ∈ W , N(Gx,ν′) = 0 as well hence

N(Px,ν′) = N(P′

Prop. II.3.9 and Prop. II.4.2 M≤ν = M′

which shows thatEW′,ν is the open aﬃnoid ofEW,ν deﬁned by the condition κ(z)∈
W′.
Finally, in each case the isomorphism we have constructed is unique since the
eigenalgebras are generated by ψ(H) over R. □
The eigenvariety is then constructed by gluing the local pieces:
Definition II.6.2. An eigenvariety for the above eigenvariety data, consists of
(a) A rigid analytic variety E/L (the eigenvariety proper).
(b) A locally ﬁnite map κ :E→W (the weight map).
(c) A morphism of rings ψ :H→O (E), that sends Up to an invertible func-
tion.
such that for any aﬃnoid subdomain W ofW in C, and real ν adapted to W , the
open subvarietyE(W,ν ) of κ−1(W )⊂E deﬁned by vp(ψ(Up))≤ ν, is isomorphic
(as analytic variety overW with a map ψ formH to their ring of functions) to the
local pieceEW,ν constructed above, and such that the E(W,ν ) form an admissible
covering ofE.
Note that the isomorphisms E(W,ν )≃E W,ν whose existences are required by
the deﬁnition are necessarily unique.
===== PDF PAGE 85 =====
II.6. THE EIGENVARIETY MACHINE 85
Theorem II.6.3. For any given eigenvariety data, an eigenvariety exists and
is unique up to unique isomorphism.
Proof — We ﬁrst prove the existence, by gluing, in two steps. First let W be an
open admissible aﬃnoid of W, in C. We will construct an ’eigenvariety’ EW with
the same data and properties as E but withW always replaced by W .
We consider the family of aﬃnoid varieties EX,ν for X⊂ W an open aﬃnoid
subdomain, andν a real number adapted toX. We deﬁne a gluing data on this fam-
ily as follows: given two EX,ν andEX′,ν′ then by Lemma II.6.1 (X∩X′, min(ν,ν′))
is adapted, andEX∩X′,min(ν,ν′) can be seen as an open aﬃnoid subset of both EX,ν
andEX′,ν′, in a unique way respecting the maps form H, and the weight maps.
Hence we have deﬁned two open aﬃnoids, one of EX,ν and one of EX′,ν′, and an
isomorphism between them. It is clear by uniqueness that those isomorphisms sat-
isfy the cocycle condition. Thus, by [ 21, Prop. 9.3.2/1] we can glue the EX,ν’s to
form a rigid analytic varietyEW , of which theEX,ν’s are an admissible covering by
admissible open aﬃnoid subdomains. Then the maps κ :EX,ν → X glue into a
single map κ :EW→ W by [21, Prop. 9.3.1/1]. Also the maps ψ :H→O (EX,ν)
glue into a map ψ :H→O (EW ) because the EX,ν’s form an admissible covering.
The uniqueness of EW up to unique isomorphism (compatible with κ and ψ) is
obvious.
Finally, to constructE we have to glue the EW ’s. The only minor diﬃculty is
that becauseW is not itself an aﬃnoid, the intersection of two admissible aﬃnoid
subdomains W and W′ ofW may not be aﬃnoid. But W∩W′ is an admissible
open subdomain and can be admissibly covered by aﬃnoids Wi. Therefore, we can
deﬁne an isomorphism between the open admissible subdomains κ−1(W∩W′) in
EW andE′
W by gluing (using again [ 21, Prop. 9.3.1/1]) the isomorphism between
theκ−1(Wi) inEW andEW′ obtained by identifying the twoκ−1(Wi)’s considered as
a subdomain ofEW orEW′ obtained from identifying them to the eigenvarietyEWi.
Since by uniqueness those isomorphisms obviously satisfy the gluing condition, [21,
Prop 9.2.1/1] and [ 21, Prop 9.3.1/1] gives us ﬁnally the eigenvariety E by gluing
theEW , with a map κ :E→W gluing the maps κ :E→ W and a morphism of
rings ψ :H→O (E) gluing the morphisms ψ :H→O (EW ). The eigenvariety E
with those maps obviously is unique up to unique isomorphism and satisﬁes all the
properties stated in the theorem. □

II.7. Properties of eigenvarieties
The most important property, which is the reason eigenvarieties deserve their
name, is the following:
Theorem II.7.1. Let w be a point of W, of ﬁeld of deﬁnition L(w). For any
ﬁnite extension L′ of L(w), the set of L′-points z ofE such that κ(z) = w is in
natural bijection with the systems of eigenvalues of H appearing in Mw⊗L(w)L′
such that the eigenvalue of Up is non-zero. The bijection attaches to z the system
of eigenvalues ψz :H→ L′, T↦→ψ(T )(z) for T∈H .
Note that sinceMw is well-deﬁned up to a link, the set of systems of eigenvalues
ofH appearing in Mw⊗L(w)L′ such that the eigenvalues of Up is non-zero is well-
deﬁned.
Proof — Obviously it is enough to prove that for all ν∈ R, z↦→ψz is a bijection
between the set of L′-points of E with κ(z) = w and vp(Up(z))≤ ν and systems
of eigenvalues of H appearing in Mw⊗L(w) L′ such that the eigenvalues of Up
have valuation ≤ ν. Choose an aﬃnoid admissible neighborhood W = Sp R of
w∈W such that ν is adapted to W (Prop. II.3.11). It is enough to prove the
last assertion withE replaced by its local piece EW,ν, since on the one hand, every
L′-point ofW satisfying the desired condition is in EW,ν, and on the other hand,
(Mw ˆ⊗L(w)L′)≤ν = (M≤ν
W )⊗R L′ (the implied map being R → L(w) ↪→ L) by
Prop. II.4.2. Now by Lemma I.4.1, since L′ is a ﬁeld, the L′-points ofEW,ν above
w are the same as the L′-points of the eigenalgebra ofH acting on Mw. The result
thus follows from Corollary I.5.10. □

II.7. PROPERTIES OF EIGENVARIETIES 87
We say that a compact operator Up on a (Pr) Banach space M over a ﬁnite
extensionL of Qp is quasi-nilpotent if for everya in a ﬁnite extensionL′ ofL, 1−au
is invertible on M⊗LL′. By Corollary II.3.7 and Prop. II.2.17, this is equivalent
to the condition det(1−TUp) = 1.
Corollary II.7.3. The image of κ :E→W consists of all the points w such
that the action of Up on Mw is not quasi-nilpotent. It is a Zariski-open subset of
W.
Proof — A point w is in the image of κ if and only if there is a system of H-
eigenvalues (over an algebraic closure ¯L of L(w)) of ﬁnite slope in Mw. If there is
such a system, there is a non-zero eigenvector for Up in Mw⊗ ¯L with a non-zero
eigenvalue, and Up is not quasi-nilpotent. Conversely, if Up is not quasi-nilpotent,
it has by Riesz’s theory a non-zero eigenvalue in ¯L, say of valuation ν, and there is
anH-eigenvector in M≤ν
w ⊗ ¯L.
To prove the second assertion, for allW = SpR∈ C, write 1 +∑∞
i=1ai,WTi the
Fredholm’s power series ofUp onMW . By (EC2), the ai,W glues to deﬁne functions
ai∈O (W ). For w∈W , the Fredholm’s series of Up on Mw is 1 +∑∞
i=1ai(w)Ti.
Hence by the above,w is in the image of κ if and only if at least one of the ai(w) is
non-zero. The image of κ is thus Zariski-open, as the complement of the analytic
subspace deﬁned by the ai’s □
We shall give below (Prop. II.7.7), under some mild hypothesis on W, a result
precising the second assertion of that corollary.
To state the next property, we shall need to recall some facts about the delicate
notion of irreducible component of a rigid analytic space X. When X = Sp R
is aﬃnoid, one deﬁnes naturally its rigid analytic irreducible components as the
closed subspaces Xi = SpR/pi of X, where the pi are the (ﬁnitely many, since R
is noetherian) minimal prime ideals of R. In general, irreducible components have
been deﬁned by Conrad (cf. [ 39]), in a rather indirect way : for X a rigid analytic
spaces one deﬁnes an irreducible component ofX as the image inX of a connected
component of the normalization of X. One says that X is irreducible if it has
only one irreducible component.
Lemma II.7.4. (i) The spaceX is the union of its irreducible components
Xi, which may be inﬁnitely many.
(ii) A subset Z of X is Zariski-dense if and only if Z∩Xi is Zariski-dense in
Xi for every i.
(iii) Every irreducible component Xi of X is irreducible.
(iv) If X is irreducible, it has a dimension, which is the common dimension of
all rings of functions R of its admissible open aﬃnoids U = SpR.
===== PDF PAGE 88 =====
88 II. EIGENVARIETIES
(v) The intersection Xi∩ U of an irreducible component Xi with an open
admissible aﬃnoid U of X is either empty or a union of irreducible com-
ponents of U.
(vi) If X is irreducible, any non-empty admissible open aﬃnoid U is Zariski-
dense in X.
(vii) If f : X→ X′ is a ﬁnite map, the image of an irreducible component of
X by f is an irreducible closed analytic subspace of X′.
Proof — For (v), see [ 39, 2.2.9]. For (vii) see [ 39, 2.2.3]. The other properties
follow easily from the deﬁnition. □
One says that a rigid analytic space X is equidimensional of dimension n if all
its irreducible components have dimension n. By (iv) and (v), this is equivalent
to saying that for all admissible open aﬃnoids U = Sp R, R is equidimensional
of dimension n in the algebraic sense, that is to say all the domains R/pi for pi
minimal prime ideals have Krull dimension n.
Proposition II.7.5. Assume thatW is equidimensional of dimension n. Then
so isE.
Proof — Since being equidimensional of dimension n is a local property as we
just saw, this results from Prop. I.3.3. □
Proposition II.7.6. IfW is reduced and irreducible, the local rings of E have
no associated primes.
Proof — If z∈E , x = κ(z)∈W and W = SpR∈C an aﬃnoid containing x,
then the local ring of E at z is by construction a localization of the eigenalgebra
associated of the R-module MW . Since R is a domain by assumption, the result
then follows from Prop. I.3.4. □
The next proposition proves the few properties of eigenvarieties for which we
need the full force of Theorem II.3.13.
Proposition II.7.7. (i) The map κ×ψ(Up)−1 :E→W× A1
rig is ﬁnite.
(ii) IfW is separated, the eigenvariety E is separated.
(iii) Assume thatW is equidimensional, and that a subset of W is Zariski-open
inW if and only if its intersection with every W ∈ C is Zariski-open in
W . If D is an irreducible component of E, then κ(D) is Zariski-open in
W.
===== PDF PAGE 89 =====

Proof — For (i) it is enough to prove that for all aﬃnoid subdomainsW = SpR⊂

Assertion (ii) follows from (i) since a ﬁnite map is separated and W× A1

trivially κ(DW ). □
Remark II.7.8. The condition in (c) of the above proposition is satisﬁed for

II.8. A comparison theorem for eigenvarieties
In this section we present two very useful theorems of Chenevier: one gives
a suﬃcient condition for an eigenvariety to be reduced, and the other allows to
compare two eigenvarieties. Both rely on the notion of classical structure.
II.8.1. Classical structures.
Definition II.8.1. LetW be a reduced rigid analytic space over Qp, and X⊂
W. We say that X is very Zariski-dense inW if for every x∈X there is a basis of
open aﬃnoid neighborhoods V of x in X such that X∩V is Zariski-dense in V .
Exercise II.8.2. LetW = A1
rig be the rigid aﬃne line. Show that any set of the form
a +bN with a,b∈ Z, b̸= 0 is very Zariski-dense.
Exercise II.8.3. Give an example of a Zariski-dense subset X of A1
rig which is not
very Zariski-dense.
Exercise II.8.4. LetW be the rigid aﬃne plane ( A1
rig)2. Find a set X⊂W which is
Zariski-dense but such that for all aﬃnoid open subsets V ofW,X∩V is not Zariski-dense
in V .
Now let us ﬁx an eigenvariety data as in §II.6.1, that is (ED1) a ring H with a
distinguished Up∈H , (ED2) a reduced rigid space W with an admissible covering
C, and (ED3) Banach modulesMW with an action ofH for everyW∈ C, satisfying
conditions (EC1) and (EC2).

Proposition II.8.6. Classical points are very Zariski dense in E.
Proof — First we prove that this set is Zariski-dense. It is enough to show (by
Lemma II.7.4) that classical points are Zariski-dense in any irreducible component
===== PDF PAGE 91 =====
II.8. A COMPARISON THEOREM FOR EIGENVARIETIES 91
D ofE. By Prop. II.7.7(iii), κ(D) is Zariski open in W, hence there is an x∈ X
that belongs to κ(D). Let us ﬁx z∈ D such that κ(z) = x and ν∈ R such that
ν > vp(Up(z)). By (CSC) and Prop. II.3.11 there is an aﬃnoid W = Sp R in
C containing x such that (W,ν ) is adapted, and Xν∩W is Zariski dense in W .
One has z∈E W,ν and by deﬁnition of Zν every point in EW,ν that lies above a
point of Xν∩W is classical. It follows, by the following lemma applied to the
map κ : SpecTW,ν→ SpecR (where W = SpR andEW,ν = SpTW,ν), that classical
points are dense in EW,≤ν, hence in EW,ν∩D because it is a union of irreducible
components of EW,ν (Lemma II.7.4). Since EW,ν∩D is a non-empty aﬃnoid (it
containsz) of the irreducible space D, it is Zariski-dense in D (Lemma II.7.4), and
this completes the proof that Z is Zariski-dense.
Actually this also shows that classical points are very Zariski-dense, because
for z a classical point of weight x, the above argument provides an aﬃnoid neigh-
borhood of z on which very classical points are Zariski-dense. □
Lemma II.8.7. Letf : X→Y be a ﬁnite ﬂat morphism of noetherian schemes,
and Z⊂Y a Zariski-dense subset. Then f−1(Z) is Zariski-dense in X.
Proof — Since f is ﬁnite ﬂat, and the schemes noetherian, f maps irreducible
components Xi of X surjectively to irreducible components Yj(i) of Y , and the
generic point of X(i) is the only point of X(i) that maps to the generic point of
Yj(i). In particular, one may assume that X and Y are irreducible. Let T be the
Zariski-closure off−1(Z). Since f is closed,f(T ) is closed inY , but it also contains
Z which is dense, sof(T ) =Y . Hence there is a point in T that maps to the generic
point of Y , and this point necessarily is the generic point of X. Since T is closed,
T =X. □
II.8.2. A reducedness criterion.
Theorem II.8.8. Consider an eigenvariety data with a classical structure as
above. Assume that the local rings of W have no embedded primes. If for every
x∈X, M cl
x is semi-simple as an H-module, then the eigenvariety E is reduced.
We show that ifz is a classical point ofE, there is an open aﬃnoid neighborhood
of z inE which is reduced. Set x = κ(z)∈ X and choose a real number ν >
vp(Up(z)). By (CSC) and Prop. II.3.11 there is an aﬃnoidW = SpR in C containing
x such that (W,ν ) is adapted, and Xν∩W is Zariski dense in W . For x∈Xν∩W ,
by deﬁnition of Xν, M≤ν

x , hence is a semi-simple H-module. Since TW,ν
is the eigenalgebra of H acting on the module MW , Prop. I.9.1 shows that TW,ν is
a reduced algebra, hence that EW,ν is reduced.

Lemma II.8.9. If Z is a rigid analytic space over Qp whose local rings at every
point z have no embedded primes, and are reduced for z in a Zariski dense subset
of Z. Then Z is reduced.
For the proof, see [ 28, Lemma 3.11].

II.9. Notes and references
The results of this chapter are due to Serre, Lazard, Coleman, Mazur, Buzzard
and Chenevier. Only the presentation and some generalizations or variants are due
to the author of this book.
The largest part of the material presented here is due to R. Coleman. He was
the ﬁrst, in [ 31] to construct families of ﬁnite slope modular forms and from this
families of eigenforms or local pieces of the eigencurve, providing a generalization
of the 10-year older work of Hida on ordinary family of modular forms that had
been conjectured in the meantime by Mazur and Gouvˆ ea. To this aim, Coleman
had to develop a Riesz’s theory for compact operators over certain type of aﬃnoid
rings, which he did, extending much earlier works of Lazard and Serre over a base
ﬁeld. Results of Coleman were soon after globalized by himself and Mazur, who
deﬁned and constructed the Eigencurve in [ 34]. The method to construct the
eigencurve from Coleman’s modules of families of ﬁnite slope modular forms was
later generalized and axiomatized by Buzzard in [ 23]. At about the same time,
Chenevier used it in [26] to construct eigenvarieties for inner forms of GLn that are
compact at inﬁnity.
More precisely, the material in section II.1 mostly follows [ 23,§2] which itself
follows closely [ 31], but we take a somewhat shorter route, which allows us to
avoid any noetherian hypothesis. The material of II.2.1, II.2.2, II.3.1, II.3.2 is a
generalization to the case where the base is a Banach algebra of results of Lazard
[70] in the case of a base ﬁeld. The proofs here in general follow Lazard’s proofs,
with the necessary adaptations. Subsection II.2.4 follows very closely Coleman [ 31]
and Subsection II.2.5 is a simple generalization of [ 93]. Subsection II.3.3 which
deﬁnes the notion of adapted pair prepares for a variant of Buzzard’s construction
of the eigenvariety, where the local pieces are “cylinders” EW,ν easier to deal with
in the applications than the more general local pieces used by Buzzard. The proof
of Theorem II.3.13 is inspired by that of [ 23, Theorem 4.6]. Sections II.4, II.5
and II.6 presents that construction which parallels that of Buzzard. The material
of section II.7 is due to Chenevier ([ 26]) who generalizes in part Coleman-Mazur
([34]) and that of section II.8 is entirely due to Chenevier ([ 28]).

Part 2
Modular Symbols and L-functions
===== PDF PAGE 98 =====

===== PDF PAGE 99 =====
CHAPTER III
Abstract modular symbols
In this chapter, we introduce the fundamental notion of modular symbols with
values in a system of coeﬃcients V , an abelian group with an action of a sub-
monoid of GL2(Q). Modular symbols will be used throughout the rest of this book,
with increasingly sophisticated systems of coeﬃcients V – ﬁrst in the next chapter,
Chapter IV, with V being a symmetric power of the standard representation of
GL2, and then in the subsequent chapters, with V being various spaces of mod-
ules of distributions. Modular symbols with this kind of coeﬃcients have strong
connections respectively with classical modular forms, and with families of p-adic
modular forms and their p-adic L-functions.
This chapter will discuss the general properties enjoyed by modular symbols
with all kind of systems of coeﬃcients. We study their deﬁnition, their behavior
by base change, the action of Hecke operators they enjoy, their connection with
algebraic topology, and fundamental for the sequel, the natural pairing between
spaces of modular symbols.
III.1. The notion of modular symbols

be the subgroup of divisors of degree 0, that is such that ∑

Let Σ be a submonoid of GL 2(Q) and let V a right Σ-module, i.e. an abelian

Definition III.1.4. If Γ is a subgroup of Σ, we deﬁne the group of V -valued
modular symbols for Γ as the subgroup of Hom(∆ 0,V ) ﬁxed by the action of Γ:
SymbΓ(V ) = Hom(∆0,V )Γ.
Let R be a commutative ring. Observe that if V is an R-module with a right

structure of R-module. The construction V ↦→ SymbΓ(V ) is functorial, from the
categories of R-modules with right Σ-actions to the category of R-modules.
Lemma III.1.5. The functor V ↦→ SymbΓ(V ) is left-exact and commute with

is a natural isomorphism SymbΓ(V )⊗RR′≃ SymbΓ(V⊗RR′) when R′ is R-ﬂat).
Proof — The left-exactness is clear since both V ↦→ Hom(∆0,V ) and the func-

→V (J)), where µ∗

III.2. Action of the Hecke operators on modular symbols
We suppose given a submonoid Σ of GL 2(Q), a subgroup Γ of Σ, and a right
Σ-module V . We assume that Γ and Σ satisfy hypothesis (I.6.3), namely
∀s∈ Σ, ΓsΓ is the union of ﬁnitely many left Γ-cosets Γsi.
This condition holds, for any Σ, when Γ is a ﬁnite index subgroup of SL 2(Z) or any
conjugate of such a subgroup: cf. [ 98,§3].
Since SymbΓ(V ) is by deﬁnition the group of Γ-invariants in the module Hom(∆0,V )
on which Σ acts, there is an action (on the right) of the Hecke ring H(Σ, Γ), see
§I.6.2. The construction V ↦→ SymbΓ(V ) is a functor form the category of right
Σ-modules to the category of right-H(Σ, Γ)-modules. When V is anR-module, and
the action of Σ is by R-linear operators, then obviously the Hecke operators are
R-linear on SymbΓ(V ).

III.3. Reminder on cohomology with local coeﬃcients
This section is a reminder on the homology and cohomology of a topological
space with coeﬃcients in a local system , or for short, with local coeﬃcients . We
suppose the reader familiar with singular homology and cohomology with constant
===== PDF PAGE 102 =====
102 III. ABSTRACT MODULAR SYMBOLS
coeﬃcients, as exposed in classical textbooks such as [ 56, Chapters 2 and 3]. Ho-
mology and cohomology with local coeﬃcients were invented by Steenrod in [ 103].
For a more modern treatment, see [ 101]. For the convenience of the reader, our
treatment is self-contained, containing all needed deﬁnitions and proofs, except for
the proof of Poincar´ e’s duality.
III.3.1. Local systems. We ﬁx a commutative ring R. Let M be an R-
module. If X is a topological space, the constant sheaf MX attached to M is
deﬁned as the sheaf in R-modules attached to the presheaf which sends any open
subset U⊂X to M. A local system (or locally constant sheaf) of R-modules on a
space X is a sheaf in R-modules G on X such that there exists an R-module M
and a covering of X by open sets U such that G|U≃MU as sheaves of R-modules
overU. The R-module M is then isomorphic to the stalk at any point of the local
system G, hence is uniquely determined by G up to isomorphism. We call M the
ﬁber of G.
Iff :Y →X is a continuous map, andG a local system onX, then the pull-back
f∗G is a local system on Y , with the same ﬁber. We denote by G(f) the module
of global sections on Y of f∗G. In other words, G(f) = lim←−U open, f(Y )⊂U⊂XG(U).
The restriction mapsG(X)→G(U) forU an open set ofX containingf(Y ) induces
a map f∗ :G(X)→G(f), called the pullback by f. More generally, if h :Z→Y is
a continuous map, the map h∗ :G(f) = (f∗G)(Y )→G(h◦f) = (f∗G)(h) is called
pullback by h.
Exercise III.3.1. Let G be a local system of ﬁber M on a topological space X, f :
Y →X a continuous map. Show that ifY is connected,G(f) is isomorphic to a sub-module
of M.
III.3.2. Local systems and representations of the fundamental group.
We now assume thatX is connected, locally path-connected, and semi-locally sim-
ply connected, which are the standard conditions under which X is known to have
a universal cover π : ˜X→X. We choose a point x∈X.
IfM is anR-module with a right action ofπ1(X,x ), we can attach toM a local
system ˜M ofX, of ﬁberM, as follows. We consider the product space ˜X×M where
M is given the discrete topology, and let π1(X,x ) acts on it by g(x,m ) = (gx,gm ).
The quotient space XM := ( ˜X×M)/π1(X,x ) inherits from π a continuous map
πM :XM→X and we deﬁne a sheaf ˜M overX as the sheaf of continuous sections
of πM, that is ˜M(U) ={s :U→XM, s continuous, πM◦s = IdU}.
It is clear that the construction M→ ˜M deﬁnes a functor from the category
of R-modules with R-linear action of π1(X,x ) to the category of locally constant
sheaves of R-modules on X.

M→ ˜M, showing that this functor is an equivalence of category.

1.– If γ is a path in X from x to y, explain how to deﬁne using γ an isomorphism

If G is a locally constant sheaf of R-modules on X, we write G∨ for the
sheaf Hom (G,RX). In other words, G∨ is the sheaf on X such that G∨(U) =
Hom(G|U,RU), the Hom here being the R-module of morphisms of sheaves of R-
modules over U. It is clear that G∨ is again a locally free sheaf of R-modules, and
that its ﬁber is M∨ := HomR(M,R ). When X is connected, and M is a ﬁnite
projective R-module, the natural map m : G(X)⊗G∨(X)→ R deﬁnes a perfect
bilinear pairing between G(X) =M and G∨(X) =M∨.
III.3.3. Singular simplices. We denote as usual by ∆q forq≥ 0 the standard
q-simplex, with q + 1 vertices numbered 0, 1,...,q .
Definition III.3.4. If X is a topological space, a singular q-simplex of X is a
continuous map σ : ∆q→X.

of ∆q numbered in S, in increasing order. If S ={0,...,q }−{ i}, we write φi

q.

III.3.6. Relative homology and relative cohomology. We shall also need,
occasionally, the relative cohomology of a pair ( X,Y ) where X is a topological
space and Y a subspace of X. We simply deﬁne Cq(X,Y,G ) as the quotient
Cq(X,G )/Cq(Y,G ), andCq(X,Y,G ) as the kernel of the restriction mapCq(X,G )→
Cq(Y,G ). The relative homologyH•(X,Y,G ) (resp. relative cohomologyH•(X,Y,G ))
is deﬁned as the homology (resp. cohomology) of the complex C•(X,Y,G ) (resp.
C•(X,Y,G )). From the short exact sequence of complexes 0 → C•(X,Y,G )→
C•(X,G )→C•(Y,G )→ 0 one gets a long exact sequence
(III.3.1) ···→ Hq(X,Y,G )→Hq(X,G )→Hq(Y,G )→Hq+1(X,Y,G )→...
Let us also note the easy excision property: if U is a subspace of X whose closure
lies in the interior of Y ,
(III.3.2) Hq(X,Y,G ) =Hq(X−U,Y −U,G ).

The maps Cq(X,G∨)→ HomR(Cq(X,G ),R ) induce natural morphisms of R-

Indeed, an element of Hq(X,G∨) is the class [z] of an element z∈Cq(X,G∨) such

and the condition dz = 0 is equivalent to ˜zδ = 0, i.e. ˜z is trivial on the submodules

the map Hq(X,G∨)→ Hom(Hq(X,G ),R ) is the morphism of R-modules (III.3.3).
Theorem III.3.7 (Universal coeﬃcient theorem). Assume thatR is a PID, that
G is a locally constant sheaf of R-modules whose ﬁber is a ﬁnite free R-module,
and that the modules Hq(X,G ) are ﬁnitely generated for q≥ 0. Then the mor-
phism (III.3.3) ﬁts into an exact sequence
Ext1
R(Hq−1(X,G ),R )→Hq(X,G∨)→ HomR(Hq(X,G ),R )→ 0
Proof — The proof is standard. The reader may consult [ 56, pages 191-195] for
a very detailed proof in the special case where G is the constant sheaf R. The case
of general locally constant G needs only very simple adaptations. For instance,
to prove the surjectivity of (III.3.3), one observes that the module Cq−1(X,G )
is a direct sum of modules of the form G(σ) (for σ a singular simplex), which by
Exercise III.3.1 are submodules of the ﬁber of G, hence are freeR-modules (sinceR
is a PID). ThusCq−1(X,G ) is free and, sinceR is a PID, its submoduleBq−1(X,G )
is free. Therefore the exact sequence 0 →Zq(X,G )→Cq(X,G ) ∂→Bq−1(X,G )→
0 is split. This shows that any map in Hom( Zq(X,G ),R ) extends to a map in
Hom(Cq(X,G ),R ), which is precisely the surjectivity of (III.3.3). We leave the
adaptation of the proof of the exactness at Hq(X,G ) to the reader. □
Corollary III.3.8. Under the hypothesis of Theorem III.3.7, we have an iso-

isomorphism Hq(X,G∨) ≃→ HomR(Hq(X,G ),R )
Proof — SinceR is a PID, andHq−1(X,G ) is ﬁnitely generated, it is a sum of mod-

threefore is torsion. Hence the kernel of surjective mapHq(X,G )→ HomR(Hq(X,G ),R )
is torsion, and because HomR(Hq(X,G ),R ) is torsion-free, this kernel is the entire
torsion of Hq(X,G ). □

III.3.10. Poincar´ e duality.Let X be a topological real manifold of dimen-
sion n. We recall that the orientation sheaf Zw on X is the sheaf U↦→Hn(X,X−
U, Z) (relative homology). This sheaf is locally constant of ﬁber Z and attached,
when X is connected, to a morphism π1(X,x )→ Aut(Z) ={±1}. An orientation
of X, if it exists, is a global section of ZX on X, or equivalently, an isomorphism
of sheaves Zw→ ZX over X. If R is a commutative ring, we call Rw the sheaf
Zw⊗ZR which is a locally free sheaf of R-modules.
The most general form of Poincar´ e duality for homology and cohomology of
local systems is the following theorem. For a proof, see [ 101, Theorem 10.3].
Theorem III.3.11. LetX be a connected manifold of dimension n.
(i) The homology group Hn(X,Rw) is canonically isomorphic to R.
We denote by [X] the element corresponding to 1 in Hn(X, Zw), and by [X]R its
image in Hn(X,Rw) for any ring R.
(ii) For any integer q such that 0≤ q≤ n and any locally constant sheaf of
R-modulesG on X, the cap-product with [X]R deﬁnes an isomorphism:
Hq
c (X,G ) ≃−→Hn−q(X,G⊗Rw).
We deduce
