III.3.11. Singular cohomology and sheaf cohomology. This subsection
supposes that the reader has some familiarity with the cohomology of sheaves as
deﬁned for example in [ 55]. Let us just recall that if X is a topological space, and
ΓX is the left-exact functor global sections from the category of sheaves of abelian
groups on X to the category of abelian groups, the sheaf cohomology groups of a
sheafF are deﬁned as RnΓX(F) where RnΓX, n≥ 0 are the derived functors of
ΓX.
Theorem III.3.13. LetX be a locally contractible and hereditary paracompact1
topological space, G a local system on X. Then the singular cohomology groups
Hn(X,G ) are canonically isomorphic to the sheaf cohomology groups RnΓX(F).
While this theorem was certainly known since the beginning of sheaf cohomol-
ogy theory, I don’t know a complete reference for this: [ 86] contains a proof in the
case whereG is a constant sheaf. We recall his proof (as presented by [92]) to show
it works for general local systems G.
We call ˜Cn(−,G ) the sheaﬁcation of the presheaf U ↦→ Cn(U,G ). It is easy
to see, since X is locally contractible, that the complex of sheaves ˜C•(−,G ) is a
resolution of G.
Lemma III.3.14. The sheaves ˜Cn(−,G ) are ﬂabby, and the morphism of com-
plex of abelian groups C•(X,G )→ ˜C•(X,G ) is a quasi-isomorphism.
It is clear that this lemma implies the theorem, for it implies that R•ΓX(F)
is the cohomology of the complex ˜C•(X,G ), hence also of the complex C•(X,G ),
that is is H•(X,G ).
To prove the lemma, we introduce, for every open subset U of X, and every
coveringU of U by open subsets of U, the group Cn
U(U,G ) of Cn(U,G ) as the
group of functions u assigning to every singular n-simplex σ : ∆ n → U of U
whose image falls in one of the open sets of the covering U an element u(σ)∈
G(σ). It is obvious that the restriction map Cn(U,G )→Cn
U(U,G ) is surjective and
it follows from a standard barycentric subdivision argument that the morphism
1This means that every open subset of X is paracompact.

U(U,G ) is a quasi-isomorphism. Moreover, since U
is paracompact, ˜Cn(U,G ) = lim−→UCn
U(U,G ) (cf. [ 92, Prop 0.2]). It follows that
the morphism C•(U,G )→ ˜C•(U,G ) is a quasi-isomorphism, and that the maps
Cn(U,G )→ ˜Cn(U,G ) are surjective. Since this also applies to U = X, and since
the restriction mapsCn(X,G )→Cn(U,G ) are clearly surjective, it follows that the
maps ˜Cn(X,G )→ ˜Cn(U,G ) are surjective; in other words, the sheaves ˜Cn(−,G )
are ﬂabby. This completes the proof of the lemma, and of the theorem.
We recall (cf. [ 53, Th´ eor` eme 2.9.1, page 138]) that forF any sheaf of abelian
groups on X, and Y ⊂X any locally closed subset with i :Y ↪→X the inclusion,
there exists a unique sheaf FY such that (FY )|Y =i−1F and (FY )|X−Y = 0. The
functorF↦→F Y is exact. One has (FY ) =i!i−1F and when Y is closed in X, this
becomesFY =i∗i−1F and one has an exact sequence 0 →F Y →F→F X−Y → 0
(cf. [ 53, Th´ eor` eme 2.9.3, page 140])
Theorem III.3.15. LetX be a locally contractible and hereditary paracompact
topological space,G a local system on X. Let Z⊂ X be a closed subset, i : Z ↪→
X the inclusion. Then the relative singular cohomology groups Hn(X,Z,G ) are
canonically isomorphic to the sheaf cohomology groups RnΓX(GX−Z).
Proof — Consider the exact sequence 0→ ˜Cn(−,G )X−Z→ ˜Cn(−,G )→ ˜Cn(−,G )Z→
0. We note that Cn(−,G )Z is ﬂabby by [ 53, Th´ eor` eme 3.1.2 and Corollaire 1
of Th´ eor` eme 3.3.1, page 151]. Thus, we have an exact sequence of complexes
0→ ˜C•(−,G )X−Z→ ˜C•(−,G )→ ˜C•(−,G )Z→ 0, whose middle term and right
term are ﬂasque resolution of G and GZ, while left term is a resolution of GX−Z.
It follows that the groups RnΓX(GX−Z) can be computed as the cohomology of
the bicomplex whose ﬁrst row is ˜C•(X,G ) and second row ˜C•(Z.G) (all other rows
being 0), and this cohomology is easily seen to be the one of Cn(X,Z,G ). □

In this section, we ﬁx a submonoid Σ of GL 2(Q), a right Σ-module V , and a
subgroup Γ of Σ, which satisﬁes (I.6.3).

We set δ( ¯H) = P1(Q) (the set of cusps) and we endow ¯H :=H∪ δ( ¯H) with

topology. In particular, ¯H is not compact (since δ( ¯H) = P1(Q) would be discrete
and compact, hence ﬁnite.)
We deﬁne a right action of Σ on the cohomology groups Hi(H,V ), Hi( ¯H,V ),

Hq( ¯H,δ ( ¯H),V ) induced by the mapv↦→v|σ onV , and a mapσ∗ :Hq( ¯H,δ ( ¯H),V )→

action of σ on c∈Hq( ¯H,δ ( ¯H),V ) is c|σ =σ∗σ∗c.

Theorem III.4.1. (Ash-Stevens) There is a canonical isomorphism of right

Hom(∆0,V ) =H1( ¯H,δ ( ¯H),V ¯H).

δ( ¯H) not empty, and the next term isH1( ¯H,VH) which is 0 since ¯H is contractible.
Note that the morphisms of this sequence are compatible with the natural right
Σ-actions on the terms.

0→V → Hom(∆,V )→ Hom(∆0,V )→ 0.(III.4.2)

III.4.3. Modular symbols and cohomology with compact support. In
this subsection, we keep the notation of the preceding subsection, but we assume
in addition that the subgroup Γ and the module V satisfy those three conditions:
(III.4.3)
Γ is discrete, and for every z∈H , the closure of the orbit Γ z in ¯H containsδ( ¯H).
(III.4.4) The only torsion elements in Γ are Id and possibly −Id.
(III.4.5) If −Id∈ Γ, then−Id acts trivially on V .
We deﬁne ¯Γ as Γ/Γ∩{Id,±Id}. Condition (III.4.3) means that ¯Γ is a fuchsian group
of the ﬁrst kind, and condition (III.4.4) means that ¯Γ has no elliptic point, hence
acts freely on H. Thus the the quotient H/¯Γ has a natural structure of Riemann
surface, which we call YΓ. As is well known ([ 98]),YΓ is the complement of a ﬁnite
set of points, δ( ¯H)/¯Γ (the set of cusps of YΓ) in a unique compact Riemann surface
XΓ which is homeomorphic to ¯H/¯Γ.
By condition III.4.5, the action of Γ onV factors through ¯Γ, and one can attach
to the ¯Γ-module V a local system ˜V on YΓ (cf.§III.3.1).

Remark III.4.10. All the results of §III.4.3 and of III.4.4 apply when Γ is a
congruence subgroup of SL 2(Z) that does not contain −Id, for any Γ-module V ,
since such a subgroup satisﬁes conditions (III.4.3), (III.4.4), as well as (III.4.5) for
anyV . They also apply when Γ is any congruence subgroup (even if it contains−Id)
provided that the multiplication by 6 is invertible in V . Indeed, we can introduce
the normal subgroup Γ′ = Γ∩ Γ(3) of Γ, whose index is divisible only by the prime
numbers 2 and 3. Since −Id̸∈ Γ(3), the results proved here apply to Γ ′ and easy
and standard arguments allow to deduce that they also hod for Γ provided that in
the modules V considered, the multiplication by 6 is invertible.
III.5. Notes and references
The results in this chapter are due to Eichler, Shimura [ 96], [97], [98], Ash-
Stevens [2] and Hida [ 57]. We have tried to provide a uniﬁed presentation, using
only basic algebraic topology, and avoid detours sometimes taken in the literature
(for example, using the fact that the topological spaces YΓ have a structure of
algebraic varieties over C, the comparison theorem between usual and ´ etale coho-
mologies, and the deep theorems of SGA 4 to prove things about modular symbols.)

CHAPTER IV
Classical modular symbols, modular forms, L-functions
Concerning spaces of modular forms and Hecke operators acting on them, we
use the same notations and conventions recalled in §I.6.
IV.1. On a certain monoid and some of its modules
In this section, we introduce a certain submonoid S of GL 2(Q) containing
SL2(Z), and someS-modulesVk andPk, which will be needed to deﬁne the modules
of classical modular symbols and the action of the Hecke operators upon them.
IV.1.1. The monoid S. We consider the following monoid:
(IV.1.1) S = GL2(Q)∩M2(Z).
On this monoid we deﬁne an anti-involution
(IV.1.2) γ↦→γ′ = det(γ)γ−1.
Explicitly, γ′ =

This anti-involution allows us to see any left- S-module as a right- S-module,
and conversely : if S acts on V of the left, we deﬁne an action on the right by
lettingγ acts on V on the right through the left-action of γ′ onV , and conversely:
for v∈V,γ ∈S
(IV.1.3) γ·v =v|γ′.
Using this involution, we can also deﬁne the contragredient of a right (to ﬁx
ideas)S-moduleV (say, over a ringR): it is the right R-moduleV∨ = HomR(V,R )
provided with the following action of S: for every l∈V∨, and every γ∈S, v∈V ,
l|γ(v) =l(v|γ′).
In terms of the right action on V attached to the left-action, this formula is simply
l|γ(v) =l(γ·v).
Obviously the natural map V → (V∨)∨ is a morphism of S-modules. We note
that if we restrict the S-action to the subgroup SL 2(Z) of S, then V∨ is the usual
contragredient of the representation V .

IV.1.2. The S-modules Pk and Vk. Let k ≥ 0 be an integer. For any
commutative ring R, letPk(R) be the R-module of polynomials in one variable z

IV.2. Classical modular symbols
In this section,R will be a commutative ring in which 6 is invertible, and Γ will
be a congruence subgroup of SL 2(Z). By Remark III.4.10, all the results of last
chapter apply to Γ and any R-module V with left Γ-action.
As already observed Γ and GL 2(Q) satisﬁes condition (I.6.3), so the Hecke
algebraH(S, Γ) (and its subalgebraH(S+,V )) are deﬁned, and acts on V Γ for any
rightS-module V .

Actually we have more.
Theorem IV.3.5. The map f ↦→ β(φf), Sk+2(Γ, C) → H1
! (Γ,Vk(R)) is an
R-linear isomorphism commuting with the action of H(S+, Γ).
Since we already know that this map is injective, we only need to prove that
dimRH1

Whenk = 0,V0(R) = R. In this case, the elements of S2(Γ, C) are the holomorphic
diﬀerentials 1-forms on X(Γ) (the compactiﬁcation of Y (Γ) as a smooth Riemann
surface). The complex dimension of this space is the genus g of X(Γ), so 2g for
the real dimension. On the other hand, the morphism β is actually composition
H1
c (Y (Γ), R)→ H1(X(Γ), R)→ H1(Y (Γ), R) where the ﬁrst morphism is given
by covariant functoriality of Hi
c(−, R), and the second by the contravariant func-
toriality of Hi(−, R). So dim RH1
! (Γ, R)≤ dimRH1(X(Γ), R) = 2 g and we are
done.
The general case is proved by a similar but more complicated computation,
where we have to take the number of cups into account: [ 98, (8.2.23)].

IV.3.2. Modular forms and complex classical modular symbols. Let
us ﬁrst recall the notion of the conjugate ¯W of a C-vector space W . As an abelian
group, and even as a real vector space ¯W is equal to W , except that we denote
by ¯w∈ ¯W the element w∈ W when we want to make clear that we see it as an
element of ¯W . We deﬁne a C-scalar multiplication on ¯W by the rule λ ¯w = ¯λw.
This provides ¯W with a structure of C-vector space. Note that the construction
W ↦→ ¯W is functorial, as a C-linear map f : W → W′ is also a C-linear map
f : ¯W→ ¯W′.
Exercise IV.3.7. Suppose given a commutative ring H that acts C-linearly on W .
ThenH acts on ¯W by functoriality. Letχ be a characterH→ C. Do we have dimCW [χ] =
dimC ¯W [χ]?
Are the C-vector spaces W and ¯W isomorphic? Obviously yes, since they have
the same real dimension, hence the same complex dimension. Are they canonically
isomorphic? No, they are not. However, if we are given an R-structure WR on
W , that is a sub- R-vector space such that the natural map WR⊗R C→ W is
an isomorphism, then this R-structure deﬁnes a canonical C-linear isomorphism
c :W→ ¯W deﬁned as follows: any w∈W can be written uniquely w1 +iw2 with
w1,w 2∈WR (we write w1 = Rew and w2 = Imw in this case) and we send w to
c(w) =w1−iw2 =w1 +iw2∈ ¯W.(IV.3.2)
Exercise IV.3.8. Check that c is really a C-linear isomorphism W→ ¯W .
Lemma IV.3.9. (i) LetW be a C-vector space. Then there is a canonical
and functorial C-linear isomorphism
W⊗R C≃W⊕ ¯W, w⊗λ→ (λw,λ ¯w).
(Here the complex structure on W⊗R C is the one coming from the second
factor, C.)
(ii) Let X be another C-vector space, with a real structure XR. Let h be a
C-linear map W→X, so that Reh is an R-linear map W→XR. Then
Reh⊗ 1 :W⊗R C→XR⊗R C
is, modulo the obvious identiﬁcations, the map
W⊕ ¯W→X, (w,w′)↦→ 1
2(h(w) +c−1(h(w′))).
(Let us insist on the meaning of c−1(h(w′)): by functoriality, h induces (in fact, is)
a C-linear map ¯W→ ¯X, and c−1 is a C-linear isomorphism ¯X→X.)
Proof — Let’s prove (i). The map is obviously C-linear. Let us check that it is
injective. Any element of W⊗R C can be written w⊗ 1 +w′⊗i. Such an element
===== PDF PAGE 129 =====
IV.3. CLASSICAL MODULAR SYMBOLS AND MODULAR FORMS 129
is sent to (w +iw′, ¯w +i ¯w′) = (w +iw′,w−iw′). If that element is 0, then w and
w′ are 0, which shows the injectivity of our map. Since the source and target have
clearly the same dimension, this proves (i).
Let us prove (ii): we have (Reh⊗1)(w⊗1) = Reh(w), and (Reh⊗1)((iw)⊗i) =
iReh(iw) =−iImh(w). Since the isomorphism W⊗R C =W⊕W identiﬁesw⊗ 1
to (w, ¯w) and iw⊗i to (−w, ¯w), the map Re h⊗ 1 sends (w, ¯w) to Re h(w) and
(−w, ¯w) to−iImh(w). Therefore it sends (2w, 0) to Reh(w)+iImh(w) =h(w) and
(0, 2w′) to Reh(w′)−iImh(w′) =c−1(h(w′)). □
We shall apply this lemma to W = Sk+2(Γ) and X = Symb Γ(Vk(C)). The

Lemma IV.3.11. Let W be a complex vector space with an hermitian pairing
( , )Γ (conjugate-linear in the second variable).

Re((w1, ¯w2), (w′
1, ¯w′
2))Γ = 1
2((w1,w′
2)Γ + (w′

The R-linear antisymmetric pairing Im( , )Γ on W , extended by linearity into

Im((w1, ¯w2), (w′

Proof — Since w⊗ 1 in W⊗ C is identiﬁed with (w, ¯w) in W⊕ ¯W , we have
Re((w, ¯w), (w′,w′))Γ = Re(w,w′)Γ.
The same result applied to iw instead of w gives
iRe((w,− ¯w), (w′,w′))Γ = Re(iw,w′)Γ.
Adding the ﬁrst equality and −i times the second gives
2Re((w, 0), (w′,w′))Γ = Re(w,w′)Γ−iRei(w,w′)Γ = (w,w′)Γ.
The same equality with w′ replaced by iw′ gives
2Re((w, 0), (w′,−w′))Γ =−(w,w′)Γ.
Summing the two equalities above we get
Re((w, 0), (w′, 0)])Γ = 0
and taking instead the diﬀerence gives
2Re((w, 0), (0, ¯w′))Γ = (w,w′)Γ.
The formula for the symmetric bilinear map Re( , )Γ follows. The formula for
Im( , )Γ is proved similarly. □

source and this map (for example when k = 0, both sides have dimension 2g +c−1
where g is genus of the Riemann surface XΓ, and c the number of cusps for Γ), it
is an isomorphism.

0→ BSymbΓ(Vk(C))→ SymbΓ(Vk(C))→H1(YΓ, ˜Vk(C))→E k+2(Γ, C)→ 0

between BSymbΓ(Vk(C)) andEk+2(Γ, C), as follows: for x∈ BSymbΓ(Vk(C)) that
we see as an element of Symb Γ(Vk(C)) and y∈E k+2(Γ, C), the number ( x,y′) is
independent of the lift y′ ofy inH1(YΓ, ˜Vk(C)). To see this, let y′′ be an other lift,
so that y′−y′′ is the image of an element z in SymbΓ(Vk(C)). But ( x,y′−y′′) =
(x,z ) = ±(z,x ) and the latter is 0 since the image of x in H1(YΓ, ˜Vk(C)) is 0.
Obviously then, the pairing between BSymb Γ(Vk(C)) and Ek+2(Γ, C) is perfect.
The proves Theorem IV.3.27, since its last assertion follows from Prop. IV.2.3. □

Definition IV.3.33. If f is a new form in Ek+2(Γ, C), we denote by ϵ(f) the
eigenvalue of the involution ι on the boundary modular symbol corresponding to
f by the isomorphism IV.3.29. We refer to this number as the sign of the new
Eisenstein series f.
In other words, ϵ(f) is the ι-eigenvalues of the unique boundary symbols with
the same Hecke eigenvalues thanf. The following proposition determines the sign.
For the proof, we refer the reader to [ 11].
Proposition IV.3.34. The sign of a normal new Eisenstein series (cf. §I.6.4)
Ek+2,τ,ψ is τ(−1). The sign of an exceptional Eisenstein series E2,ℓ is 1.
IV.3.6. Summary. We summarize the main results of this section by the
following version of the theorem of Eichler-Shimura:
Theorem IV.3.35. Let Γ = Γ1(N) andH the polynomial ring generated by the
Hecke operatorsTl (for l ∤N) Ul (for l|N), and⟨a⟩ (for a∈ (Z/NZ)∗). We have
a canonical isomorphism of H-modules
SymbΓ(Vk(C)) =Sk+2(Γ, C)⊕Sk+2(Γ, C)⊕Ek+2(Γ, C) =Sk+2(Γ, C)⊕Mk+2(Γ, C)
which transform the ι involution into the involution that acts by +1 on the ﬁrst
summand Sk+2(Γ, C), −1 and the second summand, and by some involution on
Ek+2(Γ, C). We have canonical H-embedding
Sk+2(Γ, C)↪→ Symb±
Γ (Vk(C))↪→Mk+2(Γ, C).
Finally, all those results hold when C is replaced everywhere by any ﬁeld of
characteristic 0, excepted that the isomorphisms are not anymore canonical.
Proof — The isomorphisms over C are obtained trivially by putting together
Theorem IV.3.19 and Corollary IV.3.29. The embeddings follow. Finally, to prove
that there are also such isomorphisms or embeddings over any ﬁeld L of character-
istic 0, we only need to consider the case N = Q. In this case, it follows from the
following elementary statement: if M and N are two ﬁnite-dimensional Q-vector
spaces provided with an action of a ring H, such that M⊗ C≃N⊗ C as C-vector
spaces andH-modules, then M≃ N as Q-vector spaces and H-modules. Indeed,
choosing a basis of M andN, a Q-linear application f :M→N is described by its
matrix (xi,j). The fact that f :M→N is an isomorphism, as well as the fact that
it commutes with the action of H are expressed by a system of linear equations
satisﬁed by thexi,j with coeﬃcients in Q. By hypothesis, this systems has solution
in C. Hence by Gauss method it has a solution on Q. □

Through the Atkin-Lehner’s theory does not carry over for modular symbols
(cf. Remark IV.3.31), many consequences of it carries trivially using the theorem
above:
Corollary IV.3.36. Let L be any ﬁeld of characteristic 0. Let H0 be the
polynomial algebra generated by the Tl (l ∤N) and the diamond operators ⟨a⟩ (a∈
(Z/NZ)∗). Let λ be a system of eigenvaluesH0→L appearing in Symb±
Γ1(N)(Vk(L)).
Then the set of divisors of N such that λ appears in Symb±
Γ1(N)(Vk(L)) has a mini-
mal elementN0, that we call the minimal level ofλ as in the classical Atkin-Lehner’s
theory (§I.6.4). In particular the notions of λ being new and old, l-new and l-old
make sense. Also, dim Symb±
Γ1(N)(Vk(L))[λ] =σ(N/N0).

Lemma IV.4.7. The dimension of Symb±

Proof — We have a natural embedding Sk+2(Γ, C)[λ]↪→ Symb±
Γ (Vk(C))[λ] whose
cokernel is a direct summand in Ek+2(Γ, C)∨[λ] by Theorem IV.3.19 and Corol-
lary IV.3.29. But the space Ek+2(Γ, C)[λ] is 0 (hence also Ek+2(Γ, C)∨[λ]) since
by the Peterson estimate, there is no Eisenstein series which is an eigenform with
the same eigenvalues as a cuspidal form. And Sk+2(Γ, C)[λ] has dimension 1 by
multiplicity one (Cor. I.6.17). □

Remark IV.4.10. Sometimes we want to work integrally rather than ratio-
nally, and have a smaller indeterminacy on the periods Ω ±
f . Let Of be the ring
of integer of f. We know that λ(H) ⊂ Of, so Symb ±
Γ (Vk(Of))[λ] is an Of-
lattice in the 1-dimensional space Symb ±
Γ (Vk(Kf)][λ]. The minor irritating issue
we are facing is that since Of is a Dedekind domain, but not necessarily principal,
Symb±
Γ (Vk(Of))[λ] might not be free of rank one. It is at any rate projective of
rank one.
When it happens that Symb±
Γ (Vk(Of))[λ] is free of rank one, we can deﬁne Ω±
f
by the requirement that φ±
f/Ω±
f is a generator of Symb ±
Γ (Vk(Of))[λ]. Thus the
periods Ω±
f are well deﬁned up to a unit in O∗
f. For example, if f has coeﬃcient
in Q, then Kf = Q,Of = Z which is principal, so Symb±
Γ (Vk(Z))[λ] is free of rank
one, and the periods Ω±
f are well deﬁned up to a sign.
When Symb±
Γ (Vk(Of))[λ] is not free, we have several solutions, depending on
what we want to do. For example we can replace Kf by a ﬁnite extension K′ on
which this module becomes free (for example, the Hilbert Class Field of Kf will
do), and have Ω±
f well deﬁned up to an element in O∗
K′. Or we can work at one
prime at a time: for p a prime of Of, the localization Of,(p) is principal, so it is
possible to require that Ω ±

f/Ω±
f is in Symb±
Γ (Vk(Of))[λ],
and generates that module after localizing at p. Such a requirement ﬁxes Ω±
f up to
multiplication by an element of K∗
p whose p-valuation is 0, which is often suﬃcient
in the applications. When Ω± is chosen this way, we shall say that it isp-normalized
(that’s our terminology, not a standard one).

M/(M∩A)⊕ (M∩B) and since the the scalar product on M is a perfect pairing
we know by Prop I.7.16 that this moduleC is non-zero if and only if the discriminant
of the scalar product on M∩A has positive p valuation, that is if and only if (a)
holds.
But on the other hand, the fact that the congruence module C is non-zero is
equivalent (cf. Prop. I.7.15) to the existence of a modular symbol φ′ in B such
that φ′≡ aφ+
f +bφ−
f (mod p), with a,b ∈O p, not both in p. By Deligne-Serre’s
lemma (up to allowing ﬁnite extension K′ of Kf and replacement of p be a prime
p′ of K′ dividing p) this is equivalent to the same thing with φ′ assume to be
an eigenvector for all Hecke operators. Such an eigenvector φ′ deﬁnes a cuspidal
normalized eigenformg∈Sk+2(Γ,K′) with the same Hecke eigenvalues. HenceC is
non-zero if and only if there exists a cuspidal normalized eigenform g whose Hecke
eigenvalues are congruent to those off. In our case, this is also equivalent to g≡f
(mod p), for the two residual form g mod p and f (mod p) in Sk+2(Γ,Op/p) will
have the same eigenvalues, hence the same Fourier coeﬃcients, hence will be equal
by the q-development principle.
In other words, C̸= 0 is equivalent to (b). This proves the theorem. □
IV.5. Notes and references
The most important part of this chapter, namely the identiﬁcation of spaces
of modular forms with cohomology groups of modular curves is due to Eichler and
Shimura: see [ 49], [ 96], [ 98]. The reformulation of this identiﬁcation in terms
of modular symbols is due to Ash and Stevens: see [ 2]. Modular symbols (more
precisely what we call classical modular symbols in this book) had been invented by
Manin in relation to the special values of L-functions of modular forms, and their
theory had been extended by Shokurov, Amice-V´ elu, Mazur-Tate-Teitelbaum: see
[72], [ 100], [ 1], [ 77]. The results about boundary symbols and Eisenstein series
are mostly due to the author and Dasgupta: see [ 11]. Some of the results about
the scalar products are due to Hida ([ 57]), as well as the idea of the ﬁnal theorem:
[57], [58]
This material is standard basic knowledge to specialists in the arithmetic theory
of modular forms, but its dispersal in many old papers written in German, French,
Russian and English often makes it diﬃcult to learn. An important part of it (but
nor modular symbols nor Eisenstein series) is covered in the ﬁrst part of see [ 57].
We have tried here to give a complete and truly self-contained treatment, which we
hope will be useful to the students.

V.1. Rigid analytic functions and distributions
In all this section, R denotes a commutative Qp-Banach algebra with a norm
denoted|| extending the usual absolute value on Qp, so that|p| = 1/p (as in§II.1).
V.1.1. Some modules of sequences and their dual. For r any positive
real number, we shall denote by cr(R) (resp. br(R)) the Banach R-module of
sequences (an)n≥0, with an∈ R and|an|rn→ 0 when n→∞ (resp. with |an|rn
bounded above), with the norm |(an)| = supn≥0|an|rn. When r = 1, we shall
denote1 c(R) for c1(R) (resp. b(R) for b1(R)).
Note that the Cauchy product makes cr(R) and br(R) Banach algebras overR.
Lemma V.1.1. The natural map cr(Qp) ˆ⊗QpR→ cr(R) is an isometry, and is
compatible with theR-algebra structure. In particular, for any continuous morphism
R→ R′ of Qp-Banach algebras, we have an isomorphism cr(R) ˆ⊗RR′ = cr(R′) of
R′-Banach algebras.
Proof — The ﬁrst statement is proved exactly as Lemma II.1.22. The second
follows from the ﬁrst and the associativity of completed tensor product. □
Lemma V.1.2. If there is a multiplicative element 2 ρ in R∗ with|ρ| = r, then
there are isometric isomorphisms of R-algebrascr(R)≃c(R) and br(R)≃b(R).

cr(R)→c(R) that sends (an) to (anρn). It is obviously an isometric isomorphism
of algebras. Same proof for br(R) and b(R). □

V.2. Overconvergent functions and distributions
In this section as in the preceding one, R denotes a commutative Qp-Banach
algebra with a norm denoted || extending the usual absolute value on Qp, so that
|p| = 1/p.
V.2.1. Semi-normic modules and Fr´ echet modules. Let M be an R-
module. A semi-norm on M is a map p : M→ R≥0 such that for every x∈ R,
m,n∈M, p(m +m′)≤ max(p(m),p (n)) and p(xm)≤|x|p(m). Note that one has
automatically p(0) = 0 but there may be non-zero m∈ M such that p(m) = 0.
Also if x is multiplicative, p(xm) =|x|p(m) by the same proof as Exercise II.1.1.
A family of semi-norms ( pi)i∈I on an R-module M deﬁnes a topology on M,
for which a basis of neighborhood of an element m0∈ M are the sets VS,ϵ,m0 :=
{m∈ M,∀i∈ S,pi(m−m0) < ϵ} where S runs among ﬁnite subsets of I and ϵ
among positive real numbers. Addition and multiplication by a scalar in M are
bi-continuous for this topology. We call a topology of this form semi-normic.
A Fr´ echet moduleM over R is an R-module M with a semi-normic topology
which is metrizable and complete. Banach modules are examples of Fr´ echet mod-
ules.
Exercise V.2.1. We deﬁne a normable group as a topological abelian group G whose
topology can be deﬁned by a ultrametric distance that is invariant by translation (that is
d(x +z,y +z) =d(x,y ) for all x,y,z ∈G).
a.– Show that it amounts to the same to ask thatG is the underlying topological group
of a normed group in the sense of [ 21,§1.1], that is that the topology of G is deﬁned
by a norm || : G→ R+ such that for all x,y in G,|x| = 0 if and only if x = 0; and
|x−y|≤ max(|x|,|y|).
b.– Let R be a Banach algebra over Qp andM a semi-normic R-module. Show that if
the topology of M is deﬁned by countably many semi-norms, then the underlying topolog-
ical abelian group M is a normable group, and in particular M is metrizable.
===== PDF PAGE 156 =====
156 V. RIGID ANALYTIC MODULAR SYMBOLS AND p-ADIC L-FUNCTIONS
If f : M→ N is a continuous linear map of Fr´ echet modules, then the open
image theorem states that if f is surjective, it is open. The proof is the same as in
the standard case, and can be found in [ 22, Th´ eor` eme 1, Chapitre 1,§3.3].
Let M and N be R-module, and let p be a semi-norm on M and q a semi-
norm on N. Then one deﬁnes a semi-norm p⊗q on M⊗R N by setting ( p⊗
q)(z) = inf z=∑r
i=1vi⊗wi supi∈{1,...,r}p(vi)q(wi). If M (resp. N) has a semi-normic
topology deﬁned by a family ( pi)i∈I (resp. (qj)j∈J) of semi-norms, then we deﬁne
M ˆ⊗RN as the completion of M⊗RN for the topology deﬁned by the semi-norms
(pi⊗ qj)(i,j)∈I×J, provided with the semi-normic topology deﬁned by the same
semi-norms. If N = R′ is a Banach R-algebra, then M ˆ⊗NR′ is naturally a semi-
normicR′-module, and ifR′′ is a BanachR′-algebra, there is a natural bi-continuous
isomorphism of R′′-module (M ˆ⊗RR′) ˆ⊗R′R′′ =M ˆ⊗RR′′.
We now give our attention to the case where R =L is a ﬁnite extension of Qp
normed by its absolute value. In this case, the notion of semi-normic topology is
the same as the notion of locally convex topology as explained in [ 90,§4]. More
precisely, ifO is the ring of integers of L, and V a vector space over L, a lattice
Λ in V is anO-submodule of V such that LΛ = V . A subset of V is convex if it
is of the form v + Λ where v∈ V and Λ is a lattice. A topology on V is locally
convex if every point has a basis of neighborhoods that are convex. Given a family
of lattices (Λi)i∈I such that for every i,j ∈I, there exists k∈I with Λk⊂ Λi∩ Λj,
and for all i∈ I and x∈ K∗, there exists j∈ I such that Λj⊂ xΛi, there is one
and only one topology on V for which a basis of neighborhood of v are the v + Λi.
This topology is locally convex, and all locally convex topologies arise this way.
The gauge of a lattice Λ is the semi-normpΛ deﬁned bypΛ(v) = infx∈K,v∈xΛ|x|.
If a locally convex topology onV is deﬁned by a family of lattices Λi as above, then
it is also deﬁned by the semi-norms pΛi and V is semi-normic. Conversely, given
a family of semi-norms ( pi)i∈I, the lattices of the form VS,ϵ,0 for ϵ >0 and S⊂ I
ﬁnite form a family satisfying the above condition which deﬁnes the same topology
as the semi-norms pi for i∈I.
If V is locally convex space over L, the dual V∨ of V is the space HomL(V,L )
of all continuous linear map V → L. We provide V∨ with the so-called strong
topology, deﬁned as follows. Recall that a subset B of V is bounded if pi(B)
is bounded above for every semi-norm pi deﬁning the topology of V . For every
bounded subset B of V , we deﬁne a semi-norm pB on V∨ by setting pB(l) =
supb∈B|l(b)|. The strong topology on V∨ is the one deﬁned by this family of semi-
norms. We note that our notion of dual extends the one we have already used in
the case of Banach spaces.

Remark V.2.11. Viˇ sik ([108]) and Colmez ([35]) use an interesting equivalent

derivatives and such that the last derivative is Lipschitz. We shall not use this
deﬁnition.

V.3. The weight space
The p-adic weight spaceW that we shall deﬁne below plays two diﬀerent roles
in this book, both very important. First its elements generalize the possible weights
k∈ N of a modular form, more precisely, the couples ( k,ϵ ) where ϵ is the p-part of
the nebentypus of a modular form. Second it is the set in which we will draw the
variable of our p-adic L-functions, in a way analogous to the fact that the variable
of the complexL-functionL(f,s,χ ) is a couple (s,χ ), wheres is a complex variable
and χ a Dirichlet character, or which is equivalent as we have seen in §IV.4.1, a
continuous character A∗
Q/Q∗→ C∗.

characterκ : A∗

is a canonical isomorphism Z∗

group ( Z/MZ)∗× (Z/qZ)∗ = ( Z/MqZ)∗ and a character κp on 1 + qZp. Such a
character is of course completely deﬁned by the imageκp(γ) of the chosen generator
γ of 1 +qZp. Since γpn
→ 1 when n→∞ , we have κp(γ)pn
→ 1, and this easily
implies that |κp(γ)− 1| < 1. Conversely, by completeness, any element x∈ R∗
satisfying|x− 1|< 1 deﬁnes a continuous character κp : 1 +qZp→R∗ sending γ
to x.
The functor R↦→ Hom((Z/qM Z)∗,R∗) for all Qp-algebras R is obviously rep-
resentable by a ﬁnite scheme (hence a ﬁnite rigid analytic space) over Qp, that we
shall denote ((Z/qM Z)∗)∨. It has φ(qM ) points over Cp, but less over Qp, since not
all characters must take values in Qp. When M = 1, theφ(q) points of this schemes
are deﬁned over Qp, and correspond to the power of the Teichm¨ uller characterτ
The functor R↦→{ x∈ R∗,|x− 1| < 1} for all complete Qp-algebras is rep-
resented by a rigid analytic space, the open ball B(1, 1) of center 1 and radius
1.
SinceWM(R) = (( Z/pM Z)∗)∨(R)×B(1, 1)(R) functorially in R, by the map
κ↦→ (κf,κp(γ)), we have shown thatWM is representable by a rigid analytic scheme
((Z/qM Z)∗)∨×B(1, 1). Over Cp, or even an extension of Qp containing allφ(M)-th
roots of unity, it is the union of φ(qM ) copies of the open unit ball of center 1 and
radius 1.

V.3. THE WEIGHT SPACE 165
For later use, let us record
Scholium V.3.3. Over Cp, or over any extension of Qp containing all φ(M)-th
roots of unity,WM is a ﬁnite union of componentsWM,κf indexed by the characters
κf : ( Z/qM Z)∗→ C∗
p. Each of these components is isomorphic to the open ball
B(1, 1). A generator γ of 1 + qZp being ﬁxed, an isomorphism WM,κf (Cp) →
B(1, 1)(Cp) is given by κ↦→κ(γ).

V.3.3. Some remarkable elements in the weight space. Letk∈ Z. Then
the maps z↦→zk, Z∗
p→ Q∗
p is a continuous character, hence an element of W(Qp).
We shall denote byk this element inW(Qp), and we call those elements the integral
points, or integral weights. Hence we have an inclusion Z⊂W (Qp). Note that
r(k) = +∞ (cf. Deﬁnition V.3.5).
Lemma V.3.7. The set Z of integral weights is very Zariski-dense in W (cf.
Deﬁnition II.8.1). The p-adic topology onW induces by restriction the topology on
Z for which a basis of neighborhood of a∈ Z is given by the congruences classes of
a modulopn(p− 1) for all n.
Proof — Any character κf of Z∗
p factoring through ( Z/qZ)∗ has the form z↦→
τ(z)k0 for some integerk0 well determined moduloφ(q). The intersection of Z with
the open ball B(1, 1) corresponding to κf is the set {γk, k≡ k0 (mod φ(q))}. In
any ball of center γk will sit all the points γk+φ(q)pn
for n large enough, and those
points, having an accumulation point in that ball, are Zariski-dense, which proves
the ﬁrst assertion. For two integers k,k′ seen as characters to be close, we need
their κf to be equal, so k≡k′ (mod φ(q)) and γk to be close to γk′
, which means
k close to k′ p-adically. This proves the second assertion. □

Now suppose we have chosen an embedding¯Q→ Cp, and an embedding ¯Q→ C.
Slightly more generally, let ( k,ϵ ) be a pair of an integer k∈ Z, and a Dirichlet
character ϵ. The character ϵ takes values in C∗, but those values are actually in
¯Q∗, and well determined there by our choice of embedding, and we can see them
as element of C∗
p by our other embedding. Write the conductor of ϵ aspνN, where
N is prime to p. If N | M, we can see ϵ as a character Z∗
p× (Z/MZ)∗ → C∗
p
(factoring through the quotient ( Z/pνZ)∗× (Z/NZ)∗). To (k,ϵ ) we ﬁnally attach
the character z↦→zkϵ(z) of Z∗
p× (Z/MZ)∗, that we see as an element of WM(Cp).
Those points are called the arithmetic points ofWM(Cp).

fact that as we have already noticed, the action of S0(p) onAk[r](L) and onPk[r]
are given by the same formulas. The assertion for Vk follows. □

Remark V.5.5. It might seem at ﬁrst glance that in the above proof, the
inequality||φ(Dγ)||p−nr≤||φ(Dγ)||r is gross when n≥ 1 and can be improved. But
this is not true, as for the constant function 1 for example, ||1||p−nr =||1||r. Thus,
the upper bound we get for the norm of Up for example, namely 1, can not be
improved, at least this way. This estimate will play a fundamental role in the proof
of Stevens’ control theorem below.
Theorem V.5.6 (Stevens). Let 0<r< min(r(κ),p ). Let ν be a real number.
(i) The operator Up acts compactly on SymbΓ(Dκ[r](R))
(ii) The module SymbΓ(Dκ[r](R))≤ν is projective of ﬁnite rank.
(iii) The natural maps (that is, induced by the transposed restriction maps)
SymbΓ(Dκ[r1](R))≤ν→ SymbΓ(Dκ[r2](R))≤ν
are isomorphisms for any 0<r 1≤r2< min(r(κ),p ).

belongs to Symb Γ(Dκ[r/p](R)). In other words, the map Up factors through:
(V.5.1) Symb Γ(Dκ[r](R))→ SymbΓ(Dκ[r/p](R)) restriction−→ SymbΓ(Dκ[r](R)).
Therefore, to prove (i) it suﬃces to prove that the second map is compact. But by

SymbΓ. Since any vector in Symb Γ(Dκ[r′])≤ν is in the image of Up, the surjectivity
follows from (V.5.1). □
V.5.2. Space of overconvergent modular symbols of ﬁnite slope. In all
this subsection, L will be a ﬁnite extension of Qp, and we shall work with various
vector spaces over L, all provided with an operator Up. If V is a Banach space
overL (hence potentially orthonormalizable), and Up is compact, we have already
deﬁned for each real ν, the part of slope ≤ ν of V , denoted V≤ν. We shall need
to extend the deﬁnition to vector spaces V that are not Banach (e.g. to Fr´ echet
spaces), and we do this as follows:
===== PDF PAGE 176 =====
176 V. RIGID ANALYTIC MODULAR SYMBOLS AND p-ADIC L-FUNCTIONS
Definition V.5.7. Let ν be a real number.
(i) We say that a monic polynomial P (X)∈L[X] has slope ≤ν (resp. <ν )
if all its roots in some extension of L have p-adic valuations ≤ ν (resp.
<ν )
(ii) If V is anyL-vector space with a linear operator Up acting on it, we deﬁne
the subspace of vectors of slope ≤ν (resp. <ν ) than ν, denoted by V≤ν
(resp. V<ν), as the sum of the subspaces ker P (Up) where P runs among
monic polynomials of slope ≤ν (resp. <ν ).
When V is a Banach space and Up a compact operator, this new deﬁnition of
V≤ν coincides with the one given in Deﬁnition II.4.1 in view of Lemma II.4.4. In
this caseV≤ν is ﬁnite dimensional by Prop. II.4.2. This new deﬁnition allows us to
talk of SymbΓ(D†
k(L))≤ν or SymbΓ(D†
k(L))<ν.

are isomorphisms for any 0≤r1<r 2≤ min(r(κ),p ).

functors from the category of L-vector spaces with an endomorphism Up to the
category of L-vector spaces.

V.5.3. Computation of an H0. In this§, L is a ﬁnite extension of Qp.
Lemma V.5.14. Let ∆ be the operator of discrete diﬀerentiation on A†[r](L):
∆f :=f(z + 1)−f(z).
The sequence
0→L→A†[r](L) ∆→A†[r](L)→ 0
is exact, and ∆ is continuous and open.
===== PDF PAGE 178 =====
178 V. RIGID ANALYTIC MODULAR SYMBOLS AND p-ADIC L-FUNCTIONS
Proof. The exactness in the middle expresses the fact that if ∆ f = 0, i.e.
f(z + 1) = f(z) for all z ∈ Zp, then f is constant; this is clear as we deduce
f(z +n) = f(z) for all n∈ Z by induction, and then f(z +a) = f(z) for all
a∈ Zp by continuity, thereforef constant on Zp. The exactness at the right, that
is the surjectivity of ∆ is a little harder, and quite analog to Lemma V.4.11: if
f∈A†[r](L), then it is in A[r′](L) for some r′ > r, we can write formally for any
e∈ Zp, f(z) = ∑
n≥0bn(e)(z−e)(n) where x(n) := x(x− 1)... (x−n + 1). This
is easily seen from the writing f(z) = ∑
n≥0an(e)(z−e)n using that there is a
triangular matrix with entries in Zp and diagonal terms equal to 1 that transforms
the basis ((z−e)n)n of the space of polynomials into the basis ((z−e)(n))n, and one
sees the same way that for any ρ,|bn(e)|ρn goes to 0 if and only if|an(e)|ρn goes to
0. In particular, the series ∑
n≥0bn(e)(z−e)(n) converges to f(z) for|z−e|≤ r′.
Let us now deﬁne g by the formula (for e∈ Zp) g(z) = ∑∞
n=1
bn−1(e)(z−e)(n)
n . It is
immediately seen that this series converges for|z−e|<r′′ wherer′′ is any real such
that r′′ < r′, and for a given z is independent on e. Hence, choosing an r′′ such
thatr<r ′′<r′, we have deﬁnes ag∈A†[r′′](L)⊂A†[r](L) and we see easily that
∆g =f because ∆(z−e)(n) = (z−e)(n−1). This proves that ∆ is surjective. The
same argument as in Lemma V.4.11 also proves that ∆ is continuous and open. □

V.5.5. Stevens’s control theorem.
Theorem V.5.18. LetL be any ﬁnite extension of Qp.
(i) [Stevens’s control theorem] The natural map
ρk : Symb Γ(Dk(L))<k+1−→SymbΓ(Vk(L))<k+1
is an isomorphism.
(ii) If we provide both SymbΓ(Dk(L))<k+1 and SymbΓ(Vk(L))<k+1 of the norm
||||1, then ρk is an isometry.
Proof — We shall give two diﬀerent proofs of the important result (i). The ﬁrst
one, which is the proof outlined by Stevens in his preprint [ 105] and completed by
Pollack-Stevens [85] is sleek and short, but does not give (ii). From Prop. V.5.17,
we have an exact sequence
0→ (SymbΓ(D−2−k(L))(k+1))<k+1→ SymbΓ(Dk(L))<k+1→ SymbΓ(Vk(L))<(k+1)→ 0,
We can rewrite the ﬁrst term as SymbΓ(D−2−k(L))<0, but sinceUp acts on SymbΓ(D†
−2−k(L))
with norm at most 1 (Lemma V.5.4), this is 0. The result (i) follows.
The second proof is essentially the method of Viˇ sik (cf. [ 108] or [ 77]), and
will give (ii) as well as (i). Let φ ∈ SymbΓ(Vk(L))<k+1. We shall prove that
there is a unique Φ ∈ SymbΓ(Dk(L))<k+1 such that ρk(Φ) =φ, and moreover that
||Φ||1 =||φ||1. We may clearly assume that φ is a generalized eigenvector ofUp, then
by induction a true eigenvector of Up, of eigenvalues λ∈L.
First we prove the uniqueness of Φ. Let D∈ ∆0. Write µ = Φ(D). Then µ is
a distribution of order ≤ν. Moreover for every n∈ N, a∈ Zp, and 0≤j≤k we
haveµ((z−a)/pn)j1a+pnZp) = λ−nΦ(D|Unp )(zj) = λ−nφ(D|Unp )(zj). Hence all the
valuesµ((z−a)/pn)j1a+pnZp) are determined by φ, hence µ itself is determined by
φ by Theorem V.2.13(i), and so is Φ.
Now we prove the existence of Φ, as follows. For D∈ ∆0 we deﬁne Φ(D) as
the unique distribution µ of slope ≤ ν such that for every n∈ N, a∈ Zp, and
0≤j≤k =µ((z−a)/pn)j1a+pnZp) =λ−nφ(D|Unp )(zk). Such a distribution exists
by Theorem V.2.13(ii), because the additivity (V.2.2) hypothesis follows from the
fact φ|Up = λUp and hypothesis (V.2.3) with C =||φ||1 follows from |λ|≥ p−ν;
moreover it satisﬁes||µ||1≤||φ||1. By uniqueness of that µ, it follows easily that Φ
===== PDF PAGE 181 =====
V.6. THE MELLIN TRANSFORM 181
is a modular symbol, which clearly satisﬁes ρk(Φ) =φ and||Φ||1≤||φ||1. The other
equality||φ||1≤||Φ||1 follows from the fact that ρk has norm at most one since the
natural mapD[1](L)→V k(L) has norm one by the theorem of Hahn-Banach, as
it is the dual map of the inclusion map Pk(L)→A [1](L) which is clearly of norm
one. □

V.6. The Mellin transform
V.6.1. The real Mellin transform. The real Mellin transform takes a func-
tion g(y) of one positive real variable y, with rapid decay near 0 and ∞, to the
analytic function of one complex variable
s↦→M(g)(s) =
∫
R∗
+
g(y)ysdy
y .
For instance, theL-function of a modular form f(z) is, up to a simple factor Γ(s)
(2π)s ,
the Mellin transform of f(iy), see Prop. IV.4.1.
We can see the mapy↦→ys as a character of R∗
+, and all continuous characters
R∗
+→ C∗ are of this form. Thus, we can consider the Mellin transform of f,M(f),
as a function of a continuous character R∗
+→ C∗, rather than as a function of s.
Moreoverf(iy) dy

+. Thus we can see the Mellin transform as
a map µ↦→M(µ) from the set of measures µ on R∗
+ with rapid decay with values
in the space of analytic functions on the set of characters Hom( R∗
+, C∗). Actually,
since the charactersy↦→ys areC∞, we can even deﬁne the Mellin transform M(µ)
of a distribution µ with rapid decay at 0 and ∞.

V.6.2. The p-adic Mellin transform. By analogy, we now deﬁne thep-adic

Definition V.6.2. Let us denote by R the Qp-space of analytic functions on
the weight spaceW.
===== PDF PAGE 182 =====
182 V. RIGID ANALYTIC MODULAR SYMBOLS AND p-ADIC L-FUNCTIONS
The weight space W is a disjoint union of ﬁnitely many balls B(1, 1) (see
Scholium V.3.3). For any such ball, and any positive number r < 1, r ∈ pQ,
an analytic function f attains its maximum on the smaller ball B(1,r ), and this
maximum deﬁnes a semi-norm on R. Provided with the topology deﬁned by all
this semi-norms,R is a Fr´ echet space.
If R is a Banach algebra, and σ : Z∗
p→ R∗ is a continuous character, that is
an element ofW(R), then σ extended by 0 on pZp, belongs to the set of function
A[r](R) for some r >0. A measure µ∈D †(Qp) belongs to D[r](Qp) and deﬁnes
a measure (µ⊗ 1) in D[r](R). This measure can be applied to σ giving a value
(µ⊗ 1)(σ)∈R. We denote this value by
∫
Z∗p
σ(z)dµ(z).
Proposition and Definition V.6.3. For any overconvergent distributionµ∈
D†(Qp), there exists a unique analytic function M(µ)∈R such that for every ﬁnite
extension L of Qp, and every σ∈W (L) = Hom(Z∗

M(µ)(σ) =
∫
Z∗p
σ(z)dµ(z)∈L∗.(V.6.1)
The function M(µ) is called the p-adic Mellin transform of the distribution µ.
Proof — The uniqueness of M(µ) is clear since a function on a reduced rigid
analytic space over Qp is deﬁned by its value at all L-points for L running among
ﬁnite extension of Qp.
For the existence, let us choose an admissible covering of W by Qp-aﬃnoids
Ui = SpecRi. The natural inclusion Ui→W deﬁnes a character σi : Z∗
p→ R∗
i .
Thus
∫

(V.6.1) for every σ∈ Ui(L), L/Qp ﬁnite extension. Those functions Mi(µ) on Ui
satisﬁes the gluing condition and therefore deﬁnes a function M(µ) onW, that is
an element ofR which satisﬁes (V.6.1). □

V.6.3. Properties of the p-adic Mellin transform. ForU an open subset
of Zp, and µ a distribution over Zp, we deﬁne the restriction µ|U of µ to U as
the distribution µ|U(f) = µ(f1U). We say that µ has support in U if µ = µ|U.
Obviously, the Mellin transform Mµ depends only of the restriction µ|Z∗p of µ to
Z∗
p. We callDU(Qp) the subspace of distribution inD(Qp) with support in U. It is
clearly closed, hence inherits from D†(Qp) a structure of Fr´ echet space.

V.6.4. The Mellin transform over a Banach algebra. Now, letR be any
Banach algebra over Qp. Recall that D†(R) =D†(Qp) ˆ⊗QpR by Prop. V.2.7.
Definition V.6.12. The Mellin transform over L is the L-linear map
MR :D†(R) =D†(Qp) ˆ⊗QpR→R ˆ⊗QpR
deﬁned by MR =M ˆ⊗IdR.
IfR is an aﬃnoid algebra over Qp, then an element ofR ˆ⊗QpR is just a function
on the rigid space W× SpR analytic in the ﬁrst variable and locally analytic in
the second. This is using this Mellin transform over R that we shall construct two-
variables p-adic L-function: one variable in the weight space W and one variable
in the rigid analytic space Sp R, which in the application will be an open set in an
eigenvariety: see Chapter VII.
V.7. Applications to the p-adic L-functions of non-critical slope
modular forms
As usual,N≥ 1 is an integer, and we denote byH the Hecke algebra generated
by the Tl, l ∤N, Ul, l|N, and⟨a⟩, a∈ (Z/NZ)∗.
Letf(z) =∑
n≥1anqn,q =e2iπz be a cuspidal form of weightk+2, level Γ1(N)
and character ϵ, which is an eigenform for all Hecke operators T∈H .
The idea of Stevens’s method to construct a p-adic L-function attached to f
is to see f as a symbol in Symb Γ1(N)(Vk) by Manin-Shokurov’s theorem and to
lift it uniquely as a symbol in Symb Γ1(N)(D†
k) by Stevens control theorem (Theo-
rem V.5.18). In order to do the latter, we apparently need two things: that the
level Γ1(N) be a subgroup of Γ 0(p) (that is that p|N), otherwise Symb Γ1(N)(D†
k)
is not even deﬁned; and that the slope of our modular symbol be strictly less than
k + 1. The second condition is an intrinsic limit of the method – see next chapter
for how it can be removed. The ﬁrst condition p|N is quite restrictive, but fortu-
nately, there is a way to make it true when it is not to begin with. Actually, and
interestingly, there is not one way, but two ways to do so:
===== PDF PAGE 185 =====
V.7. APPLICATIONS TO THE p-ADIC L-FUNCTIONS OF NON-CRITICAL SLOPE MODULAR FORMS 185
V.7.1. Reﬁnements. We start with an f as above, and we assume that we
are in the case where p is prime to N. An easy way to remedy to this problem is
to consider f(z) as a form of level Γ := Γ 1(N)∩ Γ0(p) (and same weight k + 2),
which it is. But as is well known, the form f(pz) is also a form of level Γ (and same
weight).
Exercise V.7.1. Check this fact.
The forms f(z) and f(pz) have the inconvenience of not being eigenvectors for
Up. But if we call α and β the two roots of the equations
X2−apX +pk+1ϵ(p),(V.7.1)
and if we set
fα(z) = f(z)−βf (pz) (V.7.2)
fβ(z) = f(z)−αf(pz),(V.7.3)
then one has:
Lemma V.7.2. The two forms fα and fβ are eigenforms for Up of eigenvalues
α and β respectively.
Proof — Let Vp be the operator f(z)↦→ f(pz). One has UpVp = Id and Tp =
Up +pk+1⟨p⟩Vp (see I.6.4). Therefore Upfα =Up(f−βVff) = Upf−βf =apf−
ϵ(p)pk+1Vpf−βf =αf−αβVpf =αfα. □
The formsfα andfβ are also eigenforms for all the other Hecke operators, with
the same eigenvalues as f, and for the Diamond operators: their nebentypus is ϵ
seen as a non-primitive character of ( Z/NpZ)∗ – that is, it is trivial at p.
Definition V.7.3. The forms fα and fβ are called the reﬁnements4 of f at p.
Remark V.7.4. It is conjectured, but not known excepted in weight 2 (that is
when k = 0), that α̸=β. See [ 32]
Exercise V.7.5. If f is a newform then f(z) and f(pz) form a basis of the space of
all forms of weightk + 2, level Γ1(N)∩ Γ0(p), nebentypusϵ, and with the same eigenvalues
as f for operators Tl, for l prime to Np and Ul for l̸=p. If α̸=β, then fα and fβ are a
basis of that space, in which Up is diagonal. If α =β, then Up is not diagonal.
We note that the complex numbers α and β are algebraic by deﬁnitions, and
actually integral. Choosing an embedding ¯Q ↪→ C (which determines α and β as
4Another terminology calls them p-stabilizations.
===== PDF PAGE 186 =====

elements of ¯Q) and then of ¯Q→ Cp (which allows us to see α andβ as elements of
Cp), we have by looking at equation (V.7.1)
vp(α) +vp(β) = k + 1 (V.7.4)
vp(α)≥ 0 (V.7.5)
vp(β)≥ 0 (V.7.6)
Hence 0 ≤ vp(α),vp(β)≤ k + 1. We also have vp(ap)≥ min(vp(α),vp(β)) with
equality when vp(α)̸= vp(β). In particular, if vp(α) = 0, then vp(β) = k + 1̸= 0
and vp(ap) = 0.
Definition V.7.6. We say that f is p-ordinary if vp(ap) = 0. If it is, then
vp(α) = 0 andvp(β) =k + 1 up to exchangingα andβ, and we callfα the ordinary
reﬁnement, and fβ the critical slope reﬁnement.
V.7.2. Construction of the p-adicL-functions. We shall deal at the same
time with the cases p ∤ N and p| N. In both cases, let Γ = Γ 1(N)∩ Γ0(p) which
of course is simply Γ 1(N) in the case p|N. Let us call g = fα if p ∤ N, where α
is one of the root of (V.7.1) with vp(α) < k+ 1. We have two choices of such α
whenf is not p-ordinary (and α̸=β), but only one when f isp-ordinary. If p|N,
then we set g = f. The Up-eigenvalue of g is α := ap and we shall assume that
vp(ap)<k + 1.
To summarize, in any case we end up with a normalized cuspidal eigenform g
of weight k + 2, level Γ containing Γ 0(p), nebentypus ϵ, and such that Upg = αg
with α < k+ 1. We now proceed to the construction. Remember that we have
chosen embeddings ¯Q→ ¯C and ¯Q→ ¯Qp.

(growth rate) The order of growth of Lp(fα,σ ) is≤vp(α).
Remark V.7.11. (i) The construction of the p-adicL-function of a mod-
ular form we have given (or for that matter, any other known construction)
is quite indirect in the sense that it uses complex transcendental method.
One could expect that to construct from an algebraic object (the sequence
of coeﬃcients an of f) a p-adic object Lp(fα,σ ), it would suﬃce to use
purely algebraic and p-adic means. But we used the modular symbols
φg±, which is deﬁned by complex transcendental means from g.
(ii) It is expected, but not known in general, that the order of growth of
Lp(fα,σ ) is exactly vp(α). The only case where this is known is when
ap = 0 (and p ∤N), in which case both L±
p (fα,σ ) and L±
p (fβ,σ ) have the
expected order (k− 1)/2 (see [83]). Also, it is known ( loc. cit. ) and due
to Mazur that at least one of the functions L±
p (fα,σ ) and L±
p (fβ,σ ) has
positive order.
(iii) We know little on the values of Lp at characters σ not of the form χzk
for 0≤j≤k, or for that matter on the derivatives of Lp at characters of
that form. There are important conjectures predicting those values, and
relating it to arithmetic invariants of f.
(iv) Fix an M prime to p. One can extend the p-adic L-function of fα form
a function on W to a function on WM (with essentially no change in the
===== PDF PAGE 191 =====
V.8. NOTES AND REFERENCES 191
proof). The formulas are essentially the same, excepted that now χ is
interpreted as a Dirichlet character of conductor pnM (and accordingly
the pn in the formula has to be replaced by pnM), and that the p-adic
multiplier ep(f,α,χ,j ) is deﬁned by a slightly more complicated formula.
See [77] for the precise formulas.
(v) The question of whether the p-adic multiplier ep(f,α,χ,j ) is 0, and what
consequences this vanishing has when it occurs, is discussed extensively in
[77].
(vi) There is an other completely independent way to construct the p-adic L-
function of a modular form: it is by using Perrin-Riou’s method applied
to Kato’s Euler system. We shall not discuss this point view here. See
[37] and [38] and the references there for more information.
(vii) The p-adic L-functions we constructed are never 0. This is easy to see
when the formg has weightk+2 > 2, since then the interpolation property
gives, forχ a non-trivial Dirichlet characterL±
p (g,χzk) =CL(g,χ−1,k +1)
where C is a non-zero constant. If k + 2> 2, that is k >0, then k + 1≥
k/2+3/2 sok +1 is either in the interior or on the boundary (when k = 1,
i.e. in weight 3) of the domain of convergence of the Dirichlet Series of
L(g,χ−1,s ). In both case, it is known that this functions does not vanish
at k + 1, because of the Euler product description in the ﬁrst case, of
the Jacquet-Shalika generalization of the theorem of Hadamard and De
La Vall´ ee-Poussin in the second case. Proving that thep-adic L-function
does not vanish for a form of weight 2 is harder, and requires a diﬃcult
theorem of Rohrlich.
(viii) When vp(α) =k + 1, we can still construct a p-adic L-function satisfying
(i) and (ii) by choosing for Φ ±
g any lift of φ±
g/Ω±
g , but then the construc-
tion is not canonical, and (i) and (ii) does not determine uniquely the
p-adic L-function. It is not possible to remediate this lack of unique-
ness (there deﬁnitely are several functions satisfying (i) and (ii)), but it is
possible to modify the construction so as to get a canonical, well-deﬁned
p-adic L-function, and to state properties that characterize it uniquely.
See Chapter VII.
V.8. Notes and references
Classical modular symbols appear in [ 72], in connection with the study of the
arithmetic properties of the special values of a modular forms and with the con-
struction of its p-adic L-function. They are studied systematically in [ 100].

Part 3
The eigencurve and its p-adic
L-functions

CHAPTER VI
The eigencurve of modular symbols
In Stevens’s overconvergent modular symbols can replace Coleman’s overcon-
vergent modular forms in the construction of the eigencurve. This was outlined by
Stevens himself in a talk in Paris in 2000, but he never publicly released his per-
sonal notes on the subject. A very quick sketch of his argument is given in [ 65] or
in [82] (a few lines in each cases) and a detailed construction of the local pieces of
Stevens’s eigencurve is to be found in [5] of which we give in§VI.1 a global version.
We then (§VI.2) use Chenevier’s comparison theorem (see §II.8) to prove that this
eigencurve is essentially the same as the Coleman-Mazur eigencurve. Nevertheless,
the construction with modular symbols is essential to our purpose of construct-
ing and studying p-adic L-functions in family, as it provides over the eigencurve a
natural module of distribution-valued modular symbols which is closely related to
L-functions. In the next section VI.3, we oﬀer a detailed discussion of the points
of the eigencurve that correspond to classical modular forms. Those points, and
their neighborhoods in the eigencurve, are the main objects of interest for arith-
metical applications. Section VI.4 introduces and studies one of the last remaining
important characters of our intrigue, the family of Galois representations that the
eigencurve carry. Finally, using this family and Galois cohomology arguments, we
study the local geometry of the eigencurve at classical points, focussing on questions
of smoothness and ´ etaleness over the weight space.
VI.1. Construction of the eigencurve using rigid analytic modular
symbols
From now on, we will ﬁx an integer N, a prime p not dividing N, and we sate
Γ = Γ1(N)∩ Γ0(p).
We will construct in this section VI.1 the p-adic eigencurve of tame level N.
We call H0 the commutative ring generated by symbols called Tl, for l not
dividing Np, Up, and ⟨a⟩ for a∈ (Z/NZ)∗. Thus H0 is the ring called H0 or
H0(N) we introduced in §I.6.4 and used in the preceding chapters, except that
the symbol Tp is now written Up. (We could actually work with the full Hecke
algebraH, including theUl’s forl ∤Np, this would lead to a diﬀerent, non-reduced,
eigencurve.)

From now on we shall make the following assumption on R:
Hypothesis VI.1.3. There is a multiplicative element π in R which has the
highest norm < 1, and if R0 ={x∈R,|x|≤ 1}, then ˜R =R0/πR0 is a PID.
We note that this hypothesis is satisﬁed for instance when R is a Tate algebra

Lemma VI.1.5. Assume that R satisﬁes VI.1.3. Let M be any potentially or-
thonormalizable Banach R-module, and N be any closed sub-module of M. Then
N is potentially orthonormalizable.
Proof — Note that our assumption implies that R satisﬁes condition II.1.11.
Changing the norm ofM we may assume thatM is orthonormalizable, in particular
|M|⊂| R|. Let M0 andN0 be the closed unit balls inM andN. We haveM0⊂N0,
andπM0∩N0 =πN0, since being in the left hand side means being inN and having
norm < 1, which is the same sing as being in πN0. Hence we have an inclusion
of ˜R-modules N0/πN0 ↪→ M0/πM0. By Lemma II.1.12, M0/πM0 is free, and so
N0/πN0 is free too because a sub-module of a free module over a PID is free, and
N is orthonormalizable by Lemma II.1.12 again. □

Remark VI.1.13. I do not know if µ∗
V is strict when V is the Banach space
Dk[r](Qp). Of course, it is the case if s> 0 because then µ∗

but for a torsion-free Γ, one has s = 0 unfortunately. When s = 0, the fact that
∆ :Dk[r](Qp)→D k[r](Qp) is not strict suggests that µ∗

This explains the indirectness of the proof of Theorem VI.1.7. Indeed, while
this theorem is concerned with modular symbols valued in the Banach modules
Dk[r](R) andDk[r](R′), we are forced to work with their Fr´ echet cousinsD†
k[r](R)
andD†

pletion commutes with taking the kernel of a continuous morphism of Banach or
Fr´ echet space, when the morphism is not supposed to be strict (see Exercise VI.1.14
below). Otherwise, the theorem would be proved exactly as Lemma III.1.5.

VI.1.4. Construction. We ﬁx as above Γ = Γ 1(N)∩ Γ0(p) (with p ∤N).
We are ready to apply the eigenvariety machine in order to construct thep-adic
eigencurve of modular symbols (of tame level N). Actually we shall not construct
only one eigencurve, but two, denoted C+,C−. To unify the two construction, let
± mean either + or −.
We describe the eigenvariety data that will feed the machine.
(ED1) For the Hecke ring we take the ring H0 generated by symbols Tl (l ∤Np),
Up and the diamond operators ⟨a⟩ for a∈ (Z/pZ)∗.
(ED2) For W we take the usual weight space deﬁned in §V.3. For C we take
the following admissible covering: each connected component of W is iso-
morphic, after the choice of a generator of 1 + qZp, to the open ball of
center 1 and radius 1: we deﬁne C as the set of closed balls (in those open
balls) of center an integer and radius ρ∈pQ, ρ< 1. Note that if W∈ C,
W = SpR, then R satisﬁes Hypothesis VI.1.3.
(ED3) If W = SpR is in C, we choose an r such that 0 <r< max(r(W ),p )
MW := Symb±
Γ (DK[r](R)).
Note that MW is a direct summand of Symb ±
Γ (DK[r](R)) hence satis-
ﬁes property (Pr) by Lemma VI.1.4. We have a natural action of the
Hecke operators ψ :H0→ EndR(MW ). The choice of r does not mat-
ter since Symb±
Γ (DK[r](R)) and Symb±
Γ (DK[r′](R)) are linked by Theo-
rem V.5.6(iii).
We check the conditions:
(ED1) The action of Up on MW is compact by Theorem V.5.6(i).
(ED2) If W′ = SpR′⊂W = SpR, the module MW ˆ⊗RR′ andMW′ are linked by
the restriction theorem (Theorem VI.1.7).
===== PDF PAGE 205 =====
VI.2. COMPARISON WITH THE COLEMAN-MAZUR EIGENCURVE 205
Hence the eigenvariety machine provides us with an eigenvariety C±, which is
an equidimensional of dimension 1, separated, rigid analytic space over Qp with
(i) A rigid Qp-analytic map κ :C±→W , which is locally ﬁnite and ﬂat.
(ii) Analytic functions Tl (for l ∤ N), Up and⟨a⟩ onC± (that is, the analytic
function Tl is deﬁned as the image of Tl ∈ H0 by the map ψ :H0 →
O(C±)).
Note that the ﬂatness of κ does not follow from general properties of eigenvariety,
but follows in our case from the fact for W = SpecR in C, R is PID, hence any
torsion free module is ﬂat over R, in particular the algebra TW,ν, so κ :CW,ν→W .
Since ﬂat is a local property, the ﬂatness of κ follows.

bounded.

VI.2. Comparison with the Coleman-Mazur eigencurve
We keep the same notation and hypotheses as above. We have constructed two
eigencurvesC+ andC− with modular symbols and we want to compare them with
the classical eigencurves of Coleman-Mazur and BuzzardC (the full eigencurve) and
C0 (the cuspidal eigencurve). For this we need to recall how these ﬁrst eigencurves
were constructed,
VI.2.1. The Coleman-Mazur full eigencurveC and the cuspidal eigen-
curveC0. Those eigencurves are constructed with the same data (ED1), that is
the ringH0, and (ED2) that is the same weight space W, but with diﬀerent data
(ED3): for W = Sp R a suitable aﬃnoid of the weight space, and for r > 0 a
suﬃciently small real number (w.r.t toW ), Coleman deﬁnes a BanachR-module of
overconvergent modular forms (resp. cuspidal modular forms) M†[r](Γ,R ) (resp.
S†[r](Γ,R )), with action of H0, on which Up acts compactly. Those modules are
linked for variousr and their formations commute with restriction of aﬃnoids. We
have natural evaluation at the cusp ∞ liner maps M†[r](Γ,R )→ R, f↦→ f(∞),
and
(VI.2.1) S†[r](R) ={f∈M†[r](R),f|γ(∞) = 0, ∀γ∈ Γ0(p)}

Remark VI.2.2. As we saw in the proof, the spaces of classical modular sym-
bols SymbΓ(Vk(L)) tend to be semi-simple as H0-module, except perhaps for the
operator Up on the subspace of old symbols of slope ( k + 1)/2 – and even this
exception is conjectured not to happen (and known not to happen when k = 2).
In contrast, the space of overconvergent modular symbols Symb±
Γ (Dk(L))≤ν are
in general not semi-simple as H0-module (except of course if ν < k+ 1 since in
this case they are isomorphic to submodules of the module of classical modular
symbols). This non semi-simplicity happens already for ν =k + 1, as we shall see.
Similar remarks can be made with modular symbols replaced by modular forms.
It is therefore a natural question whether we can remove the semi-simpliﬁcation
in (ii) of the theorem, that is roughly speaking if spaces of overconvergent modular
symbols and corresponding modules of over convergent modular forms are isomor-
phic asH0-module, and not only after semi-simpliﬁcation. This question is open
and I have personally no opinion whether the answer is yes or no. (Fabrizio An-
dreatta, reporting on a joint work with Glenn Stevens and Adrian Iovita in Goa
in August 2010 stated this question as a conjecture). We shall see below that this
question has a positive answer at most classical points (of slope k + 1, otherwise
this is trivial).

in other words a system of eigenvalues of H0.

of Ecrit
2 ).
Proof — This is just Theorem II.7.1 using (ii) of Theorem VI.2.1. □
VI.3.2. Very classical points.
Definition VI.3.2. A point x inC± of ﬁeld of deﬁnition L(x) is called very
classical2 of weight k∈ N if κ(x) = k and the system of eigenvalues λx appears in
Symb±
Γ (Vk(L(x))).
We observe that for the classical structure on C± introduced during the proof
of (i) of Theorem VI.2.1, the classical points in the sense of §II.8.1 are the points
we call now very classical. Hence, by Prop. II.8.6:
Proposition VI.3.3. The set of very classical points in C± is very Zariski-
dense.

Lemma VI.3.4. If x is a p-new very classical point with κ(x) = k∈ Z then
vp(Up(x)) =k/2.
2French tr` es classique. This terminology seems to be due to Chenevier.

Proof — By Theorem IV.3.35, the system of eigenvalues λx appear in Mk+2(Γ,L )
but not in Mk+2(Γ1(N),L ). This system is either cuspidal or Eisenstein. If it is
cuspidal, the result folllows from [ 79, Theorem 4.6.17/2]. If it is Eisenstein, then
according to the classiﬁcation of new Eisenstein series (see §I.6.4) the system has
to be the one of the exceptional Eisenstein series E2,p (since the p-valuation of the
level of normal new Eisenstein form Ek,ψ,τ with trivial nebentypus at a prime p is
always even, because the level of the form is the product of the conductors of the
two primitive Dirichlet characters ψ and τ, while the nebentypus is ψτ .) In this
case, we have vp(Up(x)) = 0 (exceptional new Eisenstein series are ordinary), and
k = 0, hence the result. □

lemma ensures that condition (CSC) is still satisﬁed. □
VI.3.3. Classical points.
Definition VI.3.6. Let L be a complete ﬁeld containing Qp. An L-point x in
C±(L) is classical if the system of eigenvalues λx appears in Symb±
Γ1(M)(Vk(L)) for
some k∈ N, M∈ N, M≥ 1.
It amounts to the same to requiring that the system of eigenvalues λx appears
in the space of modular forms Mk+2(Γ1(M),L ). It is not hard to see, using The-
orem IV.3.35 and the Hecke estimate on coeﬃcients of modular forms, that the
integer k is uniquely determined by x. We shall call it the weight of the classical
point x, but not without warning the reader that it is not always true, as we shall
see, thatκ(x) =k. Also, as for very classical points, we deﬁne the minimal levelM0
ofx as the minimal level of the system of eigenvaluesλp
x ofHp
0 in Symb±
Γ1(M)(Vk(L)).
WritingM0 = N0pt0 with p ∤ N0, we call N0 the minimal tame level and pt0 the
minimal wild level .
While the very classical points appear on the eigencurve, in a sense, by con-
struction, it is not clear a priori that there are any classical points on the eigencurve
beyond the very classical points. The aim of the next § is to produce a good supply
of classical points. Later, with help of the Galois representations, we shall prove
that this supply together with the very classical points exhausts all classical points.

Remark VI.3.13. The reader should note that for a Hida classical point x of
weightk and levelNpt, one does not haveκ(x) =k. Instead, κ(x) is the character
z↦→zkϵ(z), where ϵ is a primitive character of conductor pt. In particular, a Hida
classical point is not very classical.
Proposition VI.3.14. Let x∈C (L) be a point such that κ(x) is of the form
z↦→ zkϵ(z) with ϵ a ﬁnite order character, and assume that vp(Up(x)) < k+ 1.
Then x is either a very classical point or a Hida classical point. In particular, it is
a classical point.
Proof — Ifϵ = 1, then we already know thatx is very classical by Theorem V.5.18.
If ϵ̸= 1, it is Hida classical by Theorem. VI.3.11. □
Remark VI.3.15. At this stage, it is not clear that every classical point is either
a Hida classical point or a very classical point. We don’t even know that there are
no classical points whose minimal tame level is not a divisor of N, or such that
κ(x) is not of the form z↦→zkϵ(z) for k∈ N and ϵ a ﬁnite order character. With
the help of the family of Galois representations carried by the eigencurve, it will be
easy to prove these results: see Theorem VI.4.6 below.

of descent of representations, since tr ρx,L′(GQ,Np)⊂ L, there exists an L-algebra
D of dimension 4, with center L, satisfying D⊗L′≃ M2(L′) (hence deﬁning a

contradicting trρx(c) = 0. Hence D = M2(L), and ρx : GQ,Np → GL2(L) is the
continuous representation we were looking for.
Ifρx,L′ is absolutely reducible, then up to replacing L′ by a ﬁnite extension, we
may assume that ρx.L′ is the sum of two characters. In particular ρx,L′(GQ,Np) is

Proposition VI.4.4. Letf be a modular form, eigenform for H0, and assume
f is not an exceptional Eisenstein series. If N0pt (with N0 prime to p, t≥ 0) is
the minimal level of f, one has N(ρf) =N0.
Proof — Letf′ be the new form attached tof, sof′ is of levelN0pt. For a cuspidal
formf′, the result is a famous theorem of Carayol (cf. [24] and [71, Lemma 4.1]). If
f =Ek+2,τ,ψ withτ,ψ primitive of conductorQ andR, and minimal level QR, the
Galois representationρf isτ⊕ψωk+1
p soN(ρf) is the prime-to-p part ofQR =N0pt.
□
Corollary VI.4.5. If x is a point of the eigencurve C±, the tame conductor
N(ρx) of ρx divides N.
Proof — This follows from the preceding proposition and Lemma VI.4.3. □
Theorem VI.4.6. The only classical points on C± are the very classical points
and the Hida classical points.
Proof — If x is a classical point of weight k and minimal level N0pt with N0 not
divisible by p, then by deﬁnition ρx is the representation attached to the system
of eigenvalues λx which appears in Symb Γ1(N0pt)(Vk(L)) and is new. Since the
diamond operators ⟨a⟩ for a∈ (Z/ptZ)∗ commutes with H0, λx must appear in
SymbΓ1(N0pt)(Vk(L))[ϵ] for some character ϵ of (Z/ptZ)∗. Hence the restriction to
Z∗
p of the determinant of ρx isz↦→zk+1ϵ(z). By Cor. VI.4.2, κ(x)(z) =zkϵ(z), and
by Cor. VI.4.5, N0|N.
Now we distinguish two cases: the ﬁrst case is when t = 0 or t = 1, and the
characterϵ is trivial. In this case λx appears in Symb Γ0(p)∩Γ1(N0)(Vk(L)), hence in
SymbΓ(Vk(L)) and we have κ(x) = k by the above paragraph. Hence x is a very
classical point (p-new if t = 1 and p-old if t = 0). The second case is when t≥ 2
or ϵ is non-trivial. Since t = 0 implies obviously ϵ = 1, this means either that
t = 1 and ϵ non-trivial, or t≥ 2. Note that in both case, ϵ is primitive: for t = 1,
any non-trivial character if ( Z/ptZ)∗ is primitive, and for t >1, this follows from
Lemma VI.3.7(ii). Summarizing, λx appears in Symb Γ1(Npt)(Vk(L))[ϵ] and κ(x) is
the character z↦→zkϵ(z) with ϵ primitive: this is exactly the deﬁnition of x being
Hida-classical. □
Definition VI.4.7. Let x be a classical point on the eigencurve C±. We shall
say that x is normal if we have both:
(N1) The system of eigenvalues λx,0 ofH0 is not the system of eigenvalues of
E2.

(N2) The system of eigenvalues λx,0 ofH0 is not the system of eigenvalues of a
new Eisenstein series E2,ψ,τ of weight 2 with τ and ψ primitive Dirichlet
characters of conductor Q and R, and there is a prime factor l of N such
that such that τ|GQl
=ψ|GQl
.
Note that all cuspidal classical points, and all classical points corresponding to
an Eisenstein series of weight > 2, or of weight 2 but with square-free level, are
normal.
Lemma VI.4.8. Letx be classical point on the eigencurve of tame minimal level
N0. If x is normal, then in a suﬃciently small neighborhood of x all classical points
have minimal level N0.
Proof — If x is normal, there exists by Atkin-Lehner’s theory a new form f of
tame levelN0 whose system of eigenvalues forH0 isλx, andρx =ρf. For l a prime
diﬀerent from p, we need to show that nl(ρy) is constant on a neighborhood of x.
There is nothing to prove forl ∤N. Forl|N, the result follows from Lemma VI.4.3,
excepted when (ρf)|GQl
is 1⊕ωp up to an unramiﬁed twist (and in particular, ρf
is unramiﬁed at ℓ andρf(Frobℓ) has two eigenvaluesα andαl.) But we claim that
that this cannot happen if f is normal. If f is cuspidal, and ρf unramiﬁed at l,
then the two eigenvalues ofρf(Frobℓ) are known to have the same complex absolute
value, which is not the case here. If f is Eisenstein, then (ρf)|GQl
is 1⊕ωp up to an
unramiﬁed twist only if f is exceptional, excluded by (N1), or if f = E2,τ,ψ, with
τ|GQl
=ψ|GQl
and those two characters are unramiﬁed, excluded by (N2). □
VI.4.3. Local properties at p of the family of Galois representations.
In this subsection and in the rest of the chapter, we suppose the reader somewhat
familiar with p-adic Hodge theory, or at least to know the basic properties of Sen
weights and of the functor Dcrys. A good introduction to this subject can be found
in the notes of the Clay Hawaii Summer School, in particular [ 18] and [8].
Lemma VI.4.9. Forx∈C±, the Hodge-Tate-Sen weights of (ρx)|GQp are 0 and
−dκ(x)− 1, where dκ(x) is the derivative at 1 of the character κ(x) : Z∗
p→L∗.
Proof — Ifx is very classical andκ(x) =k∈ N, the Galois representationρx is the
one attached to a modular form of weight k + 2, hence the Hodge-Tate weights of
(ρx)|GQp are (according to our normalization) 0 and−k−1, that is 0 and−dκ(x)+1
since κ(x) :z↦→zk has derivativek at 1. □
Lemma VI.4.10. For x∈C ±, the space Dcrys((ρx)|GQp)φ=Up(x) has dimension
at least 1 over L.
===== PDF PAGE 219 =====

Proof — If x is very classical and p-old, then ( ρx)|GQp is crystalline and its
φ-eigenvalues are Up(x) and pk+1Up(x)−1. Then the result follows from Kisin’s
theorem ([66], [10, Theorem 3.3.3(i)]). □

VI.5. The ordinary locus
Definition VI.5.1. The ordinary locusC±
ord of the eigencurve C± is the locus
of points x such that vp(Up(x)) = 0.
Proposition VI.5.2. The ordinary locusC±
ord is a union ofof connected compo-
nents ofC±. In particular, it is equidimensional of dimension 1. The very classical
points are very Zariski-dense in it. The restriction κ :C±
ord→W of the weight map
κ is ﬁnite.
Proof — Recall that Up∈O (C±) is bounded by 1, and that the ball of functions
bounded by 1 in O(C±) is compact (Prop. II.7.9). Thus every subsequence of the
sequence Un!
p has a converging subsequence, and all those converging subsequence
have the same limit since these limits obviously assume the value 1 on x such that
|Up(x)| = 1 and 0 elsewhere. It follows that e = limn→∞Un!
p exists in O(C) and
take the value 1 onC±
ord, 0 elsewhere. In particular e is an idempotent inO(C±), and
C±
ord is deﬁned by e = 1, hence is a union of connected components. The assertion
about density follows. For the ﬁniteness, see [ 26, Prop. 6.11]. □
Remark VI.5.3. Well before the eigencurve was constructed, Hida has deﬁned
the so-called Hida-family, as some eigenalgebras over the Iwasawa algebra Λ of
function bounded by 1 in O(W). One can show that C±
ord is the base change to W
of the corresponding Hida family.
Here we see the trade-oﬀ between Hida families and eigenvarieties. Hida families
only contains the ordinary modular forms, but they are deﬁned over the Iwasawa
algebra, which allows ones to reason modulo p when we need to.
Lemma VI.5.4. Ifx is a very classical point of weight k such that vp(Up(x)) = 0
or vp(Up(x)) = k + 1, then (ρx)|GQp is an extension of an unramiﬁed character χ1
by an Hodge-Tate character χ2 of weight−k− 1.
Proof — In both cases, x is old, and corresponds to a reﬁnement of an ordinary
modular form. The result is thus known by a deep result of Hida and Wiles, cf.
[109]. □

VI.6. Local geometry of the eigencurve
VI.6.1. Clean neighborhoods. Letx be a point of the eigencurveC± of ﬁeld
of deﬁnition L. We want to study the geometry of the eigencurve near x. In order
to avoid the minor but annoying complications related to rationality questions,
we shall make a base change to L. That is, we consider in this section both the
eigencurve and the weight space as rigid spaces over L instead of Qp (without
changing notations). In particular, all aﬃnoids of C± andW will be L-aﬃnoids
without further notice. Let us call w =κ(x), which is a closed point of W of ﬁeld
of deﬁnition L. After we make this base change, saying that κ is ´ etale atx is
the same as saying that it is an isomorphism on its image over any small enough
neighborhood of x.
Lemma and Definition VI.6.1. There exists a basis of aﬃnoid admissible
neighborhoods U of x∈C± such that
(a) There exist an admissible open aﬃnoid W = Sp R ofW (so R is an
aﬃnoidL-algebra), andν∈ R adapted toW , such thatU is the connected
component of x inC±
W,ν.
(b) The point x is the only point in U abovew.
(c) The map κ :U→W is ´ etale at every point ofU except perhaps x.
We shall call such a neighborhood a clean neighborhood of x.
Proof — By construction, the C±
W,ν are a basis of aﬃnoid neighborhoods of x in
C±. Fix such a neighborhood. Choose two admissible open disjoint subsets U1 and
U2 ofC±
W,ν such that U1 containsx andU2 contains every points above w excepted
x (this is possible since κ−1(w)∩C±
W,ν is a ﬁnite set). Since κ :C±
W,ν→W is ﬁnite
ﬂat, the κ−1(W′) for w∈W′⊂W form a basis of neighborhoods of κ−1(w) ([15,
2.1.6]), so it is possible to ﬁnd a W′∈ C such that κ−1(W′)⊂ U1∪U2. For such
a W′, the connected component U of x inC±
W′ν = κ−1(W′) is contained in U1,
hence contains no point in κ−1(w) other than x. By the openness (already for the
Zariski topology) of the ´ etale locus of a ﬁnite ﬂat map, ifW′ is small enough then
κ :U→W′ is ´ etale excepted perhaps atx. □

VI.6.2. ´Etaleness of the eigencurve at non-critical slope classical points.
Theorem VI.6.4. Letx be a normal classical point onC± of weight k, and such
that Up(x)2̸=pk+1⟨p⟩(x) If x is of non-critical slope, that is if vp(Up(x))<k + 1,
then κ is ´ etale atx. In particular, C± is smooth at x.
Proof — We choose a clean neighborhood on x and keep all the notations used
above. Let N0 be the minimal tame level of x and write w = κ(x). We have
d = dimMw = dim Symb±
Γ (Dw)(x) The latter is, by Stevens’ control theorem (us-
ing that x is of non-critical slope), dim Symb ±
Γ (Vw)(x). Hence, d = σ(N/N0) by
Lemma VI.6.3. But by Lemma VI.6.2, d =eσ(N/N0). Hence e = 1. □
===== PDF PAGE 224 =====
224 VI. THE EIGENCURVE OF MODULAR SYMBOLS
Remark VI.6.5. In this theorem, vp(Up(x))<k + 1 is the serious hypothesis,
while x normal and Up(x)2̸=pk+1⟨p⟩(x) are the technical hypotheses. We discuss
here whether those technical hypotheses are really necessary.
The hypothesis Up(x)2̸= pk+1⟨p⟩(x) when x is very classical is necessary be-
cause when Up(x)2 = pk+1⟨p⟩(x), the eigenalgebra deﬁned by the action of H on
Mw is non semi-simple (cf. Exercise I.6.20), hence κ is not ´ etale atx. On the other
hand, it is conjectured, as already noted, that Up(x)2 =pk+1⟨p⟩(x) never happens
for a classical point, and this is known for k = 0.
About the hypothesis that x is normal, there is one noteworthy abnormal case
where the conclusion of Theorem VI.6.4 still holds and can be proved with only a
slight modiﬁcation of the proof: it is when N = 1 and x is the point corresponding
to the ordinary Eisenstein series E2,p (actually, this point lies on the ordinary
Eisenstein line in the eigencurve of tame level 1, which is isomorphic to the weight
spaceW through the weight map κ.) I do not know if the eigencurve is ´ etale at
the other abnormal classical points x of non-critical slope, but it is unlikely.
Remark VI.6.6. The same argument using Coleman’s control theorem instead
of Stevens’s shows that the eigencurve of modular forms C as well is smooth at x.
As a consequence, the injection C± ↪→C is an isomorphism in some neighborhood
of x.

Definition VI.6.8. We say that a point x onC± with κ(x) = k∈ N has a
companion point y if there exists a point y∈C ±(−1)k+1
such that κ(y) =−2−k,
Tl(y) = l−k−1Tl(x) for all l ∤ N, Up(y) = p−k−1Up(x) and ⟨a⟩(y) = ⟨a⟩(x) for
a∈ (Z/NZ)∗. The point y, if it exists, is necessarily unique and is called the
companion of x.
Remark VI.6.9. Our deﬁnition of a companion is slightly diﬀerent from the
standard one, which would be phased exactly the same but for C± andC±(−1)k+1
both replaced by the full Coleman-Mazur-Buzzard eigencurve C. Our deﬁnition
is clearly more natural in our context of modular symbols, the requirement that
===== PDF PAGE 226 =====
226 VI. THE EIGENCURVE OF MODULAR SYMBOLS
the companion lies on the eigencurve of sign ±(−1)k+1 being the counterpart for
the ι-involution of the twist by l−k−1 for the Tl-operators. But we claim that our
deﬁnition is the right one even beyond the context of modular symbols, because
it removes the diﬀerence of behavior with respect to the existence of a companion
that exist between cuspidal form and Eisenstein series with the standard deﬁnition,
cf. for instance [ 30,§7].
Theorem VI.6.10. Letx be a very classical point onC± of weightk. We assume

than H1

Remark VI.6.12. Excepted for the restriction to forms that are reﬁnement of
forms of level prime to p, the equivalence between (c) and (d) above is the famous
result of Breuil and Emerton [ 17] (which was before a conjecture of Gross) on the
equivalence between having a companion form and having a Galois representations
that splits at p. The proof we give here is completely diﬀerent (and arguably, more
elementary) than the one given in [ 17]. This proof is due to John Bergdall, see
[14].
Proof — We write byN0 the minimal tame level of x. It is also the minimal level
of x since as we have already observed, a critical point x is p-old.
Equivalence between (a) and (b): Using notations and results of §VI.6.1,
we have d = eσ(N/N0), dim Symb±

its source and target are the same, that is if and only if d = σ(N/N0), which is

Proof that (d) implies (c) when x is cuspidal: Assume non-(c), so x has
a companion point y. Since Up(y) =p−k−1Up(x), we have vp(Up(y)) = 0, so y is in
