Proof of Theorem VI.6.11 when x is cuspidal: Since x is cuspidal, the
Galois representation ρx of GQ,Np is absolutely irreducible. Consider the pseu-
dorepresentation (τT,δ T) : GQ,Np→ T, where T is the completed local ring of the
eigencurve of modular formC at the point x, and where τT ,δT are the composition
ofτ andδ with the restriction mapO(C)→ T. By Theorem I.6.23, τT andδT are the
trace and determinant of a unique continuous representation ρ :GQ,Np→ GL2(T)
whose residual representation is ρx.
We consider the following deformation problem: for all Artinian local algebra
A with residue ﬁeld L, we deﬁne D(A) as the set of strict isomorphism classes of
representationsρA :GQ→ GL2(A) deforming ρx such that
(i) The restriction (ρA)|GQp has a constant weight equal to 0;
(ii) the A-module Dcrys((ρA)|GQp)ϕ=˜β is free of rank one for some ˜β lifting β;
(iii) for l|N, the restriction (ρA)|Il to the inertia subgroup at l is constant.
By [66, Prop. 8.7] (using the fact that β̸=α and that 0̸=k + 1), and [10, Prop.
7.6.3(i)] (for condition (iii)), we see that D is pro-representable, say by a complete
Noetherian local ring R.
We claim that ρ⊗ T/I is, for all coﬁnite length ideal I in T, an element of
D(T/I): property (i) follows from Sen’s theory, see e.g. [ 10, Lemma 4.3.3(i)];
property (ii) is Kisin’s theorem (see [ 66] or [ 10,§3.3.3]). For property (iii), let
l| N. Let Nx and N gen
s(x) be the special monodromy operator of ρ|Il at x and the
generic monodromy operator of T|Il at a component s(x) of the eigencurve through
x (cf. [ 10, deﬁnition 7.8.16]). To prove (iii), by [ 10, Prop. 7.8.19 and Lemma
7.8.17] it is suﬃcient to prove that Nx∼N gen
s(x) (see [10,§7.8.1] for the deﬁnition of
the equivalence relation ∼ and the pre-order ≺ on the set of nilpotent matrices).
Assume ﬁrst that the cuspidal modular form f attached to x is not special at l, so

Thus, ρ deﬁnes a morphism of algebras R→ T. A standard argument shows
that this morphism is surjective. Since T has Krull dimension 1, if we prove that
the tangent space of R has dimension at most 1, it would follow that the map
R→ T is an isomorphism and that R is a regular ring of dimension 1. This would
prove thatC is smooth at x.

Now since x∈C ± andC± is equidimensional of dimension 1, it follows that
the inclusionC± ↪→C is a local isomorphism near x, and therefore that C± is also
smooth at x.
Proof that (a) implies (d) when x is cuspidal: Assume non-(d), that is
(ρx)|GQp is the sum of two characters. Then as we have seen in the above proof,
any deformation in D(L[ε]) has a constant Sen weight k + 1. Since R = T, this
means that one Sen weight of the restriction to any tangent vector at x ofC± is
constantk + 1. In other words, the tangent map of κ maps any tangent vector at
x to 0, that is, κ is not ´ etale atx, which is non-(a).
We have thus ﬁnished the proof of both theorems when x is cuspidal. When
x is Eisenstein, it remains to prove that (d) is equivalent to (a), (b), and (c), and
thatC± is smooth at x. With our hypotheses that x is normal, the proof is a
straightforward generalization of [ 9]. We quickly remind the main steps for the
convenience of the reader.

VI.6.4. Critical slope eigenforms and points on C±.
Proposition VI.6.13. Letg be modular form, of weightk+2, level Γ = Γ1(N)∩
Γ0(p), which is new at N. We assume that g is an eigenform of H0. Then the
unique point xg ofC corresponding to g (Theorem VI.3.1) belong to both C+ and
C−. If g is cuspidal, then xg is a very classical point of both C+ andC−. If g is
Eisenstein, then xg is a very classical point of Cϵ(g) (where ϵ(g) is the sign of the
Eisenstein series g, deﬁned in IV.3.33) but not of C−ϵ(g).
Before beginning the proof we observe that a form g as in the statement is
necessarily old at p, hence is the reﬁnement g(z) = fβ(z) = f(z)−αf(pz) of a
newform f of level Γ 1(N) which is ordinary at p (that is, its coeﬃcient ap is a
===== PDF PAGE 231 =====
VI.6. LOCAL GEOMETRY OF THE EIGENCURVE 231
p-adic unit). Here α and β are the roots of X2−apX +pk+1ϵ(p) of p-valuation 0
and k + 1 respectively.
Proof — By hypothesis, the system of H0-eigenvalues of g appears in Mk+2(Γ).
By Theorem IV.3.35, ifg is cuspidal it appears in Symb±
Γ (Vk) for both values of the
sign±, while ifg is Eisenstein, it appears in Symbϵ(p)
Γ (Vk) but not in Symb−ϵ(p)
Γ (Vk).
Hence when g is cuspidal, the point xg belongs toC+ andC− and is very classical
in both.
If g is Eisenstein, this implies that xg belongs toCϵ(g) and is very classical in
it, and that xg if it belongs to C−ϵ(g) is not very classical (and by Theorem VI.4.6,
is not classical either.) It thus remains only to prove that xg belong toC−ϵ(g). By
Exercise I.6.14, g∈ S†(Γ), hence xg belongs to the cuspidal eigencurve C0, and
since by Theorem VI.2.1,C0⊂C± inC for both values of the sign±,xg belongs to
bothC+ andC−. □
Corollary VI.6.14. Ifg is as in the proposition, theH0-modules Symb+
Γ (Dk)(x)

is the degree of both weight maps C+→W andC−→W at xg.
Proof — SinceC+ andC− are isomorphic in a neighborhood of xg, and g is very
classical in at least one of these curve, the preceding theorem applies and gives the
corollary. □
We know want to review what is known or conjectured about the local degree
e of the weight map at xg for the diﬀerent type of modular eigenform g as in the
proposition.
There are three cases to consider:
When f is a cuspidal non CM new form of weight k, ordinary at p, it is
conjectured but not known in general, that e = 1, that is that κ is ´ etaleat xg.
Indeed, it is conjectured3, that the restriction atp of the Galois representationρg =
ρfβ, which is reducible singe g is ordinary, is always non-split, which is assertion
(d) of Theorem VI.6.10.
When f is an Eisenstein form , f is always ordinary at p, and it is again
conjectured, as a part of Jansen’s conjecture (cf. [ 68, Question 2, page 349] and
[8,§5.2]) that e = 1, i.e. that κ is ´ etaleat xg. See [ 9] for details.
3This was at ﬁrst a question of Greenberg, now become a folklore conjecture. See [ 52].

When f is cuspidal CM , it has CM by a well-determined imaginary qua-
dratic ﬁeld K (i.e., K is the unique quadratic ﬁeld such that (ρf)|GK is the sum of
two characters) and f is ordinary at p if and only if p splits in K. In this case, it
is known that e≥ 2, in other words, κ is never ´ etaleat xg.
Exercise VI.6.15. Prove this result, as follows.
a.– Prove that (ρf)|GK is the direct sum of two distinct characters, χ and χ′
We use a notation of the proof of Theorem VI.6.10, namely the pseudo-representation

It is also a consequence of Jansen’s conjecture that e = 2 in this case. For more
detail about this case, see [ 6].
VI.6.5. Complements on the geometry of the eigencurve at classical
points. In the preceding subsection, we have focused on the normal very classical
case in order to avoid two many complications of variants. Here we give similar
results concerning the remaining cases, with very rough sketches of proof.
There are two noteworthy abnormal cases where the same (and actually even
stronger) results hold, with some modiﬁcation of the proof:
Proposition VI.6.16. Assume that x is a very classical point of weight κ(x) =
0, of critical slope vp(Up(x)) = 1 , and such that the system of eigenvalues of x
restricted toH0, λx,0 :H0→ L is either the system of Eigenvalues of E2 or the
system of eigenvalues of E2,τ,τ where τ is a non-trivial Dirichlet character (note
that such very classical points x exist on C± if and only if ± = +1 ). Then the
weight map κ is ´ etale atx, and in particular the conclusions of Lemma VI.6.7,
Theorem VI.6.11, and Theorem VI.6.10, hold. Moreover, the assertions (a) to (d)
of Theorem VI.6.10 are true.
Proposition VI.6.17. Assume that the tame level is a prime powerN =lν, and
that x is a very classical point of weight κ(x) = 0, of critical slope vp(Up(x)) = 1.
Assume that the system of eigenvalues of x restricted toH0,λx,0 :H0→L is either
the system of eigenvalues of E2,l or the system of eigenvalues of E2,τ,τ where τ is
a primitive non-trivial Dirichlet character, necessarily of conductor a power of l.
(Note that such very classical points x exists onC± if and only if ± = +1). Then
the conclusions of Lemma VI.6.7, Theorem VI.6.11, and Theorem VI.6.10, hold.
Moreover, the condition (a) to (d) in Theorem VI.6.10 are true.

Proof — In the proof of Lemma VI.6.7, the only thing that changes is the proof
that the space (VI.6.3) has dimension 1. Namely, we now have to prove that
ker
(
H1(GQ,Np,ωp)→H1
/f(GQp,ωp)
)
is of dimension 1. By Kummer theory, this dimension of this space is the rank of
the group of N-units in Q, so is 1 if (and only if) N is a prime power. For the
extensions in the other direction, nothing changes.
In the proof of the theorems, we see that nothing changes until the computation
of the rank ofιB, but the result is the same (that is 1) by the above paragraph. The
computation of the rank of ιC is not changed. Hence the theorems hold. Assertion
(d) of Theorem VI.6.10 hold since a N-unit of Q of inﬁnite order obviously is also
of inﬁnite order when seen in Qp. □
Remark VI.6.18. For the other abnormal very classical critical-slope points,
Lemma VI.6.7 is false, as there are in this case several independent non-isomorphic
extension of ψ byτωp in the category of GQ,Np-representation that are crystalline
atp. I do not know if Theorem VI.6.11 and Theorem VI.6.10 hold in this case, but
it is unlikely. IfC± was smooth at x in this case, then Ribet’s lemma would single
out a canonical non-trivial extension of ψ byτωp as above, and I don’t see why any
of those extensions would deserve such a privilege.
Concerning classical points that are not very classical, one has
Theorem VI.6.19. Letx be a normal classical point. If x is cuspidal, assume
than H1
g (Q, adρx) = 0. Then the eigencurve C± is smooth at x.
However, the details of the proof, as well as the statement of Theorem VI.6.10
needs to be slightly modiﬁed. For instance, the preferred representation should not
be required to be crystalline, but rather to have two crystalline periods after twists
by speciﬁc characters. We leave these changes to the interested reader.
There are also points x∈C ± such that λx is the system of eigenvalues of a
classical modular eigenform f of weight 1. Those points are not classical in our
sense, since their system of eigenvalues are not seen in spaces of classical modular
symbols, but could reasonably be called classical. Concerning these points, one has:
Theorem VI.6.20 (Bella¨ ıche-Dimitrov). Let x∈C ± be a point corresponding
to a classical cuspidal modular eigenform f of weight 1 which is regular at p. Then
C± is smooth at x. MoreoverC± is ´ etale overW at x if and only if f is not a CM
form for a quadratic extension of Q in which p splits.
The proof is completely diﬀerent from the proof of Theorems VI.6.10 and VI.6.19.
It uses transcendence theory in the form of Baker’s theorem on linear independence
of logarithms of algebraic numbers. See [ 12] for details.

234 VI. THE EIGENCURVE OF MODULAR SYMBOLS
VI.7. Notes and References
The construction of the eigencurve given in section VI.1 and its comparison
to the Coleman-Mazur eigencurve VI.2 are based on the author’s paper [ 5], but
extends it from the local to the global case. That is, the global eigencurve is con-
structed here, instead of just the local pieces C±
W,ν as in [5], which requires proving
the delicate restriction theorem (Theorem VI.1.7). As said in the introduction, the
whole idea on the construction of the eigencurve with modular symbols valued in
family of distributions is based on a talk by Glenn Stevens (known to the author
through the grapevine), and an important part of the technical machinery neces-
sary to actually make the construction work comes from the paper by Pollack and
Stevens on modular symbols with value distributions (not families thereof) [ 84],
whose results were exposed and expanded in the preceding chapter (cf. Chapter
V).
The material on points on the eigencurve ( §VI.3) is standard. The idea that
classical point, with a non-trivial nebentypus at p, appear on family interpolating
very classical points, with trivial nebentypus at p, seems eventually due to Hida.
The construction of the Galois representation onC± is due to Chenevier (though it
was known before due to Coleman and Mazur, with a more indirect proof) and its
local study follows small parts of [10]. The study of the geometry of the eigenvariety
is based on work by the author, either alone ([5] and [6]), with Chenevier ([9]), and
with Dimitrov ([12]).
===== PDF PAGE 235 =====
CHAPTER VII
p-adic L-functions on the eigencurve
In Chapter IV, we deﬁned, using the method of Manin, Viˇ sik and Amice-Velu
as reinterpreted by Mazur-Tate-Teitelbaum and Stevens) the p-adic L-function of
a classical cuspidal eigenform of non-critical slope of weight ≥ 2.
In this chapter, using the eigencurve, we extend this construction to most over-
convergent eigenforms, including the classical eigenforms of critical slope (cuspidal
or Eisenstein), as well as (most) classical forms of weight 1. We then prove that
thesep-adicL-function can be put together to deﬁne a family of L-functions on the
eigencurve (sometimes called a 2-variable p-adic L-function.)
VII.1. Good points and p-adic L-functions
In this section, we ﬁx a prime p, an integer N not divisible by p, and a choice
of sign±. We denote by Γ the congruence subgroup Γ 0(p)∩ Γ1(N),H0 the Hecke
algebra generated by the Tl’s for l ∤ Np, Up, and⟨a⟩ for a∈ (Z/NZ)∗. As in the
preceding chapter,C± denotes the p-adic eigencurve of modular symbols of tame
levelN and sign±.
VII.1.1. Good points on the eigencurve.
Lemma VII.1.1. LetL be a ﬁnite extension of Qp, x be a point onC±(L), with
κ(x) =w∈W . The following statements are equivalent:
(i) The dual of generalized eigenspace Symb±

T at x (we use the notations M andT attached to U deﬁned in§VI.6.1).
(iii) For any suﬃciently small clean neighborhood U of x, the module M∨ is
free of rank one over T at x.
Proof — The equivalence between (ii) and (iii) is clear. If U is a clean neighbor-
hood, x is the only point of U above w, so Tx is the ﬁber Tw ofT at w and the

implies that (i) is equivalent to (ii). □

Definition VII.1.2. A point x∈C ± satisfying the conditions of the above
lemma is called a good point.
Ifx is a good point, there exists a clean neighborhood of x where all points are
good (by (iii) of Lemma VII.1.1).

Exercise VII.1.4. Let x be a classical normal good point on C±. Show that the

Theorem VII.1.5. Let x be a point of C± such that the weight w :=κ(x) has
the form w : z↦→ w(z)zkϵ(z) for some k≥ 0 and some ﬁnite order character ϵ of
conductorpt. If x has tame level N andC± is smooth at x, thenC± is a good point.
Moreover, ifOW,w is the local ring ofW± atw,u a uniformizer of this discrete
valuation ring, and OC±,x the local ring of C± at x, then there is an isomorphism
ofOW,w-algebras
OW,w[t]/(te−u) =OC±,x,
where e is the index of ramiﬁcation of κ at x. This isomorphism induces an iso-
morphismTx =L[t]/te.
Proof — Choose a clean neighborhood U ofx, and deﬁne R,M,T as usual. The
localizationOC,x at x is by assumption a PID. By Exercise I.3.2, the localization
of the ﬁnite modules M and M∨ at x are torsion-free overOC,x, hence are locally
free. Hence by shrinking U if necessary we may assume that M∨ is free over T .
The rank of M∨ overR is equal to the rank of M, namely d, while the rank ofT is
e, and by Lemma VI.6.2 and the hypothesis that x has tame level N,d =e. Hence
M∨ is of rank one over T , and hence x is a good point.
The second set of assertions is clear since OC,x/OW,w is an extension of DVRs.
□

VII.2. p-adic L-functions of an overconvergent eigenform
In this section, we ﬁx a prime p, an integerN not divisible byp. Write as usual
Γ = Γ 1(N)∩ Γ0(p). We denote as usual by C andC0 the full and cuspidal p-adic
eigencurve of tame level N.
VII.2.1. Deﬁnition. For L a ﬁnite extension of Qp, and for κ∈W (L) an
element of the weight space, let g∈M†
κ(Γ,L ) be an overconvergentH0-eigenform,
which deﬁnes a point x in the full eigencurveC of Coleman-Mazur.
SinceC =C+∪C−, the point x belongs toC+, orC−, or both. Note that when
x∈C 0, x belong to both C+ andC−.
Definition VII.2.1. For every value of the sign ± such that x∈C± and x is
good inC±, we deﬁne the p-adic L-function of g onW±(Cp),
σ↦→L±
p (g,σ )
as the p-adic L function L±

Thusg may have onep-adicL-function deﬁned on half the weight space, or two
p-adicL-functions deﬁned on both halves of the weight space, each of them is only
well deﬁned up to a scalar in L∗.
VII.2.2. Classical cuspidal eigenforms of non-critical slope.
Proposition VII.2.2. Let g ∈ Sk+2(Γ,L )<k+1 be a classical cuspidal H0-
eigenform of non-critical slope and let x be the point ofC corresponding tog. Then
x belongs toC+ andC− and is a good points of both eigencurve.

atx so in each case, a suﬃciently small clean neighborhood T =R andM∨, which
is free over R is free overT , and x is good.
In the caseλ(Up)2̸=pk+1 =λ(⟨p⟩) (which is conjectured never to happen), then
Sk+2(Γ,L )(g) has dimension 2 but Sk+2(Γ,L )[g] has dimension 1 (Exercise V.7.5).

VII.2.5. Classical eigenforms of weight 1. A classical modular form of
weight 1 corresponds to no classical modular symbol, and the traditional method
to attach to them p-adic L-function fails. However, the eigencurve method allows
to deﬁne a p-adic L-function in this arithmetically important case too.
Let f ∈ S1(Γ1(N), C) be an new form of nebentypus ϵ. Let us call ap the
eigenvalue ofUp. Let us factor X2 +apX +ϵ(p) as (X−α)(X−β). Here α andβ
are both p-adic units, hence of p-adic valuations 0. The reﬁnements fα and fβ are
thus both ordinary and critical slope. We say that f is regular if α̸= β. In this
case we have
Theorem VII.2.6. Let x be the point corresponding of C to fα (or fβ). Then
x belongs toC+ andC− and is a good point there. In particular, we can deﬁne the
p-adic L-function of fα, Lp(fα,σ ) on bothW+ andW−.
Proof — As recalled in Theorem VI.6.20, the smoothness of the point x inC+ and
C− is proved in [12]. The fact that x is a good point follows from Theoram VII.1.5.
□
For generalizations, see for instance [ 16] and [ 48]. For applications, see for
instance [43].

VII.3. The 2-variable p-adic L-function
In this section, given a good point x∈C±, we show that on a suitable aﬃnoid
neighborhood U ofx we can deﬁne a two-variablep-adicL-function, ˜Lp(y,σ ), ana-

U (which is always good):
∀σ∈W±, ˜Lp(y,σ ) =c(y)Lp(y,σ ),
where c(y)∈L(y)∗, L(y) being the ﬁeld of deﬁnition of y.

p (g,σ ) is deﬁned only up to a scalar
in L(y)∗ of absolute value 1. Hence, the absolute value |c(y)| is well-deﬁned. An
important complement of the preceding interpolation property is the fact that fory
running among all such points inU, the normalization constantc(y) stays bounded
above, and below from zero.

VII.4. Notes and References
The ﬁrst “missing” p-adic L-function, i.e. attached to a cuspidal critical-slope
eigenform, was constructed by Pollack and Stevens (see [ 84] and [85]), in the case
of a cuspidal new form f of critical form with no companion point.
Most of the material of the chapter comes from [ 5], the unpublished paper [ 6],
and [11]. The second part of Theorem VII.3.1 is new.

CHAPTER VIII
The adjoint p-adic L-function and the ramiﬁcation
locus of the eigencurve
This chapter is largely inspired by Kim’s 2006 Berkeley thesis [ 65]. Indeed the
core result, namely the construction of a scalar product on the module of families
of modular symbols interpolating the classical scalar product on classical modular
symbols is taken directly from [ 65], with only minor modiﬁcations. However, we
improve on Kim’s treatment by proving an explicit and more general result of
perfectness on this scalar product (than the one implicitly stated in [ 65, Theorem
11.2]), and more importantly by changing the way the adjoint p-adic L-function
is constructed from the scalar product, and the way this scalar product is proved
to be related to the ramiﬁcation locus of the eigencurve. Actually, we propose a
conceptual treatment of those two aspects together in subsection VIII.1 below. It
seems to us that there is an gap in the argument of [ 65] concerning the descent
of the adjoint p-adic L-function to the eigencurve from its normalization that our
method allows to circumvent. At any rate, we obtain more precise results on the
relation between the geometry of the weight map and the adjointp-adicL-function.
Let us now describe in more details these results. As usual, we denote byC0 the
cuspidal p-adic eigencurve on tame level N, and H0 the Hecke algebra generated
by the operators Tl for l ∤Np, Up, and the diamond operators.
We construct, using Kim’s scalar product on modular symbols, a canonical
sheaf of idealsLadj ofO(C0), and we prove (Theorem VIII.4.2) that its closed locus
contains all points ofC0 where the weight map κ is not ´ etale.
We do not know whether the idealLadj is everywhere locally principal, but it is
so in a neighborhood of most points of interest, including all classical points that
are of minimal tame level N (except possibly for a few Eisenstein series of weight 2
whenN is not square-free) and all their companion points when they have one. In
a suitable neighborhood U of such a good point, we can deﬁne the adjoint p-adicL-
function Ladj as a generator of the ideal Ladj, and we can determine exactly where
this function vanishes (see Theorem VIII.4.7):
249
===== PDF PAGE 250 =====
250 VIII. ADJOINT p-ADIC L-FUNCTION AND RAMIFICATION OF THE EIGENCURVE
(i) If x∈ U is a point that either has non-integral weight κ(x)̸∈ N, or is
cuspidal classical1 then Ladj(x) = 0 if and only if the weight map κ is not
´ etale atx.
(ii) Otherwise Ladj(x) = 0.
We observe2 that in case (ii) it may or may not happen that κ is ´ etale atx.
When x is a smooth point of the eigencurve (again this includes almost all
classical points), we can deﬁne two invariants at x: the degree of ramiﬁcation e(x)
of κ at x (so κ is ´ etale atx if and only if e(x) = 1), and the order of vanishing of
Ladj atx. It is natural to ask how these invariants are related : an answer is given
in Theorem VIII.4.8.
Finally we justify the name of the adjoint p-adic L-function Ladj by proving
that at cuspidal classical points of non-critical slope x corresponding to the form
fx, the value Ladj(x) is related to the value of the archimedean adjoint L-function
offx at the near central point. Kim ([ 65, Theorem 12.3]) proves such a formula in
the case fx has wild level pr with r≥ 1 and primitive nebentypus. We extend this
formula to the case of a form of level p and trivial nebentypus, in particular a form
fx which is a reﬁnement of a form of level prime to p
VIII.1. The L-ideal of a scalar product
In all this §, R is a Noetherian domain (of fraction ﬁeld K), and T is a ﬁnite
R-algebra.
VIII.1.1. The Noether diﬀerent of T/R. Letm :T⊗RT→T denote the

Lemma VIII.1.1. As an ideal of T⊗RT the idealI is generated by the elements
of the form t⊗ 1− 1⊗t for t∈T .
Proof — It is clear that t⊗ 1− 1⊗t∈ I. Conversely, if ∑

ktk⊗uk =∑(1⊗uk)(tk⊗ 1− 1⊗tk) which shows the result.
□

VIII.1. THE L-IDEAL OF A SCALAR PRODUCT 251
A simple but important remark is in order. If M is a T⊗RT -module, it has
two natural structures of T -modules, using the two natural morphisms of algebras
T→T⊗RT : t↦→t⊗ 1, t↦→ 1⊗t. On the sub-module M[I] ={m∈M,Im = 0},
the twoT -structures coincide, and in fact, M[I] is the largest sub-module of M on
which the twoT -structures coincide. In particular, we can see M[I] as a T -module
unambiguously.
Definition VIII.1.2 ([80]). The Noether’s diﬀerent dN(T/R) is the ideal of T
which is the image of (T⊗RT )[I] by m.
The Noether’s diﬀerent is also called homological diﬀerent.
The main interest of this notion is in the following theorem (due, it seems,
in this generality to Alexander-Buchsbaum). Recall that for any extension T/R
of algebras, a prime P of T is said unramiﬁed over R if for p = P∩R, we have
pTP = PTP, and TP/PTP is a ﬁnite separable extension of Rp/pRp. It is said
ramiﬁed if it is not unramiﬁed.
Theorem VIII.1.3. A prime ideal P of S is ramiﬁed over R if and only if
dN(T/R)⊂ P. In other words, the closed subset of Spec T deﬁned by the Noether’s
diﬀerent is the ramiﬁcation locus of Spec T→ SpecR.
For the proof, see [4, Theorem 2.7]. Their theorem is even more general, as they
do not actually assume that T/R is ﬁnite, but the much weaker assumption that T
is Noetherian and I is ﬁnitely generated. We shall apply this result in a situation
where T/R is not only ﬁnite but also ﬂat. In this case, P is unramiﬁed over R if
and only if it is ´ etale, and if and only if it is smooth. Hence the closed set of SpecT
deﬁned by the Noether’s diﬀerent is also the set of points where Spec T→ SpecR
is not ´ etale.
There are at least two others notions of diﬀerent. We shall not need them, but
for the sake of completeness, let us recall them quickly:
The oldest notion is Dedekind’s diﬀerent which is deﬁned only under supple-
mentary hypotheses. Namely one assumes that T is reduced, and calling K the
fraction ring of R and L the total fraction ring of T (which is ﬁnite product of
ﬁelds Li, each of them ﬁnite over K), we assume that L is ´ etale overK (that
is, each of the Li is a separable extension of K). In Dedekind’s deﬁnition as in
most treatments of this notion in the literature, it is moreover assumed that R is
Dedekind, but this is not actually necessary. Here is how the Dedekind diﬀerent
is deﬁned: the trace map tr L/K deﬁnes a non-degenerate K-bilinear pairing on L:
(x,y )↦→ tr (xy). Let T∗ be the set of x∈L such that trL/K(xy)∈R for ally inT .
It is clear that T∗ is aT -module, and that it is actually the largest T -submodule of
L such that tr L/K(T∗)⊂R. In particular, since tr L/K(T ) = R, we have T⊂T∗.
===== PDF PAGE 252 =====
252 VIII. ADJOINT p-ADIC L-FUNCTION AND RAMIFICATION OF THE EIGENCURVE
The Dedekind’s diﬀerent dD(T/R) is the ideal [T :T∗] ofT , that is the set of y∈T
such that xT∗⊂ T . This deﬁnition is the one given in all textbooks on algebraic
number theory (where R is invariably assumed to be Dedekind). See e.g. [ 94,
Chapter III] for a fairly complete treatment in this case.
The most modern notion is K¨ ahler’s diﬀerentdK(T/R) which is deﬁned as
the 0-th ﬁtting ideal of the module of diﬀerential forms Ω T/R (cf. [ 63]). More
concretely, we can compute K¨ ahler’s diﬀerent as follows: choosex1,...,x n a family
of generators of T asR-algebra, and let (P1,...,P m) inR[X1,...,X n] the ideals of
their algebraic relations over R. In other words R[X1,...,X n]/(P1,...,P m)≃ T ,
the map sending Xi to xi. Note that we have m≥ n since T is ﬁnite over R by
assumption but that it is not always possible to choose m = n (when it is, T is
said to be complete intersection overR). Form the m×n-Jacobian matrix in R[X]
whose (i,j ) coeﬃcients is ∂Pi
∂Xj
. The K¨ ahler’s diﬀerent is then theT -ideal generated
by the image in T of the (n×n)-minors of that matrix. This is very easy from the
deﬁnitions of the module of diﬀerentials and of the Fitting ideal, both given in [50].
Recall that we always assume thatR is a noetherian domain of fraction ﬁeldK,
and thatT/R is ﬁnite. The following result summarizes the main relations between
those three notions.
Theorem VIII.1.4. When T/R is ﬂat, the ideal dN(T/R) and dK(T/R) deﬁne
the same closed subset of Spec T (that is, have the same radical), namely the set of
points where SpecT→ SpecR is non-´ etale. The same is true if instead of assuming
that T/R is ﬂat, we assume that R is integrally closed, that T is reduced, and that
the total fraction ring L of T is ´ etale overK.
If T/R is ﬂat and the Dedekind’s diﬀerent is deﬁned, it is equal to Noether’s
diﬀerent dD(T/R) = dN(T/R).
If T/R is ﬂat and locally complete intersection, then dN(T/R) = dK(T/R).
Proof — Since the 0-th Fitting ideal of a module deﬁnes the same closed subset
as the annihilator of that module, the closed set deﬁned by the Kahler’s diﬀerent is
the support of ΩT/R which is by deﬁnition the non-´ etale locus of SpecT→ SpecR.
When T/R is ﬁnite and ﬂat, the non-´ etale locus is the same as the ramiﬁcation
locus of SpecT→ SpecR since ´ etale = ﬂat and unramiﬁed. The non-´ etale locus is
also the same as the ramiﬁcation locus in the case we assume that R is integrally
closed that the total fraction ring of T is a product of ﬁnite separable extensions
of Frac(R), for in this case, an unramiﬁed point of Spec T is ﬂat over SpecR by [4,
§4]. This completes the proof of the ﬁrst paragraph of the theorem.
For the second assertion, see [ 80] or [ 4,§3] (which contain more results along
this lines)
For the third assertion, see e.g. [ 67, Theorem 8.15]. □

VIII.1.2. Duality. IfM is aT -module which is ﬁnite and ﬂat as anR-module,
the module M∨ := HomR(M,R ) is also ﬁnite and ﬂat over R, and has also a T -
module structure. Moreover we have a canonical isomorphism ( M∨)∨ = M as R-
modules and even as T -modules, and for N a second T -module which is ﬁnite and
ﬂat as anR-module the transpose deﬁnes a canonical isomorphism HomT (M,N ) =
HomT (N∨,M∨).
Note that the formation of Hom R(M,N ) and in particular of the T -module
M∨ commutes with any base change R→R′ in the obvious sense. (This is proved
exactly as the special caseM =N in§I.1.) The formation of HomT (M,N ) however
only commutes in general with localizations of R.
If T is ﬁnite and ﬂat over R, then the preceding apply to M = T and we can
deﬁne the T -module T∨, whose formation commutes with arbitrary base change.
We say that theR-algebraT is Gorenstein ifT∨ is ﬂat of rank one overT . Locally,
this is same as assuming the existence of an isomorphism of T -modules T∨≃T .
Proposition VIII.1.6. The natural isomorphism HomR(M,N ) = M∨⊗RN
restricts to an isomorphism HomT (M,N ) = (M∨⊗RN)[I].
Proof — Recall that the natural isomorphism is the one that sends ∑
ili⊗ni∈

ili(m)ni (one checks
locally that it is an isomorphism). It is suﬃcient to observe then that ∑

in (M∨⊗RN)[I] if and only if ∑

i(li⊗tni) for all t∈T , if and only
if f is T -equivariant. □
Corollary VIII.1.7. Assume that T/R is ﬂat. The R-algebraT is Gorenstein
if and only if (T⊗RT )[I] is ﬂat of rank one as T -module.
Proof — We may assume that R is local, and we have to prove in the case that
T/R is Gorenstein if and only if ( T⊗RT )[I] is free of rank one as T -module.
We have (T⊗R T )[I] = Hom T (T∨,T ). So if T/R is Gorenstein, T∨ ≃ T
and (T⊗RT )[I]≃ HomT (T,T ) = T as T -modules. Conversely, if ( T⊗RT )[I] is
isomorphic to T , then let f∈ HomT (T∨,T ) corresponding to a generator of that
module. Then for t∈T , tf = 0 implies that t = 0. Therefore for t∈T , tf(T ) = 0
implies that t = 0. Since T is ﬁnite over R, it has no proper ideal having this
property, andf is surjective. By equality of R-rank, f is an isomorphism. □
Corollary VIII.1.8. If T/R is Gorenstein, dN(T/R) is a locally principal
ideal.

Corollary VIII.1.13. Assume that T/R is Gorenstein, and that M and N
are ﬂat of rank one over T . Then Lb and dN(T/R) are locally principal, and
Lb = dN(T/R) if and only if b is non-degenerate. More precisely, if b = tb0
where b0 : M×N → R is a non-degenerate T -equivariant scalar product, then
Lb =tdN(T/R).
Proof — Note that since T/R is Gorenstein, locally T∨≃ T as T -module, so
we have M ≃ N ≃ T∨ and we are in a special case of the above proposition.
In particular, Lb is principal (locally), and we already know that the Noether’s
diﬀerent is principal (locally) since T/R is Gorenstein.
For the rest of the proof we assume that R is local.
Clearlyb is non-degenerate if and only if for allt∈T ,tb = 0 impliest = 0, which
is equivalent to the same assertion for ˜b. Since T is Gorenstein, (T⊗T )[I] = T ,
and we see that b is non-degenerate if and only if ˜b is a generator of (T⊗T )[I].
Writing ˜b =t˜b0 with t∈T , and where ˜b0 is a generator of (T⊗T )[I], we have
m(˜b) =tm(˜b0) hence Lb =tdN(T/R) with equality if and only if t is invertible, i.e.
if and only if b is non-degenerate. □
Remark VIII.1.14. Let us go back to the hypothesis of Prop VIII.1.3: M∨ and
N∨ free of rank one over T . If b is non-degenerate, then b deﬁnes an isomorphism
M≃N∨ as T -modules, so T∨≃T and T is Gorenstein, and the corollary implies
that Lb = dN(T/R). Conversely, if Lb = dN(T/R), is it necessarily true that b is
non-degenerate (and therefore T Gorenstein)? I’d like to know the answer.
VIII.2. Kim’s scalar product
Let us use the same notations of §VI.1: W = SpR is an open aﬃnoid subset

VIII.3. The cuspidal eigencurve and its good points
The cuspidal eigencurve C0 (p-adic, of tame level N) can be constructed by
applying the eigenvariety machine to three diﬀerent eigenvariety data: to the mod-
ules of cuspidal overconvergent forms, as we did in Chapter VI or to the modules of
overconvergent cohomologyH1
! (YΓ,DK[r](R))± (see Chapter III) for both values of
the sign±. That the three eigenvarieties obtained these ways are isomorphic (with
unique isomorphisms respecting the Hecke operators and the weight maps) is proved
exactly as in Theorem VI.2.1, if we take into account that Stevens’s control theo-
rem work as well forH1
! (YΓ,−) instead of SymbΓ(−), and that by Eichler-Shimura,
H1
! (YΓ.Vk(L))±≃Sk+2(Γ,L ) as Hecke-modules.
We shall use only the two deﬁnitions of C0 with overconvergent cohomology.
If x∈C 0(L), and U = SpT is a clean neighborhood of x, let as in §VI.6.1
W = SpR the image κ(U), and, for r >0 suﬃciently small, let M± be the ﬁnite
free R-module ϵH1
! (DK[r](R))± (it is independent of r) where ϵ is the idempotent
corresponding to the connected component U of κ−1(W ).
We need to deﬁne a notion of good point on the cuspidal eigencurve C0 analog
to the one we deﬁned on the eigencurvesC± (cf. Deﬁnition VII.1.2). The following
lemma is proven exactly as Lemma VII.1.1
Lemma VIII.3.1. Letx be a point on C0 (of ﬁeld of deﬁnition L), with κ(x) =
w∈W . The following statements are equivalent:
(i) For both choices of the sign ±, the dual of the generalized eigenspace
H1
! (YΓ,Dw(L))±
(x) is free of rank 1 over the algebra Tx of the schematic
ﬁber of κ at x.
(ii) For both choices of the sign ±, and for any clean neighborhood of x, the
modules (M±)∨ are ﬂat of rank one over T at x
(iii) For both choices of the sign ±, and for any suﬃciently small clean neigh-
borhood ofx, the modules (M±)∨ are free of rank one over T at x.
Definition VIII.3.2. A point x∈C 0 is called good if it satisﬁes the equivalent
assertions of the above proposition.
Exactly as in Theorem VII.1.5, we can prove that if x is a smooth point of C0
whose weightκ(x) is of the form z↦→zkϵ(z) with ϵ of order pt, and new away from
p, then x is good.
We shall need the following lemma, which summarizes results obtained earlier:

Theorem VIII.4.8. LetL be a ﬁnite extension of Qp andx∈C 0(L) be a smooth

2e(x)− 1.

CHAPTER IX
Solution to exercises
Solution to Exercise I.1.1 (From [50, Proposition 2.10].) The result is clear
when M is free. In general, there is an exact sequence P→ Q→ M→ 0 where
P and Q are ﬁnite free. Let P′ =P⊗RR′ and Q′ =Q⊗RR′. By tensorizing by
R′ we have an exact sequence P′→ Q′→ M′→ 0 hence by applying the functor
HomR′(−,N′) an exact sequence
0→ HomR′(M′,N′)→ HomR′(Q′,N′)→ HomR′(P′,N′).
On the other hand by applying Hom R(−,N ) to P → Q → M → 0 and then
tensorizing by R′, we get using that R′ is R-ﬂat, an exact sequence
0→ HomR(M,N )⊗RR′→ HomR(Q,N )⊗RR′→ HomR(P,N )⊗RR′.
The natural morphism Hom R(P,N )⊗R R′ → HomR′(P′,N′) between the last
terms of the two displayed exact sequences is an isomorphism since P is ﬁnite
free, and the same holds for the middle term. Hence by diagram chasing we see
that HomR(M,N )⊗RR′→ HomR′(M′,N′) is an isomorphism.
Solution to Exercise I.1.2 See the solution to Exercise I.4.2.
Hint to Exercise I.1.3 By construction of the characteristic polynomial, this
question reduces to the case where M is free, in which case it is trivial.
Solution to Exercise I.3.2 Let 0 ̸= m∈ M be a torsion element, that is
such that xm = 0 for some 0 ̸= x∈T . Since T is ﬁnite over R, there is a monic
polynomialP (X) =Xd+ad−1Xd−1+··· +a0∈R[X] such thatP (x) = 0. Choosing
P of minimal degree d, we may assume that a0̸= 0, for otherwise Q(x)x = 0 with
Q(X) :=Xd−1+ad−1Xd−2+··· +a1∈R[X], and thus sinceT is a domainQ(x) = 0,
contradicting the minimality ofd. But then 0 = P (x)m =a0m sincexm = 0, which
shows that m is R-torsion, in contradiction with our running assertion that M is
locally free over R.
Solution to Exercise I.3.6 The surjective tautological mapH⊗R→T A factors
throughT since any element h∈H⊗ R such that ψ(h) = 0 obviously acts by 0 on
A. Hence we have a natural surjective map T →TA. Its kernel is by construction
the ideal generated by the elements ψ(h) of EndR(M) that vanish on A. If M/A is
271
===== PDF PAGE 272 =====
272 IX. SOLUTION TO EXERCISES
torsion, such an endomorphism ψ(h) is torsion in EndR(M), hence is 0 since M is
a faithful R-module.
For 2. and an example of non-injectivity, takes A = B = 0. For an example
of non-surjectivity, take A = B = R and M = A⊕B withH generated by one
element T which acts as the identity on M. Then the map T →TA×T B is the
diagonal embedding R→R2 which is not surjective.
3. is proved exactly as the second part of 1.
For 4. let e = (T−a)/(b−a) inT , so e acts by 0 on A and 1 and B, that is
the image of e∈T is 0 in TA and 1 in TB. If tA∈T A and tB∈T B, there exists
uA∈T that maps on tA inTA (since the map T →TA is surjective by 1.) and
uB∈T that maps on tB inTB (same reason). Then t := (1−e)tA +etB∈T maps
to (tA,tB) inTA×TB.

Solution to Exercise I.4.2 The eigenalgebraT is the quotient of Zp[T ] by the
ideal of operators that act trivially on M. Let P (X)∈ Zp[X]. If P (T ) = 0 as an
operator on M, then P is divisible by the minimal polynomial ( X− 1)2 of T in
Qp[X], so is divisible by (X−1)2 in Zp[X] (since the algorithm of euclidean division
by a monic polynomial like ( X− 1)2 is identical in Zp[X] and Qp[X]). It follows
thatT ≃Zp[X]/(X− 1)2 with the isomorphism sending T on X.
The eigenalgebraT′ is simply Fp since T acts as the identity on M⊗ Fp = F2
p.
The mapT⊗ Zp Fp = Fp[X]/(X− 1)2→T ′ = Fp is the map that sends X on 1. Of
course this map is not an isomorphism.
Solution to Exercise I.5.4 This is standard: by induction on the number
of generators of ψ(H), one reduces to the case of one generator T of ψ(H). In
this case let P (X) be the characteristic (or minimal) polynomial of T and write
P (X) = (X−a1)n1... (X−ar)nr where theai are distinct. Let Pi(X) be the same
polynomial with the factor (X−ai)ni removed. Then the Pi’s are relatively prime,
and a Bezout relation between them shows thatM =∑
i ker(T−ai)ni, a sum which
is obviously direct.

the equality follows from the equality of dimensions, since the rank of a system of
linear equations over k does not change when we extend the scalar to k′. Same
argument for M(χ).

Solution to Exercise I.6.14 a.– By Theorem I.6.11, a basis ofEk(Γ1(N)∩Γ0(p))
consists of the forms Ek,χ,ψ,t′(z) with LRt′| Np and χψ is trivial on ( Z/pZ)∗⊂

both prime to p, because if one of them, say L were not, χ would be non-trivial on
(Z/pZ)∗, so ψ would also be non-trivial on ( Z/pZ)∗, and R would also be divisible
byp, contradictingLR|Np sinceN is not divisible byp. Thus this family of forms
is a basis.
Recall that the Ek,χ,ψ,t where LRt| N (and LRt̸= 1 if k = 2) are a basis
ofEk(Γ1(N). In particular, Ek,χ,ψ,t and Ek,χ,ψ,pt are p-old in Ek(Γ1(N)∩ Γ0(p)),
except for E2,1,1,p =E2,p. Thus we see that the dimension of Ek(Γ1(N)∩ Γ0(p)) is
twice the dimension ofEk(Γ1(N)), twice the same plus one if k = 2.

Let K′ be a ﬁnite Galois extension such that GK′ acts trivially on the set of
points of non-closed points of SpecT (this is possible sine T⊗ K is ´ etale overK),
and deﬁneR′, m′,k′ as usual. Recall thatTR′ =T⊗ RR′ is ´ etale overR′ if and only
ifT is ´ etale overR, since R′/R is faithfully ﬂat. Observe that every irreducible
component of SpecTR′ has generic degree 1 overR′ since its generic point is deﬁned
overK′. Thus every irreducible component of Spec TR′ is isomorphic (through the
structural map) to Spec R′. We thus see that SpecTR′ is non-etale if and only if it
has more irreducible component that connected component, and 2. follows from 1.
applied toTR′.

Solution to Exercise I.7.13 The inclusion between ﬁniteR-modules (M∩A)⊕
(M∩B) ↪→ M becomes after tensorizing by K the isomorphism A⊕B ≃−→M.
This implies that the cokernelC of this inclusion is torsion. It is ﬁnite as a quotient
of M. This proves a.
The map pA restricted to M∩A has kernel M∩A∩B = 0, so this map
identiﬁes M∩A with a submodule of MA. Now consider the composition M
pA
→
MA→ MA/pA(M∩A) = MA/(M∩A), which is surjective as a composition of
surjections. Its kernel is the set of m∈ M such that pA(m)∈ pA(M∩A) that is
such thatm diﬀers from an element of M∩A by an element of kerpA =M∩B. In
other words, the kernel of this map is ( M∩A)⊕ (M∩B), and this maps realizes
an isomorphism M/((M∩A)⊕ (M∩B))→MA/(M∩A), which proves b.
The kernel of the map ( pA,pB) is (M∩B)∩ (M∩A) = 0 so this map may
be used to identify M to a submodule of MA⊕MB. The composition MA ↪→
MA⊕MB→ (MA⊕MB)/M has for kernel the set of m∈ MA such that there
exists m′∈ M satisfying pA(m′) = m, pB(m′) = 0. The latter condition on m′ is
equivalent to m′∈M∩A, so the condition on m is equivalent to m∈pA(M∩A)
and our map realizes an injection C =MA/(M∩A)→ (MA⊕MB)/M. This map
is easily seen to be surjective, which proves c.

Solution to Exercise II.1.1 If x is multiplicative, we can compute for z∈R:
|z| =|xx−1z|≤| x||x−1z|≤| x||x−1||z| =|z|, and all these inequalities are thus
equalities. In particular |z| =|x||x−1z|. Setting z =xy, we get|xy| =|x||y|. The
converse and the second assertions are clear.
Solution to Exercise II.1.2 If two norms are equivalent, it is trivial that they
deﬁne the same topology. Conversely, if|| 1 and|| 2 deﬁne the same topology, then
{m∈ M,|m|1 < 1} is open, hence contain a set of the form {m∈ M,|m|2 < η}
for some η. For any non-zero m∈ M, there exists an integer n such that 1/p≤
|pnm|1< 1, so that p−n|m|2 =|pnm|2<η , or|m|2<ηp n≤ηp|m|1. By symmetry,
the two norms are equivalent.

Solution to Exercise II.1.19 First assume that P is orthonormalizable with
basis (ei)i∈I. One has |α(ei)| ≤ |α| for every i, hence by the Open Mapping

theorem one can choose bounded elements mi of M such that f(mi) = α(ei).
There is therefore a continuous morphism β : P → M sending ei to mi, and one
has fβ (ei) =α(ei) for every i, hence fβ =α.
When P is potentially orthonormalizable, just change the norm.
In the general case, assume P⊕Q is potentially orthonormalizable. Then for

equal toα onP and to zero onQ deﬁnes by the preceding case a map ˜β :P⊕Q→N
such that f ˜β = ˜α, and upon deﬁning β = ˜β|P one has fβ =α.

Solution to Exercise II.3.4 Write N = N(Q,ν ) for simplicity. Since aN is

vp(an(x))−nν≥ vp(an)−nν≥ vp(aN)−Nν = vp(aN(x))−Nν , which shows,

provided that a0(x)̸= 0 for all x, that is provided a0 is invertible in R. If vp(a0) is
not constant, however, a0 is not multiplicative.
Solution to Exercise II.3.5 (1) is trivial. For (2), consider x∈ SpR. Since Q
is ν-dominant, its dominant term is invertible so Qx has also degree N, and since

is strongly ν-dominant.

Z[SL2(Z)]-module generated by {∞}−{ 0}. Since those divisors clearly generate
∆0 as Z-module, this would be suﬃcient to prove Manin’s lemma.
To start the induction, note that when ( ABC) has only three points in Z2, we
know that it has area 1/2 by 1.a. So by 1.b. we are done.
In general, if ( ABC) has more than three points in Z2, let D = (e,f ) be a
fourth point. By replacing D by an other point of Z2 in the segment [AD] (which
lies inside the triangle ( ABC)), we can assume that e and f are relatively prime
integers. Note that D is not on [AB] (resp. nor on [ AC]) since we have assumed
that a and c (resp. b and d) are relatively prime. Therefore the triangles ( ABD)
and (ADC) are non-ﬂat, and have strictly less integral points that ( ABC). By
induction hypothesis, we know that{a/c}−{ e/f} and{e/f}−{ b/d} both belong
to the Z[SL2(Z)]-module generated by {∞}−{ 0}. Therefore their sum also does,
which proves the induction step.
2. is an easy computation: (1 + σ)({∞}−{ 0}) ={∞}−{ 0} +{0}−{∞} = 0,
and
(1 +τ +τ 2)({∞}−{ 0}) ={∞}−{ 0} +{0}−{ 1} +{1}−{∞} = 0.

Solution to Exercise III.1.3 By induction on the number of integral points in
a polygon, any convex polygon can be covered by triangles whose vertices are in Z2
and that contain no other point of Z2. Since we know by a. that Pick’s theorem
is true for any of those triangles, it is suﬃcient to check that Pick’s theorem is
additive, that is holds for any polygon with vertices in Z2 that is the union of
two smaller polygon with vertices in Z2, disjoint interior, and an edge in common,
provided Pick’s theorem is true for those two smaller polygons. This is an easy
computation. We leave the details to the reader.
For non-convex polygons, the answer depends on what you call apolygon. Read
[69].

Solution to Exercise III.3.1 Replacing G by f∗G, we may assume Y = X,
f = Id, that is we need to prove thatG(X) is a sub-module of M ifX is connected.
If X is empty, G(X) = 0 and the result is true. Assume X is not empty and let
x∈ X. We claim that the natural map G(X)→ Gx≃ M is injective. If s,s′
are two sections of G, both the sets where they are equal and diﬀerent are open,
because this is true for section of a constant sheaf, and this assertion is local. Thus
if s,s′∈ G(X) have same image in Gx, they are equal on some open subset U of
X containingx, and the locus where they are equal, being a non-empty open and
closed subset of X, is X.

Solution to Exercise V.2.3 Points a. and c. are clear from the deﬁnition. For
point b., recall that the canonical surjectionπ :X→X/ kerf is open; indeed, ifU is
open, we need to show thatπ(U) is open which means thatπ−1(π(U)) =U +Kerf
is open, which is clear. Hence f = ¯f◦π is open if and only if ¯f is open. Since ¯f is
a continuous isomorphism, it is open if and only if it is an homeomorphism. (After
[21,§1.1.9].)
For d., cf [21,§1.1.9]
For e., note that both X and Y are normable groups by the above exercise. If
f is strict, f(X)≃X/ kerf is complete, hence closed in X. Conversely, if f(X) is
closed, it inherits a structure of Fr´ echet, and the isomorphism¯f :X/ kerf→f(X)
is thus an homeomorphism by the open mapping theorem ([ 90, Prop. 8.6]).
For f., recall thatf strict means that the continuous isomorphism ¯f :X/ kerf→
f(X) is an homeomorphism. The map f′ : X′/ kerf′→ f′(X′) is just ( ¯f)⊗ 1 by
ﬂatness ofR′ overR. Hence we are reduced to the case where f is an isomorphism.
In this case, that f is an homeomorphism means that for every semi-norm q in our
family of semi-norms on Y , one has two semi-norms p1 and p2 in our family on
X and two positive constant C1 and C2 such that C1p1≤ f∗q≤ C2p2. But then
clearly C1p′
1≤f′∗q′≤C2p′
2 and it follows that f′ is an homeomorphism.
For g., note that we obviously have ker f⊂ ker ˆf, hence ’kerf⊂ ker ˆf. Since
f is strict, for every ϵ >0, there exists δ >0 such that if |f(x)| < δ,|x +k| < ϵ
for some k∈ kerf. Let x∈ ˆX such that |f(x)| < δ/2. Let xn be a sequence
of elements of X converging to x. For n large enough, one has |xn−x| < ϵand
| ˆf(xn)− ˆf(x)|<δ/ 2, so|f(xn)| =| ˆf(xn)|<δ and|xn+kn|<ϵ for somekn∈ kerf.
Thus|x +kn| < 2ϵ which proves that ˆf is strict. To prove that ker ˆf = ’kerf, let
x in ker ˆf. The reasoning above show that for every ϵ >0, there is a kn∈ kerf
such that|x +kn|< 2ϵ. Thus x∈ ’kerf and ’kerf = ker ˆf. The assertion for Im f
is similar and left to the reader.

Hint to Exercise VI.4.11 Look up the appendix of [ 27].
Solution to Exercise VI.4.11 We have ρx = 1⊕ωp. Since Up(x) = p, in
a neighborhood of x all classical point y are such that ρy is irreducible (cf. the
appendix of [ 27].) Hence by Ribet’s lemma (cf. e.g. [ 7]) we can construct a
non trivial extension, in the category of GQ,lp-representations, of 1 by ωp, which is
crystalline atp by Lemma VI.4.10. If there was inﬁnitely many classical points y in
the given neighborhood of minimal tame level 1 (instead of l), then that extension
would also be unramiﬁed at l: but there is non-trivial extension of 1 by ωp in the
category of GQ-representations that is unramiﬁed everywhere and crystalline at p,
because Q has only ﬁnitely many units (cf. the appendix of [ 27]).
